Under `showHidden`, arrays and tuples add `[length]`, class instances the getters and setters their classes define (from the method table: a getter and setter of one name defined by different classes show as one), errors `[stack]: [Getter/Setter]`, `[message]` (when not empty) and `[cause]` first; functions show no `length`, `name` and `prototype` (descriptors don't carry them). **Console** (`console.c`, Node's Console): every console method is a runtime call, `pts_console(method, argc, argv, spread)` for those that take values and `pts_console_dir`/`pts_console_group_end` for the others; info, debug and dirxml are log, warn is error, groupCollapsed is group (as Node defines them). Each call formats its whole output first, indents every line by the open groups, writes lone surrogates as U+FFFD (as Node's streams encode strings) and writes the line under the stream's lock; console.error flushes stdout first, so the two streams keep the program's order when they share a file, as Node's synchronous writes do. count and the timers keep labels in a list behind one lock; warnings about missing labels go to stderr without Node's `(node:<pid>)`. trace makes an Error named "Trace", whose stack holds the program's frames. `console.dir`'s options must be an object literal of `depth` (null for no limit) and `showHidden` (PTS1001 otherwise). `console.table` is Node's console.table and cli_table ported to `table.c`, reading the rows through the runtime's own-keys lists and dynamic reads (D-086), so it takes any value; its cells use the inspector's `breakLength` and `maxArrayLength`, which became options too. Console calls are left out of the stack-overflow guard's recursion test (D-069), though `%s` can call a `toString`. **valueOf**: a class's `valueOf()` that returns a primitive is listed in its method table with its result kind, and the runtime's ToPrimitive (`+`, relational operators, ToNumber, `%d`, and `==` between an instance and a primitive) calls it before falling back to the string, as OrdinaryToPrimitive does.
- **Reason:** `console.log` is how every differential test observes a program, and format strings, `console.error` and groups are common in real programs; porting Node's code rather than approximating it is what made instances, arrays and maps match byte for byte (D-O16). Keeping format and inspect in the runtime handles dynamic first arguments and needs no generated code per call. valueOf is needed for `%d` to match and was a documented gap in ToPrimitive.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Lowering format strings at compile time (the format is often not a literal, and the arguments' runtime types decide `%s`). Flushing stdout only at exit (stderr lines would come out before earlier stdout lines in a shared file). A process-wide mutex around formatting (each line is built first, so the stream's own lock suffices). Function sources in descriptors for `%s` of a function (binary size for a debugging corner; `String(f)` doesn't work either).

### D-089 · M3.7 split; the corpus; the gaps it closed (M3.7a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** M3.7 becomes M3.7a (the corpus, its harness, the first round of fixes, and every remaining divergence recorded) and M3.7b (fixing the recorded causes that two or more corpus programs share); no scope is dropped. **The corpus** is `pts/tests/corpus/<theme>/*.pts`: 300 realistic plain-TypeScript programs, 50 for each of algorithms, text, oop, data, functional and errors, written by subagents to a brief (realistic, deterministic, no features the spec leaves out or later milestones bring, never tailored to pass). `TestCorpusMatchesNode` runs them under pts and Node (`--experimental-transform-types`, `TZ=America/New_York`) and requires the same stdout and exit code; they have no expected output of their own. `pts/tests/corpus/compare.sh file…` does the same from the shell (with `PTS=` for another compiler) and shows the first differing lines; it ignores `// skip:`, so it also shows which skipped programs pass now. A program that still diverges carries `// skip: <cause>`, and `PROGRESS.md` lists the causes. **The fixes**, each with a run test: `filter` with a type guard makes the narrower array (elements narrowed as a variable is); a literal in an array literal with no contextual type takes the element type tsc infers for the array (`impliedType`, at any depth, widened as a variable's type is), so `[{ tags: ["a"] }, { tags: [] }]` makes one type of object; intersections of object types are object types, and `Object.assign({}, …)` is the spread it is equivalent to; `arr.toString()`; a computed `T | undefined` or dynamic argument for an optional parameter is evaluated into a temporary before the call, with every impure argument before it, so undefined leaves the argument out (the callee's object is evaluated after them, which only an object expression with effects could tell); members of unions of objects and other values (`x.length` for `string | string[]`) are read and called at run time, as through `any`; `x as T` for any T is free where x's type is assignable to T or has T's representation, and otherwise checked as a dynamic value converting to T is (D-070); keyed elements of objects (`totals[status]` for `Record<Status, number>`, `o[k]` for `k: keyof T`, tuple unions) are read and written at run time with checks, and generic instances are told apart by literal type arguments too (`pick(o, "a")` and `pick(o, "b")` return different types); tuples are iterated and have the array methods that don't change an array, on a copy into an array of the union of their element types; relational and arithmetic operators on unions follow JS's rules at run time; a method's `this` type is its class; an enum object used as a value is a new hash-map object of its members (and of numbers to names); a conditional with an empty array literal makes the other branch's array type, and nested `any[]` data converts to typed arrays by a checked copy; `a[i] ?? d` and `a[i] === "s"` read undefined past the end, as JS does; `obj && test` for an object (never falsy) is test; static getters and setters (plain `=` calls the setter); a union that recurs through arrays or hash-map objects (`type Json = … | Json[]`, `{ [k: string]: string | Context[] }`) holds dynamic values, a dynamic value narrowed to an array or hash-map object of dynamic values is that object, checked, and array methods and `Object.*` on dynamic receivers go through the runtime; a property tsc narrowed to a primitive is checked where it is read as one, since a call tsc doesn't follow may have changed it (it was read as garbage); static members of generic classes belong to one class of statics that all instances share, `x instanceof Box` tests every instance of a generic class, and a field read through a dynamic receiver of a generic class's instance is found at run time; `o.constructor.name` and `new.target.name` (the runtime's `pts_value_constructor_name`), and the `object` type is a dynamic value; `Number.isNaN`, `isFinite`, `isInteger` and `isSafeInteger` as values; an object literal for a union whose members overlap takes the member with the most properties it provides; tuples convert to strings as arrays do, maps and sets as `[object Map]`/`[object Set]`; where any value fits (printing, `===`, `??`), a narrowed value is read as the union it was narrowed from, since tsc narrows `const v: T | undefined = list[9]` to T though it holds undefined; a function whose result may be undefined returns undefined when it ends without `return`; `c + ""` calls c's `valueOf`; and a dynamic value converting to a union with dynamic-data members is converted with a check (a literal's object where a recursive type's hash-map object goes was cast unchecked, and crashed).
- **Reason:** The brief asks for a corpus of realistic programs as the semantic oracle, and the corpus showed that ordinary TypeScript leans on type-level features (keyed access, `keyof`, `as`, recursive JSON types, enum objects, guards) whose values the static representation can't follow; the checked dynamic machinery of D-070–D-077 already gives JS's semantics for them, so these features reuse it where a static lowering would need a new representation. Writing programs by theme with subagents, checking each against Node, then fixing causes by how many programs share them, keeps the work on what real code needs. A recorded skip keeps `main` green while each divergence stays visible.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Tailoring programs to pass (the corpus exists to find divergences). Expected-output blocks in corpus programs (Node is the oracle, and CI requires it). Lowering keyed access as a switch over the keys (doesn't cover `keyof T` in generic code or tuple unions). Copying recursive values into typed representations (unbounded copies, and writes through one copy wouldn't show in another). A module-level enum object (an enum object is rarely compared by identity). Throwing for every narrowed read whose value disagrees (JS reads the value; checks only where a primitive is required).

### D-090 · Loose locals: elements that aren't there are undefined where programs test for it (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** A local whose initializer reads an element of an array, a string or a hash-map object (`const next = argv[i + 1]`, `const fn = TABLE[name]`), or that an array pattern binds from an array (`const [key, value] = s.split("=")`), is *loose* when its type has no undefined and the program tests it for undefined somewhere: compares it with `undefined` or `null`, tests it for truth (`if`, `!`, `?:`, `&&`, `||`, loop conditions), uses it before `??`, `??=` or `?.`, takes its `typeof`, or passes it to a function of the program whose parameter's type holds undefined (a type guard taking `unknown`). A loose local holds `T | undefined` (`collectUndefinedTests`, `isLoose`, `bindingType`): its initializer reads the element as a dynamic value (undefined past the end or for a missing key, as in JS); where it is tested it is that union; anywhere else tsc says it is a `T`, and the read is checked as a dynamic value's is (`looseRead`: `TypeError: Expected string, got undefined` where JS would go on with undefined). Other places that read elements now read undefined too: the object of `?.` (`terms[t]?.length`, `sorted[1]?.amount`; tsc's result type gains undefined, and outside a place that takes any value the result is checked), the branches of a conditional whose type holds undefined (`dir ? exits[dir] : undefined`), a string element compared with `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=` (`fmt[i] >= "0"` past the end is false), a string element compared with a union of string literals; `x ?? y` where tsc took x never to be undefined has the type of both sides; `JSON.stringify(x)` compared with `===` stays dynamic. D-015, D-038 and D-059 still hold elsewhere: an element read straight into a place that needs a `T` (`const n: number = a[9]`, an argument, a function's result) throws `RangeError`.
- **Reason:** The corpus showed this to be the most common divergence from Node (17 of 300 programs, more as other causes were fixed): realistic code reads past the end or a missing key into a local and checks it afterwards, because `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` is off and the check is the only way to handle absence. Testing for undefined is the program saying it expects the value may be missing, so only those locals pay for a union; all other element reads keep their typed fast path, so hot loops over arrays (`sum += a[i]`) don't change. Fourteen corpus programs that were skipped by design now match Node.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Making every element-initialized local a union (boxes numbers and checks every use in hot loops). Representing undefined inside `T` (a NULL string, a NaN payload for numbers: every operation on every value would have to know). Turning `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` on (rejects the corpus's ordinary programs at compile time, against tie-breaker 3). Flow analysis to find which uses are reachable with undefined (tsc already narrows; the check at a use costs one compare).

### D-091 · Corpus divergences: dynamic receivers, fitted overrides, tuple rests, void values (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** Fixed for causes the corpus shares, each with a run test: (1) a dynamic value converts to an object, array, map or function type by a checked conversion wherever it comes from (an erased copy's `T`, a recursive union's object), not only where tsc types it any or unknown; (2) a property of a dynamic value that tsc gives an object type (an `unknown` a type guard narrowed, an object of a recursive union) is read and written at run time and the value checked against the property's type (`valueProperty`); (3) `x satisfies T` is x; (4) `{}` written as a type, or given by `unknown ?? "x"`, is a dynamic value (an empty object literal stays an object); (5) a value of type void is undefined, and a function that returns nothing used as a function type that returns a value returns undefined where that type holds it, and nothing (tsc took it to return never) where it doesn't (`voidResult`); (6) a rest parameter of a tuple type without optional or rest elements (`...args: [host: string, port: number]`, and `A extends unknown[]` instances) is a parameter per element, as tsc expands it in function types, and the body starts by making the tuple; (7) an override that declares fewer parameters than the method it overrides gets the others as unused parameters, which its own callers leave out (`LeftOut`), and an override may return an instance of a subclass of the class the overridden method returns (the method table entry is cast; calls through it cast back); an override that changes a parameter's type, or leaves out a parameter with a default, is still PTS1001; (8) callbacks whose result is a union (`w && !seen.has(w)`) are tested for truth as JS does (they were always truthy: a bug); (9) `JSON.stringify(x) ?? String(x)` keeps undefined for the `??`.
- **Reason:** Each cause was shared by two or more corpus programs, and each fix reuses the checked dynamic machinery (D-070–D-077) or a lowering that already existed (expanded tuple parameters in function types, optional parameters' LeftOut), so typed code keeps its representation. Fitting overrides instead of rejecting them follows tie-breaker 3; the casts are between pointers to instances, which share their layout's prefix.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Bridge methods for every changed override signature (two entries per method name in method tables, which interface sites and `any` find by name). Rejecting `{}` (common in `unknown` narrowing). A copy of `T`'s representation for void (no value to copy).

### D-092 · URI functions, computed `in`, JSON revivers, casts of dynamic variables (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) `encodeURI`, `encodeURIComponent`, `decodeURI` and `decodeURIComponent` are runtime functions (`uri.c`) of their argument's ToString, percent-encoding UTF-8 as ECMAScript 19.2.6 says, with V8's `URIError: URI malformed` for a lone surrogate or an escape that isn't valid UTF-8; decodeURI keeps the escapes of reserved characters. (2) `k in o` with a key computed at run time looks the key up through o's descriptor, as for a dynamic value (`pts_dynamic_has`), on any object, array or hash-map object. (3) `JSON.parse(text, reviver)` converts the reviver to `(key: string, value: any) => any` and the runtime walks the parsed value as InternalizeJSONProperty does (children first, keys as they were before the walk, undefined deleting a key or leaving a hole). (4) `e as T` for a variable holding a dynamic value (a catch binding, an `unknown`) converts its value with a check, not the class tsc narrowed it to: tsc's subtype reduction may narrow an error to a class whose members another class shares (`SyntaxError | SchemaError` to SchemaError). (5) An object pattern reads a key missing from a `Record<string, unknown>` (or `any`) as undefined. (6) Union members whose interfaces have the same fields (`{ kind: "add"; left; right } | { kind: "mul"; left; right }`) are alike at run time, so a literal providing them takes the first.
- **Reason:** Each removes the first divergence of two or more corpus programs (seven now match Node). The runtime functions follow the specification's algorithms; the others reuse the dynamic lookups and checked conversions of D-070–D-077.
- **Alternatives rejected:** ICU or a URL library for URI encoding (the specification's algorithm is short). A compile error for computed `in` on typed objects (they all have descriptors).

### D-093 · Object types of numeric property names are hash-map objects (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** An object type whose properties all have array-index names and one type (`Record<Level, number>` for a numeric enum `Level`, `Partial<Record<State, State>>`, `{ 0: string; 1: string }`) is a hash-map object of that type (D-059), keyed by the names' strings as JS keys such an object; literals of it, `o[k]` with numeric keys, `Object.keys`, `in`, JSON and printing work as for `Record<string, T>`. An element read whose own type holds undefined (an optional property of such a type) reads undefined for a missing key where there is no context to say otherwise. Object types that mix numeric and other names, or numeric names of different types, are still PTS1001.
- **Reason:** Numeric enums as record keys are the common source of numeric property names (two corpus programs); JS stores them as string keys, which is what a hash-map object is, so no new representation is needed.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A shape with fields named `0`, `1` (C identifiers need mangling, and dynamic keys `o[k]` would need a switch).

### D-094 · Array copies no one can tell apart, function values as methods, loose array patterns (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) **Arrays and tuples converting to an array type of other elements** (`Leaf[]` as `HNode[]`, `(Push | Halt)[]` as `Instr[]`, `Team[]` as `(Team | null)[]`, `number[]` as `(number | string)[]`, a filter's `[string, number | null][]` as `[string, number][]`, a readonly tuple as `readonly B[]`) are copied, each element converted with a check (arrays through the runtime's checked conversion, D-071; tuples element by element), **only where the copy can't be told apart from the array**: a new array (an array literal, `new Array`, `Array.from`/`Array.of`, `split`, `Object.keys`/`values`/`entries`, the array methods that make a new array, and `sort`/`reverse`/`fill`/`copyWithin` of a new array), a `const` whose only use is the conversion and whose initializer is a new array, or an argument for a parameter of a function of the program that only reads it (its elements, `length`, the methods that don't change an array, `for…of`, spreads; in the function itself or in callbacks of array methods, which run before the method returns; not a parameter property, not a rest parameter) (`isNewArray`, `isReadOnlyArgument`). Anywhere else (the callee sorts, pushes or keeps the array, or the array is used again) it stays PTS1001, with a message that says why. (2) **A function value given for a method of an object type** (`{ combine: (a, b) => a + b }` for an interface's `combine(a, b)`, `{ label: join }`) goes through an adapter that leaves out the `this` parameter of the shape's method field (D-044), which fixes the known issue that reported it as "converting object1 to string"; an object pattern binds a method of a shape or interface as a closure bound to the object, as `o.m` is (D-044). (3) **Every local an array pattern binds from an array** (`const [op, arg] = line.split(" ")`) is loose (D-090), not only those the program tests for undefined: JS binds undefined past the end, and programs use such a local only where it is there; its uses as a `T` are checked. (4) Closures' dynamic entries are named `pts_dynamic_fn_<name>` in C (a program's function `get` clashed with the runtime's `pts_dynamic_get`).
- **Reason:** Passing an array of a subclass, or of some members of a union, where an array of the wider type is expected is ordinary TypeScript (eight corpus programs), and the arrays involved are almost always new (`map`'s result into an annotated variable) or only read by the callee. A copy is exact there and reuses the checked conversions of D-071/D-072; where a copy could be told apart (the callee sorts in place, or pushes and the caller looks), a compile error is better than silently different output (tie-breaker 2 over 3). The remaining items each removed the first divergence of two or more corpus programs.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Views with checked writes (the same buffer under the wider type; every write at sites of view-target types compares the array's descriptor, but the runtime's `slice` and `splice` give new arrays the source's descriptor, so their results would refuse the wider type's values unless every array-making function took the static type: a large change for the few programs that mutate through the wider type). Copying everywhere (a callee that sorts or pushes would silently not change the caller's array). Retyping a new array in place (works only for elements of the same representation; a copy of a new array costs little). Making only tested array-pattern locals loose (a command parser's `arg` is used only for the commands that have one, never tested).

### D-095 · Values made for the union of object types they go to (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** Where a value goes to a union with object types among its members (a `Result<T, E>`, a discriminated union) and tsc types the expression as something narrower whose objects have another representation, the lowering makes the value for the union: (1) a call of a generic function whose result goes there is instantiated with the type arguments tsc infers from its return type and that union (`PtsInferTypeArgumentsFromResult`, a fork export), when the call type-checks with them (its arguments assignable to the parameters, its result to the union), so `return err({ kind: "empty" })` in a function returning `Result<number, ParseError>` calls `err<ParseError>`, not `err<{ kind: "empty" }>`; an erased copy's dynamic result (D-078) is converted to the union directly; (2) a conditional whose branches are of different types, an arrow function or function expression without a declared result type whose contextual function type returns such a union (`(input) => c ? { ok: true, … } : { ok: false, … }` as a `Parser<T>`), take that union rather than the union tsc infers from the literals (which adds each literal's missing properties to the others as optional, `{ ok: boolean; rest?: undefined }`); (3) an object literal returned from a function that declares no result type (no contextual type) takes its member of the result tsc inferred for the function; (4) an intersection that no member covers and that isn't of object types only (`E & Error` for an E of object types, which `e instanceof Error` narrows to; a branded `string & { brand }`) is a dynamic value, and a union narrowed to such a type is read as a dynamic value; (5) interfaces are keyed by the identity of their fields' unions and map types (as they already were by classes, arrays and interfaces), since a recursive union printed while its members are being made prints differently: the interface of `{ op: "neg", arg: e }` was another than the alias member `{ op: "neg"; arg: Expr }`.
- **Reason:** Results, parsers and state machines written as discriminated unions of object types are ordinary TypeScript (six corpus programs), and tsc's types for the expressions that make them are narrower or normalized unions whose objects the static representation can't convert without copying. JS's values don't depend on the type arguments or on tsc's normalization, so choosing the union the value goes to gives the same values and needs no copy. tsc's own inference (rather than a structural matcher of ours) decides the type arguments.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Copying objects into the target's representation (identity and later writes would differ, as for arrays in D-094). Always instantiating generic calls from their context (types the result needlessly wider where the inferred instance converts already, more instances). A structural unifier in the lowering (tsc's inference already handles unions, aliases and nesting).

### D-096 · Keyed spread properties, fromEntries into any, hasOwnProperty, own properties JS hides, calls of function unions (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) In an object literal with spreads, computed properties at its end whose key's type is a union of string literals that each name a field of the literal's shape (`({ ...acc, [op.kind]: acc[op.kind] + 1 })` for a `Record<Kind, number>`) are written after the object is made, each by a checked write found at run time (D-089's keyed elements); every field exists already, so the order JS gives them is kept. A computed property of one constant name works in such literals too. (2) `Object.fromEntries` whose result tsc types as `any` (its pairs are arrays, not tuples: `xs.map((x) => [key(x), x])`) makes a hash-map object of dynamic values; pairs that are arrays give their elements 0 and 1, undefined past the end. (3) `o.hasOwnProperty(k)` on any object that isn't a hash-map object, and `Object.prototype.hasOwnProperty.call(o, k)`, are `Object.hasOwn(o, k)`. (4) `Object.hasOwn` (and so these) also finds the own properties that JS doesn't list: an array's, tuple's or string's `length`; an error's `stack`, its `message` when not empty, its `cause` when given, and its `name` when it isn't its builtin class's (set by its constructor or a field). (5) A call of a value of a union of function types (`(() => number) | (() => string)`) goes through the members' dynamic entries (D-074), its result converted with a check to the type tsc gives the call. (6) A tuple element read at a constant index is narrowed as tsc narrows it (`found[0]` after `typeof found[0] !== "string"` throws), with a check, as a property's is (D-089).
- **Reason:** Each removed the first divergence of two or more corpus programs (counting reducers over `Record<Union, number>`, indexing helpers, error serializers), reusing the dynamic machinery of D-070–D-077 and D-086.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A switch over the key's literals for keyed spread properties (the runtime lookup already exists and is checked). Tracking whether an error's message was given (an empty message is rare; V8 makes `""` own but `undefined` not).

### D-097 · Literal methods of other parameters, methods for function properties, partial maps (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) A method of an object literal whose parameters, as declared, aren't those of the method field it goes in (`deposit(amount, memo)` for an interface's `deposit(amount: number, memo?: string)`, whose field passes D-055's value and flag for `memo`) is lowered with its own parameters and goes in the field through an adapter, as a function value of another function type does (D-041). (2) A method of an object literal given for a property of a function type (`push(x) { … }` for `push: (x: number) => void`) is a closure without `this`, as the property's calls pass none; one that uses `this` there is PTS1001. (3) `Partial<Record<K, V>>` is a hash-map object of V, as D-093 made object types of numeric names: a program adds its keys over time, and JS lists them in that order, which a shape's fixed order can't follow. (Other partial types, `Partial<Point>`, stay shapes with optional fields, which class instances and other shapes convert to.) (4) `k in o` makes descriptors carry declared types, as writes through `any` do (D-072), so that every method is in its class's method table for the lookup (`"norm" in p` was true only when something else had listed them).
- **Reason:** Each removed the first divergence of corpus programs (a bank module's object of closures, running statistics, totals by kind); the adapters and hash-map objects already existed.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Insertion order kept per object for shapes with optional fields (a descriptor per order reached at run time; descriptors are static). Making every object type with only optional properties a hash-map object (options bags are passed class instances and other shapes, which hash-map objects can't take as they are).

### D-098 · A function's name and length (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** `f.name` and `f.length` of a function value (a function type, a union of them, or through `any`) are runtime reads (`pts_value_function_name`, `pts_value_function_length`) of its closure's descriptor, which now carries `length` (`pts_type.length`) beside the name it already had (D-041): the function's parameters before the first with a default and the rest parameter, as JS counts them, from its declaration (`ir.Func.Length`; TS's `this` parameter not counted), and JS's lengths for Math's functions, `String`/`Number`/`Boolean` and the Number tests as values. An adapter or a bound function has the name and length of the function it wraps (a bound one's name prefixed `bound `, as console.log shows it; its length isn't reduced by the bound arguments). A method value's length counts its parameters before the first that may be left out, so an optional (`?`) parameter ends the count as a default does.
- **Reason:** `fn.name` in logging and `fn.length` in currying helpers are common (three corpus programs); the descriptors already name every closure for console.log.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A field per closure object (descriptors are shared by every closure of a function). Exact lengths of bound functions (a count per bind site in the bound closure's descriptor; rare).

### D-099 · Enum reverse mappings of members, missing keys before `??`, undefined optional arguments (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) `E[x]` for an enum E names a member only when x is a string literal (`E["Center"]`); `E[E.Center]` is the reverse mapping of the member's value, as in JS (it was read as the member itself: a bug that printed 2 for `Align[Align.Center]`). (2) `o[k] ?? d` for a hash-map object o whose element type tsc takes to be there has the type of both sides, as for arrays (D-090), so a missing key gives d (`names[rank] ?? rank`); where only o's element type fits, the result is checked. (3) An argument of a library method's optional parameter (an array's or string's `slice`, `indexOf`, …) whose type is one type or undefined is the left-out value where it is undefined at run time (`rows.slice(i, limit === null ? undefined : i + limit)`), and a conditional with an `undefined` or `null` branch isn't given the numeric type of its other branch.
- **Reason:** Each removed the first divergence of corpus programs (four now match Node); the first was a silent wrong value.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Rejecting `E[E.m]` (it is the common way to name a member's value).

### D-100 · Optional and rest tuple elements; no silent drops (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) A tuple type with optional elements (`[string, number?]`) is a tuple shape whose optional elements are fields of `T | undefined` marked optional, holding the absent value (D-065) for an element left out; a literal that leaves trailing elements out stores the absent value. A tuple's length is its elements up to the first left out (`pts_tuple_length`), which `length`, `in`, console.log, JSON, `String(t)`, spreads, `Object.keys` and copies into arrays use; a write to a left-out element adds it (`t[2] = true` makes the length 3). A tuple type with a rest element (`[number, ...number[]]`, also as a rest parameter's type) is an array of the union of its element types: literals make arrays, elements are read as an array's. (2) Spreading an array literal into a call passes its elements (`f(...[1, 2] as [number, ...number[]])`), and spreading a rest tuple held in a variable passes its leading required elements (read with D-015's check) and then the rest, so it fits fixed parameters followed by a rest parameter. (3) A safety net: an expression whose lowering fails without a diagnostic would drop its statement from the program; if the program has no other error, the first such expression is now reported (PTS1001, "which the compiler failed to lower without saying why"). Uses of a declaration that failed still fail quietly when its error is reported.
- **Reason:** Instruction lists and records written as tuples with optional fields and variadic tuples are ordinary TypeScript (four corpus programs). Finding the rest-tuple literal case showed that a lowering returning nil without a report silently removed a statement from a program that built: a correctness hazard worth a general guard.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A length word in every tuple (a layout change for all tuples; the absent value already marks left-out fields). Optional tuples as arrays (elements of different types would be a union array, losing the typed fields destructuring reads).

### D-101 · Abstract and overridden generic methods (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** A generic method may be abstract (`abstract accept<R>(v: Visitor<R>): R`) and may override a generic method of a superclass. Calls of a generic method that is abstract or that a subclass overrides are dispatched by name: an interface call (D-036) of the method named so in the method table of the receiver's class, where every class's generic method of that name has its erased copy (D-078, D-079), with the erased signature, and the result converted to the call's type with a check (`dispatchedGenericCall`). Calls through `super` still call the superclass's instance; other generic methods of the same name stay monomorphized. An erased copy that makes an instance of a generic class makes it for `any` (D-078's known issue), so a dispatched method that returns `new Some(x)` where an `Option<number>` goes still throws. Overriding a plain method with a generic one, or a generic method with a plain one, stays PTS1001; so do generic accessors.
- **Reason:** The visitor pattern (`accept<R>`) and generic hierarchies are ordinary TypeScript (two corpus programs); the erased copies in method tables and the interface sites that call them already existed, and monomorphized instances can't be chosen by the receiver's class at compile time.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Virtual slots per instance (an instance per type argument list in every class of the hierarchy, made as calls need them, with vtables that grow as instances are made). Rejecting abstract generic methods (the visitor pattern is common).

### D-102 · Generic arrow functions in module-scope consts are monomorphized where called (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** A module-scope `const` whose initializer is a generic arrow function or anonymous function expression that makes instances of generic classes (`const make = <T>(x: T): Box<T> => new Box(x)`; directly, or by calling another such const: `makesGenericInstances`) is also a generic function: a call that names the const (`make(5)`) calls the instance its type arguments imply, as a call of a generic function declaration does (D-049; an arrow's expression body is the instance's result), and a use of the const where tsc's context is a non-generic function type is the instance that context implies (D-078). Other uses of the const (passing it on generically, reading `make.name`) see the value the const holds, the erased copy. Calls by name don't read the const, so one before its declaration has run doesn't throw ReferenceError. Generic consts that make no generic instances keep their erased copies everywhere: their dynamic values follow JS where an instance couldn't type-check (`typeof x === "number" ? x + 1 : …` is a branch tsc keeps for a string x).
- **Reason:** Erased copies make instances of generic classes for `any` (`Box<any>`), which don't convert to the call's instance (`Box<number>`): the known issue of D-078, which three tests hit (`generic_values_classes`, and the corpus's Observable and its operators). Arrow functions in consts are the common way to write generic helpers, and their calls name them as declarations' calls do.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Converting instances of one generic class instantiation to another by copying (identity, and later writes through either would differ). Monomorphizing every generic arrow function (a nested one captures its function's variables; and instances of functions that test their type parameters' values by `typeof` have branches that don't type-check, as two tests showed).

### D-103 · Typed arrays move to M5.4 (plan change)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** Typed arrays (`Uint8Array`, `Int32Array`, `Float64Array`, …), which two corpus programs use, are implemented with M5.4's `.par` views, which the spec already defines on typed arrays, rather than in M3.7b; the two programs stay skipped with their cause until then. No scope is dropped.
- **Reason:** Typed arrays are a feature of their own, not a divergence of existing lowering: JS's modular element conversions (ToUint8, ToInt32, Uint8Clamped's rounding), writes out of range that are ignored, `subarray` views sharing a buffer, a numeric default `sort`, console.log's `Uint8Array(3) [ … ]` and JSON's object form. M5.4 needs them as `.par` sources and will define their representation for parallel access.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Typed arrays as plain arrays of their element type (writes would grow them or throw RangeError, conversions would follow ParallelScript's lossless rules, and console.log would print `[ … ]`).

### D-104 · Subclass narrowing, casts of never, fallbacks' contexts, defaults behind function types, literals asserted to unions (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) A union variable tsc narrows to a subclass of one of its class members (`p instanceof King` for `p: Piece | null`) is that subclass, converted with the instance check unions' class narrowing already has. (2) `x as T` where tsc narrowed x to `never` (an assignment in a callback it doesn't follow) is a checked cast, not an upcast: `never` is assignable to everything, but x holds its declared type's values. (3) The right operand of `x ?? y` and `x || y` takes undefined only where the whole does: tsc types y by x's type where nothing types the whole, but the result never holds x's undefined, so `line += m.get(k) ?? s[i]` reads `s[i]` as a string (RangeError past the end, D-015) rather than failing to convert. (4) An arrow with a default given where a function type takes a parameter of `T | undefined` (`(state = [], action) => …` as a `Reducer<S, A>`): the adapter passes undefined as a left-out argument, so the default applies (D-055). (5) An object literal asserted to a union of object types that it fits no member of (`{ type: "@@init" } as A` for an action union) is kept as the object it is, boxed as a union value, as JS keeps it; reads through the union find its properties by its descriptor.
- **Reason:** Five corpus programs (`try_finally_loop_cleanup`, `chess_move_validator`, `grid_bfs_shortest_path`, `redux_store` twice) used these ordinary patterns and failed to compile with "converting X | null to X" or a union conversion.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Converting the asserted literal to a member by copying (no member has its fields; tsc's `as` already says the program vouches for it). A checked conversion of `??`'s result to the required type (it would throw TypeError where D-015 gives RangeError, and only for strings past the end).

### D-105 · Object.isFrozen: a frozen mark per object, outside the objects (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** `Object.freeze(o)` marks o frozen and returns it; `Object.isFrozen(x)` tests the mark, and is true for primitives (strings, numbers, booleans, null, undefined), as in JS. The mark is a runtime hash table of the frozen objects' addresses in memory the collector doesn't scan, behind a mutex; each entry is a disappearing link (`GC_general_register_disappearing_link`), which the collector clears when the object dies, before its address can be reused, so a new object is never taken for a dead frozen one. Writes to frozen objects still aren't stopped (D-086); `Object.seal`, `preventExtensions` and their tests stay unsupported.
- **Reason:** Two corpus programs print `Object.isFrozen` of a frozen snapshot. An object has only its descriptor word for a header, so a mark bit would grow every object or need a frozen twin of each descriptor, which breaks the identity tests (`object->type == type`) that classes and caches rely on. Freezing is rare and testing it rarer, so a side table costs nothing elsewhere.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A header bit (a word more per object). Twin descriptors (identity tests). A finalizer per frozen object (finalizable objects survive an extra collection).

### D-106 · Untyped `new Map()` by context, recursive object types of their own, uninhabited members, generic consts returning generic object types (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) `new Map()` and `new Set()` without arguments or type arguments take the type of their context with null and undefined left out (`current = new Map()` for a `Map<K, V> | null`), and `x ?? new Map()` has x's type without undefined (tsc says `Map<any, any>`, the constructor's first overload). (2) A shape or interface whose own members' types refer to it while it is being made (`left: Expr` in a member of the union `Expr`) stays a type of its own, though another object type has the same members: the union's two members of the same fields (`add` and `mul`) used to share one and leave the other's references on an empty interface, so literals of either fitted neither. (3) A union value converts to a union that has all of its members except those no value has: object types with a required property of type `never` (`NIL: List<never>` as a `List<number>`). The union types themselves keep such members, since tsc infers `never` for type arguments of instances that do run (`fromArray([])`). (4) A module-scope generic const is also monomorphized where called by name (D-102) when its function returns objects of a type that mentions its type parameters, other than an array's (`List<T>`, `Parser<T>`, `Map<K, V>`): its erased copy would make them for any, which doesn't convert to the instance's type.
- **Reason:** Four corpus programs (`config_parser_line_col`, `ratings_recommendations`, `cons_list`, `persistent_queue`) now match Node, and `expr_tree_fold` gets past its union; they used ordinary patterns: untyped `new Map()` in typed places, ASTs with two binary-operator members, persistent lists with a shared `NIL: List<never>`, and helper consts like `const cons = <T>(head: T, tail: List<T>): List<T> => …`.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Dropping uninhabited members from union types (an instance for `never` makes such objects, broke a passing program). Monomorphizing every generic const (D-102's reason: branches that test `typeof` of a type parameter's value don't type-check in instances); consts returning arrays keep their erased copies and checked copies, so checks of what erased code returns stay as tested.

### D-107 · Callbacks' undeclared array parameters (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** An arrow function or function expression passed to an array method takes the shape of the method's callback type (D-041's widening, `closureSignature`), which may give its array parameter a type other than the array's: `unknown[]` for `args.map(…)` on a rest parameter typed by a generic constraint, the guard's narrowed element type after `zoo.filter(canSwim)`. When the literal doesn't declare that parameter (fewer parameters, no rest parameter), it never reads it, so the method's helper passes a left-out value for it rather than converting the array; a callback that declares it still gets the array itself, converted as before.
- **Reason:** Two corpus programs (`writer_logging`, `zoo_animals`) failed with "using a T[] as a value[] where it may be changed or kept": the conversion of the array for a parameter no code reads.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Copying the array into the parameter's type (once per element call, or once per call, for nothing). Not widening callbacks to their context (adapters between function types rely on it).

### D-108 · Spread copies take their source's property order (M3.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** An object literal whose first part is a spread that gives every property of the literal's shape (`{ ...form }`, `{ ...acc, [k]: v }`, `{ ...p, x: 5 }`) takes, at run time, the descriptor of its source when the source is an object of the same shape: the copy then lists its properties in the source's order, as JS's copy does (later properties only replace values). Each shape with several property orders has a table of its order descriptors (`pts_orders_<shape>`), and `pts_object_take_order` looks the source's descriptor up in it; all of a shape's descriptors share its layout. Other spreads keep the order the literal implies statically.
- **Reason:** A shape's static order is its type's property order, which for `Record<K, V>` over a union of literal keys is sorted (tsgo orders literal unions by value), and an object of an interface type may come from a literal of any order; two corpus programs printed such copies in the wrong order.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Ordering fields by first literal seen (still wrong for other literals). An order word in every object (layout change for all objects; descriptors already carry the order).

### D-109 · M4.1 split; generators as state machines over heap frames (M4.1a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** M4.1 is split: M4.1a generators (`function*` declarations, expressions and class methods, `yield`, `yield*`, generator objects, `for…of`/spread/`Array.from`/array destructuring over them), M4.1b the iteration protocol (`[Symbol.iterator]` methods, `Iterable<T>`/`Iterator<T>` values of other objects, Map/Set/array/string iterators as values, generator methods of object literals, `yield` inside larger expressions, the corpus's iterator programs). How generators compile:
  - **Objects.** A generator object is a runtime object (`pts_iterator`, descriptor `Generator`, kind `PTS_KIND_GENERATOR`) followed by its function's frame, allocated with `GC_MALLOC` and scanned conservatively. In the IR its values are dynamic values (`Value`), and the ES library's iterator types (`Generator`, `Iterator`, `IterableIterator`, `IteratorObject`) map to `Value`; `IteratorYieldResult<T>`/`IteratorReturnResult<R>` are shapes `{ done: boolean; value: T | undefined }`, made value first as V8 prints them, so `IteratorResult<T, T>` is one shape.
  - **The transform.** An `ir.Func` marked `Generator` keeps its structured body; `ir.Yield` is a statement. The C emitter writes a starter (the function calls call: it makes the frame and stores the arguments and the closure) and a step function (the body), where every statement-level local (parameters, declared locals, catch bindings, cells) is a member of the frame struct; expression temporaries stay C locals, since a yield is never inside an expression. A yield stores its value and its resume point in the frame, leaves the try blocks around it and returns; the step function starts with a `switch` that jumps to the resume point. C allows a `goto` into loops and blocks, so only `try` blocks need care: a resume point inside one jumps to the try's entry, which pushes its handler again (`_setjmp`) and dispatches on, one try at a time.
  - **Resuming.** `pts_iterator_resume(it, mode, sent)` implements next/throw/return with JS's rules for generators that haven't started, have finished or are running (`TypeError: Generator is already running`). After a yield, the lowering inserts `if resumed by throw: throw sent` and `if resumed by return: return sent`, the latter through the function's `finally` lowering (D-068), so `return()` runs finally blocks, which may yield again. A throw out of the body finishes the generator: the runtime pushes a marker on the handler chain around the step call, and `pts_throw` marks the generators it passes done (no `_setjmp` per resume).
  - **Where a yield can be.** First in its statement: the statement itself, the first variable's initializer, an assignment's right side, or a return's value; elsewhere PTS1001 (M4.1b may spill the earlier operands to the frame). The statement's remainder reads the resumed value (`ir.Resumed`).
  - **Consumers.** `for…of` over an iterator resumes it until done and closes it (`return()`) when the loop ends early by break, return, a jump out or a throw, through the same `finally` lowering (`withFinally`); spread and `Array.from` drain it into an `any[]`; an array pattern takes as many values as it has elements and closes the iterator (`pts_iterator_take`). `yield*` resumes an iterator over its operand (a generator itself, or a runtime iterator over an array's, string's, map's or set's items) as it is resumed, forwarding throw and return.
- **Reason:** The brief and SPEC §5.2 require state machines with GC-managed frames, no native stack per task. Keeping the body structured and letting C's `goto` do the jumping avoids a CFG transform of the IR; every local living in the frame avoids a liveness analysis; making generator objects dynamic values reuses the runtime's descriptors for printing, `typeof`, JSON and `any`. A generator pipeline of two generators over 10 million numbers runs in 0.42 s (Node 0.83 s).
- **Alternatives rejected:** A separate stack per generator (the brief forbids it). An IR-level CFG/state-machine lowering of all control flow (much more code; C does the jumps). A typed IR type per `Generator<T, R, N>` (no gain over dynamic values plus tsc's types at use sites; M4.1b's user iterators need the dynamic form anyway). `_setjmp` around each resume for exception safety (a per-step cost; the marker is two stores).
- **Known gaps:** default parameter values of a generator function are evaluated at the first `next()`, where JS evaluates them at the call; reading a destructured element past an iterator's end into a typed local throws RangeError as array reads do (D-038).

### D-110 · Map and Set iterators as values; Iterable<T> as a dynamic value (M4.1b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) `m.keys()`, `m.values()` and `m.entries()` of a map or set used as values (not straight into `for…of`, spread or `Array.from`, which keep D-056's direct loops) make a runtime iterator (`pts_map_iterator_new`, descriptor `Map Iterator`/`Set Iterator`, kind `PTS_KIND_GENERATOR`) over the map's live iteration (`pts_map_iterator`), so `m.keys().next().value` gives the oldest key and later insertions show; entries are `any[]` pairs, which conversions to `[K, V]` copy into tuples. `MapIterator`, `SetIterator`, `ArrayIterator` and `StringIterator` are iterator types (dynamic values) like `Generator`. (2) `Iterable<T>` is a dynamic value: arrays, strings, maps, sets and generator objects box into it; `for…of` over it opens an iterator (`pts_iterator_open`: a generator itself, or a runtime iterator over the value's items), and spread, `Array.from`, `new Set(…)` and `new Map(…)` read its items as an `any[]` (`pts_value_to_array`, checked into the typed result). (3) An array pattern takes a set's elements or a map's entries. (4) `ReadonlyMap` and `ReadonlySet`, which have no value declaration in the library, are found by their interface declarations. (5) The iterator result's value is converted with a check (a runtime iterator's pairs are dynamic data), and a union property of type `any` reads members' fields of any type by their kind (`IteratorResult<any, undefined>`). (6) An array's `keys()`, `values()` and `entries()` used as values are runtime iterators over what D-085's direct loops iterate (the array itself, read live; its indices; its pairs), and `new Set(m.keys())`/`new Map(m.entries())` of another kind's items go through a map iterator. (7) console.log prints a map's or set's iterator as Node does, with the items it has yet to yield (`[Map Iterator] { 'a', 'b' }`, `[Map Entries] { [ 'a', 1 ] }`, `[Set Iterator] { 'x' }`), and other iterators as `Object [Generator] {}`/`Object [Array Iterator] {}`. `for…of`, spread and `Array.from` straight over `m.keys()` or `a.entries()` keep their direct loops.
- **Reason:** The corpus's LRU caches evict with `m.keys().next().value` (four programs), five spread `m.values()` into `Math.max`, three take `Iterable<T>` parameters; together with a `ReadonlyMap` and destructuring, eleven programs now match Node.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Snapshot iterators (an `any[]` copy of the items: JS's map iterators are live, and the LRU idiom deletes while iterating elsewhere). A typed IR type for `Iterable<T>` (every iterable kind would need a conversion to it; the runtime already iterates any value by its descriptor).

### D-111 · The iteration protocol over the program's objects; yields inside larger expressions (M4.1b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) A class's or object literal's member `[Symbol.iterator]` (an instance method, a generator method or a property holding a function) is the member `@@iterator` in the IR, in descriptors and in method tables (`memberName`, `propertyName` for tsc's `\xFE@iterator@N`); other computed and symbol names stay PTS1001. console.log shows it last as `Symbol(Symbol.iterator): …`, and `Object.keys`, `for…in` and JSON leave it out (`pts_name_is_symbol`); its function is named `[Symbol.iterator]`, and generator functions print as `[GeneratorFunction: name]` (`pts_generator_function`, a descriptor parent). (2) `for…of`, spread, `Array.from`, `new Set`/`new Map`, array patterns and `yield*` over a class, shape or interface value box it and open it at run time: `pts_iterator_open` calls its `@@iterator` method through its dynamic entry (D-074), and an object result that isn't one of the runtime's iterators is wrapped in an object iterator, whose steps call the object's `next(v)`, `return(v)` and `throw(v)` the same way and read `done` and `value` off the results (JS's rules for a missing `return`/`throw`; V8's `Iterator result x is not an object`). `it.next()`/`return()`/`throw()` on an iterator type wraps the same way (`pts_iterator_of`), and `x[Symbol.iterator]()` is `pts_iterator_get` (a generator object or runtime iterator itself, an object's method's result, or a new runtime iterator over an array, string, map or set). A program with a class or shape that has a method or function property named `next` or `@@iterator` gets dynamic entries (its descriptors carry declared types, D-072). (3) Object literals' generator methods are closures of generator functions; literals contextually typed by an iterator result (`return { value, done: false }` for an `IteratorResult<T>`) make the result shapes of D-109, and unions of those count as object unions for `?:`. (4) A yield that isn't first in its statement is hoisted: the statement's once-evaluated expression (an expression statement's, the first variable's initializer, a return's or throw's value, an if's or switch's condition, a for…of's or for…in's iterated value) is lowered as statements that evaluate, in JS's order, each yield and each operand that JS evaluates before one into frame locals, then the statement with those locals in place (`l.yielded`, `l.kept`). Operands without side effects (literals, names, property and element reads of them, function expressions, literals of them) are not kept: they are read after the yields. A yield in a branch of `?:` or the right operand of `&&`, `||`, `??` makes the expression an if statement assigning a local. Yields in loop conditions and updates, case labels, later variables of a declaration, default values and optional chains stay PTS1001.
- **Reason:** The brief's iteration protocol: user iterables are the common way TypeScript programs expose sequences (linked lists, ranges, trees). Going through the runtime by name reuses D-074's dynamic entries, the same path `any` already takes, so classes, literals and interfaces all work without a typed IR iterator type; per-loop cost is one dynamic call, per element one call of the generator's step or of `next()` plus two property reads. Hoisting keeps D-109's design (no yield inside a C expression) while covering `console.log(yield x)`, `(yield a) + (yield b)` and `[yield 1, yield 2]`.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Calling `[Symbol.iterator]` directly for statically known classes (a second path for no gain the dynamic one doesn't give within a factor that matters now; M8 can add it). A typed `Iterator<T>` IR type (every object kind would need conversions to it). Snapshotting a user iterator's items into an array (iterators may be infinite, and `return()` must run when a loop stops early). Spilling every operand before a yield, names and literals included (needless frame locals; a literal's type depends on its context). A general CFG transform for yields in loop conditions (rare in programs; the hoisting covers the statement forms that run once).
- **Known gaps:** names, literals and property reads before a yield are read after it, so a change to them while the generator is suspended shows where JS would see the old value; `x += yield v` reads x after the yield; a static `[Symbol.iterator]`, a generic one, and reading `x[Symbol.iterator]` without calling it are PTS1001; an object literal's iterator result without `done` prints `done: false`.

### D-112 · M4.2 split; async functions over generator frames; promises in V8's job order (M4.2a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** M4.2 is split: M4.2a async functions and `await`, `Promise` (`new Promise`, `then`, `catch`, `finally`, `Promise.resolve`, `Promise.reject`, `instanceof Promise`), the microtask queue in V8's order, unhandled rejections, console.log of promises; M4.2b `Promise.all`, `allSettled`, `race`, `any`, `withResolvers` and top-level await. How it works:
  - **Values.** `Promise<T>` and `PromiseLike<T>` are dynamic values (`Value`); a promise is a runtime object (`pts_promise`, kind `PTS_KIND_PROMISE`) with a state, a result, a list of reactions and `[[PromiseIsHandled]]`. An await's value is converted to the await's type with a check.
  - **Async functions** are generator functions (D-109) marked `Async`: the same frame, step function and resume points, each `ir.Yield` being an await of its value, with only the throw check after it. The starter calls `pts_async_start`, which makes the function's promise and runs the body until it first stops, catching what it throws (the first part runs inside its caller); later parts run from microtasks, under one handler that `pts_run_microtasks` sets for all of them, which rejects the promise of the body that threw. Each frame reuses one reaction for its awaits (it awaits one thing at a time). Awaits go where yields go (D-109's first-in-statement forms and D-111's hoisting, labeled statements included); top-level await is PTS1001 until M4.2b. Async declarations, expressions, arrows, class methods and object literal methods are supported; async generators wait for M4.4.
  - **Order.** ECMAScript's algorithms with V8's choices: `await v` is PerformPromiseThen on PromiseResolve(v) without a then() of its own (one job for a settled promise or a plain value); resolving a promise with a thenable queues NewPromiseResolveThenableJob, which for a promise of the runtime adds an adopting reaction (two more jobs, as V8's then() with the resolving functions takes); `finally` makes thenFinally/catchFinally runtime closures that wait for what `onFinally` returns; reactions are jobs once their promise settles (one queue of reactions per thread, the queue's root uncollectable). Objects of the program's with a `then` method are thenables (their then is called through its dynamic entry), so `then` joins D-111's names that give a program dynamic entries.
  - **Calls.** The program's handlers, executors and thenables' `then` are called through dynamic entries, so a program that calls `then`, `catch`, `finally` or `new Promise` has them (D-072's declared types); the resolving functions and finally's handlers are runtime closures with a dynamic entry and no signature, which typed code reaches through adapters (D-075).
  - **Draining.** In a program with async functions or promises, `main` runs `pts_run_microtasks()` after the modules: jobs run until the queue is empty, then a promise rejected without a handler ends the program as an uncaught exception does (exit code 1, the reason on stderr), as Node's default `--unhandled-rejections=throw` does. M4.3's event loop will drain after each macrotask.
  - **The heap starts at 4 MiB** (`GC_expand_hp`, as Go's minimum heap target; `GC_INITIAL_HEAP_SIZE` overrides it): a loop of three million awaits ran 718 collections of a 540 KiB heap; with it, 235 ms (Node 301 ms); the other benchmarks are 0–7 % faster.
- **Reason:** The brief's state machines with GC-managed frames (D-L14), reusing D-109 so that awaits get try/finally, loops and hoisting for free; dynamic entries are the runtime's existing way to call typed closures; following V8's job order exactly is what makes ordering tests match Node.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A separate async lowering (a second state machine). A `_setjmp` per resume (8 % of the await loop's instructions). An allocated job per reaction (one more allocation per await). Typed `Promise<T>` IR types (handlers of every signature would need typed reaction paths; the dynamic entry already converts). Draining microtasks inside `pts_runtime_exit` (a later `process.exit` must not run them).
- **Known gaps:** a thenable's `then` that is a getter that throws is not caught; console.log of a promise rejected with an Error shows the error without its stack frames, as console.log of errors does (D-068).

### D-113 · Promise's combinators, AggregateError, top-level await (M4.2b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) `Promise.all`, `allSettled`, `any` and `race` are the runtime's PerformPromiseAll/AllSettled/Any/Race over the iterable (opened with `pts_iterator_open`, so arrays, generators and the program's iterables all work, item by item): `Promise.resolve` of each item, then `then()` with element functions (runtime closures sharing the combination's state; allSettled's two share a called flag), the combination settled when the last outstanding item does; what the iteration throws rejects the returned promise. Results are `any[]`s, which the await converts to the static array or tuple type. (2) allSettled's results and `Promise.withResolvers()`'s object are plain objects the runtime makes (hash-map objects of any, so console.log prints them as Node does); their lib types (`PromiseFulfilledResult`, `PromiseRejectedResult`, `PromiseWithResolvers`, and unions of them) are dynamic values whose properties are read and called as `any`'s (`isRuntimeObject` in `isAny`); `withResolvers`' functions convert to their function types through adapters. (3) `AggregateError` is a native error class of the runtime's (`pts_aggregate_error`: Error's members, laid out flat as builtin classes are, then `errors`, an `any[]`), with the constructor `(errors, message?, options?)`; `Promise.any` rejects with one ("All promises were rejected"). (4) Top-level await in the entry module: its top-level code is an async function (D-112), whose module-scope bindings that functions use stay C globals; `main` drains the microtasks and then, if the module's promise is still pending, prints Node's warning and exits with code 13; a rejection is an unhandled rejection (exit code 1). Top-level await in another module is PTS1001.
- **Reason:** The spec's algorithms keep Node's job order (the combinators' ordering test matches); plain objects avoid typing the runtime's objects per type argument, and reading them dynamically is what `any` already does; most programs await a combinator's array right away, where a typed copy happens anyway.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Draining the iterable first (`pts_value_to_array`): a generator's items would run before any then(). Typed shapes for allSettled's results (the runtime would need each instance's descriptors; the union's members share `status: string`, so a dynamic object can't be told apart by type). Async modules other than the entry (needs the module graph's async evaluation order; rare in programs).
- **Known gaps:** console.log of an AggregateError shows `errors` as a property, where Node shows `[errors]` with its stack.

### D-114 · The event loop: Node's timers over one libuv timer; immediates; queueMicrotask (M4.3)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) The runtime has a libuv loop per thread (the main thread's; M5.2 adds one per core), started at the program's start in programs that use timers or immediates (so that timers count from then, as Node's loop time does). `main` drains the microtasks after the modules, then runs the loop until nothing referenced is left (`uv_run` default mode), then checks the entry module's top-level await (D-113). (2) Timers are Node's: `setTimeout`/`setInterval(callback, ms, ...args)` coerce the delay as Node does (anything but a number of 1 to 2^31 - 1 ms is 1), are due their delay after the loop's cached time (`uv_now`), and wait in a heap of our own ordered by due time and then by when they were scheduled, behind one `uv_timer_t` armed for the first. When it fires, every timer due by then runs in order, each followed by a microtask drain (Node ≥ 11); an interval is due again its delay after the round's time; a timer cleared in its own callback isn't rescheduled. `clearTimeout`/`clearInterval` of anything but an active Timeout do nothing; `ref`/`unref`/`hasRef`/`refresh` are Node's (the uv timer is referenced while a referenced timer waits). (3) `setImmediate`/`clearImmediate`: a queue that a `uv_check_t` runs after the poll phase (a `uv_idle_t` keeps the poll from blocking meanwhile), only the immediates queued before the round, each followed by a microtask drain. (4) `queueMicrotask(f)` is a job in D-112's queue; a throw out of it, or out of any timer or immediate callback, is an uncaught exception (exit code 1). (5) `performance.now()` is the time since the runtime started, from `uv_hrtime`. (6) The prelude declares these globals (`Timeout`, `Immediate`, `Performance`); Timeouts and Immediates are dynamic values (runtime objects printed as `Timeout {}`); callbacks are called through their dynamic entries, so a program with timers has them.
- **Reason:** One uv timer and our own heap (as Node's timer lists do) keep the GC out of libuv's structures: the heap and queue live in collected memory the loop state roots, while libuv's handles are malloc'd and never point at collected objects. Following Node's phases (timers, then poll, then check) and its microtask drains makes the ordering tests match Node.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A `uv_timer_t` per timer (handles in collected memory that libuv links together, which must be closed asynchronously before they may be freed). Node's per-duration lists (the heap gives the same order for the cases that are deterministic in Node). Starting the loop lazily at the first timer (Node's loop time starts with the process, so a long synchronous start would shift timers).
- **Known gaps:** console.log of a Timeout prints `Timeout {}` (Node prints its internals); numeric timer ids (`clearTimeout(+t)`) are not supported; there is no I/O yet (M4.5) to order against.

### D-115 · Async generators and `for await` (M4.4)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) An async generator (`async function*`, declarations, expressions, methods) is a generator function marked `AsyncGenerator`: its awaits and yields are both `ir.Yield`s (`Await` marks the awaits, and the step function sets the frame's `awaiting` before it stops), and `yield v` awaits v first, as the spec's yield in async generators does. A call makes an `AsyncGenerator` object (`pts_async_generator_new`) without running the body. (2) The runtime keeps the spec's queue of requests: `next(v)`, `throw(v)` and `return(v)` (`pts_async_iterator_resume`) each add one and return its promise; while the body isn't running, the first request resumes it (or, for a finished generator, completes at once: `return(v)` awaits v first); the body runs until it awaits (resumed from a reaction), yields (the request completes with `{ value, done: false }` and the next one resumes it at once) or ends (`done: true`; a throw rejects the request and finishes the generator). Results are plain objects, which conversions copy into D-109's result shapes (which now always have V8's `{ value, done }` order as a descriptor). (3) `for await (const x of v)` in async functions, async generators and the entry module (where it makes the module async, D-113) opens an async iterator (`pts_async_iterator_open`: an async generator itself, what an object's `[Symbol.asyncIterator]()` returns (`@@asyncIterator`, as D-111 names `@@iterator`), or the spec's async-from-sync iterator over a sync iterable, whose items are awaited), awaits each `next()` (D-112's awaits, in frame locals), reads `done` and `value` off the result, and awaits `return()` when the loop leaves early (`withFinally`). (4) `[Symbol.asyncIterator]` methods and well-known symbol names generalize D-111's (`@@` and the symbol's name; `[Symbol.x]` as JS function names and in stacks). (5) Async functions print as `[AsyncFunction: f]` and async generator functions as `[AsyncGeneratorFunction: f]` (descriptor parents, as D-111's generator functions). (6) Reading `value` of an iterator result union whose members' types differ (`IteratorResult<number, string>`) adds undefined, which D-109's shapes hold; object unions ignore promise members, so `?:` of result literals in an async function's return (`T | PromiseLike<T>`) works. `yield*` in async generators stays PTS1001.
- **Reason:** The same frames and hoisting as D-109 and D-112 (an async generator is both); the spec's queue keeps Node's order when several `next()` calls are pending; plain result objects are what JS makes and what `any` code reads.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A second state machine for async generators. Typed result shapes made by the runtime (it would need each instance's descriptor). A `_setjmp`-free path like D-112's for async generators (they are rarer; one handler per step keeps the code short).
- **Known gaps:** `return(v)` on a suspended async generator resumes it with v itself (the spec awaits v first at the yield); `yield*` in async generators.

### D-116 · M4.5 split; the pts: modules are ParallelScript sources over intrinsics; process and path (M4.5a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** M4.5 is split: M4.5a the `pts:` module mechanism, `pts:process` (and the global `process`) and `pts:path`; M4.5b `pts:fs`'s synchronous API with Node's errors; M4.5c `pts:fs`'s promise API (`pts:fs/promises` and `promises`) over libuv's file requests on the loop. How the modules work:
  - **Sources, not declarations.** A `pts:` module is a `.pts` file embedded in `pts/lib` (`pts:path` is `ptslib:///path.pts`), resolved by the compiler option `paths` (`"pts:*": ["ptslib:///*.pts"]`, no fork edit) and compiled with the program like its own modules, only when imported. `lib.IsLibPath` now means the declaration files (the prelude); `lib.IsModulePath` the modules. Frames and `#line`s name a module by its specifier (`at checkExitCode (pts:process:48)`), as Node's frames say `node:fs`.
  - **Intrinsics.** A module reaches the runtime through functions it declares without a body (`declare function pts_process_cwd(): string;`): a call is a direct call of the runtime's C function of that name (an extern `ir.Func`), with arguments converted to the declared types. They take and return plain types (strings, numbers, string arrays); the modules build objects in ParallelScript. User code can't name them (they aren't exported, and only `pts:` modules' bodiless declarations count).
  - **Globals from modules.** The global `process` is `pts:process`'s default export: the prelude declares it as `import("pts:process").Process`, the lowering binds the global to the module's binding, and the front end runs the module first (before any other) when a module of the program uses the global (`usesGlobal`, by symbol, so a program's own `process` doesn't count).
  - **pts:process** (Node's `node:process`): `argv` (the executable's absolute path twice, as Node's single-executable apps have it, then the arguments, so `argv.slice(2)` is the arguments everywhere), `env` (a copy of the environment at startup, `ProcessEnv`, a hash-map object; writes don't reach the OS environment), `exit(code?)` (flushes and exits at once; no microtasks or timers run; a non-integer code is Node's RangeError), `exitCode` (a setter the runtime's exit reads), `cwd()`, `platform` ("linux"), `arch` ("x64"), `pid`, `execPath`, `stdout.write`/`stderr.write` (through the same C streams as console, so writes interleave in order). Named exports and the default (the same object as the global).
  - **pts:path** is Node's `lib/path.js` posix object ported line by line (taken from Node 24's own sources, including the internal `normalizeString`): `resolve`, `normalize`, `isAbsolute`, `join`, `relative`, `toNamespacedPath`, `dirname`, `basename`, `extname`, `format`, `parse`, `sep`, `delimiter`; the default export (and `posix`) is a `PlatformPath` object whose methods call them and whose `posix` is itself. No `win32` and no `matchesGlob`.
  - **Tests against Node:** the differential harness maps `pts:path`, `pts:process`, `pts:fs` and `pts:fs/promises` to `node:` modules for Node, so tests that import only those are compared with Node like plain TypeScript.
- **Reason:** Writing the modules in ParallelScript keeps the runtime small (six C functions for process), makes them ordinary modules (named, namespace and default imports, values, classes, console.log) without new lowering per function, and ports Node's JS directly, which gives Node's results; it also dogfoods the compiler. `paths` needs no change to the fork. Binding the global to the module keeps one implementation.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Ambient `declare module "pts:fs"` declarations with each function lowered to a builtin (a lowering case per function and per overload; options objects, defaults and function values each need work). A resolver hook in the fork (`paths` does it). A global `process` lowered to builtins separate from the module (two implementations). Building `process.env` lazily (Node's is there at startup).
- **Known gaps:** `process.argv[1]` is the executable, not the source file; `process.env` writes don't reach `setenv`; no `nextTick`, `hrtime`, `memoryUsage`, `on(…)` events, `stdin`; `pts:path` has no `win32`; a module and its intrinsics compile with every program that imports it (about 0.2 s of clang for `pts:path`).

### D-117 · pts:fs's synchronous API over libuv's synchronous calls; Node's system errors; overloads (M4.5b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) `pts:fs` (`pts/lib/fs.pts`, D-116) has Node's synchronous functions `readFileSync`, `writeFileSync`, `appendFileSync`, `existsSync`, `accessSync`, `statSync`/`lstatSync` (with `throwIfNoEntry`), `mkdirSync` (with `recursive`, Node's MKDirpSync ported), `readdirSync` (with `withFileTypes` and `recursive`, Node's breadth-first order), `unlinkSync`, `rmdirSync`, `rmSync` (`recursive`, `force`), `renameSync`, `copyFileSync`, `symlinkSync`, `readlinkSync`, `realpathSync` (Node's JS algorithm, so its errors name the same call and path), `chmodSync`, `mkdtempSync`, `constants`, `Stats` (Node's fields; dates as getters) and `Dirent`; named exports, and a default export object (`FileSystem`) with the same methods plus the deprecated `F_OK`…`X_OK`. (2) The intrinsics (`pts/runtime/src/fs.c`) call libuv's synchronous file system functions (`uv_fs_*` with no loop and no callback), as Node's synchronous functions do, so readdir's sorted order, statx's birth times and the errno values are Node's. (3) Errors are Node's uvException: a native error class named "Error" (`pts_system_error`, kind of `AggregateError`, D-113) with `errno`, `code` (`uv_err_name`), `syscall`, `path`, `dest`, message `CODE: uv_strerror, syscall 'path' -> 'dest'`; console.log, JSON, `e.code` through any, casts to object types and `NodeJS.ErrnoException` (declared by the prelude as pts:process's `ErrnoException`) all see Node's properties. Reading Error's fields (`message`) on an object type that extends Error reads them by name. Node's argument errors carry their `code` (`ERR_INVALID_ARG_VALUE`, `ERR_OUT_OF_RANGE`) as subclasses of TypeError/RangeError. (4) Text only: files are read and written as UTF-8 (reading replaces invalid sequences with U+FFFD as the WHATWG decoder does, `pts_string_from_utf8_lossy`; writing replaces lone surrogates); reading without an encoding (a Buffer in Node) is a compile error until typed arrays (M5.4), through the new `@notSupportedYet <what>` JSDoc tag a pts: module puts on an overload. (5) Overloads: a call of a function or method declared with overloads converts the implementation's result to the chosen overload's result type with a checked conversion (`const s: string = pick("x")` where the implementation returns `string | number`), and calls of overloaded methods now find their implementation.
- **Reason:** libuv's synchronous calls are what Node runs, so behaviour matches without reimplementing it; the module is ParallelScript (D-116), so options objects, overloads and classes are ordinary code; a native error type is the only way to have an object that is an `Error` (instanceof, stack, console.log's form) with Node's own properties.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Calling libc directly (readdir order, birth times and error mapping would differ from Node's). A ParallelScript `SystemError` class extending Error (console.log would show `SystemError [Error]`). A string from `readFileSync(path)` (Node gives a Buffer; a silent difference).
- **Known gaps:** Buffers and binary data (M5.4); encodings other than UTF-8; file descriptors (`openSync`, `readSync`, …), `watch`, streams, `utimes`, `truncate`, `chown`; console.log of a `Dirent` doesn't show Node's `Symbol(type)` entry; `rmSync` of a directory without `recursive` throws an Error with Node's message and code `ERR_FS_EISDIR` where Node's is a `SystemError`; stack frames inside the module may be inlined away.

### D-118 · pts:fs's promise API: jobs on libuv's threadpool settling promises from the loop; internal library modules (M4.5c)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) `pts:fs/promises` (`pts/lib/fs/promises.pts`) has Node's `fs/promises` functions `readFile`, `writeFile`, `appendFile`, `access`, `stat` (with `throwIfNoEntry`, which also covers ENOTDIR, as Node's binding does), `lstat` (without it, as Node), `mkdir` (recursive: Node's MKDirpAsync), `readdir` (`withFileTypes`, `recursive`: Node's depth-first promise order), `unlink`, `rmdir`, `rm`, `rename`, `copyFile`, `symlink`, `readlink`, `realpath` (libuv's realpath, as Node's promise version, so its errors say `realpath`), `chmod`, `mkdtemp`, `constants`, and a default object (`FilePromises`); `pts:fs` exports it as `promises` (`export { default as promises }`, a live binding, so the import cycle between the two modules never reads it early) and its fs object has a `promises` getter.
