(2) The runtime's file system calls are jobs (`fs.c`): `run` makes the system calls (libuv's synchronous `uv_fs_*`) into malloc'd memory without touching the collector, `finish` makes the result or Node's error on the program's thread; synchronous intrinsics run both at once, asynchronous ones queue `run` on libuv's threadpool (`uv_queue_work`, Node's pool of 4) and settle a promise (`pts_promise_pending`/`pts_promise_settle`) in the after-work callback, then run the microtasks (D-114). A pending job's promise and output array live in an uncollectable node until it is done. Intrinsics that return a Promise are marked (`ir.Func.Settles`) so a program that calls them runs the event loop. (3) What the two modules share (types, constants, flags, `Stats`, `Dirent`, option helpers) is `pts:internal/fs`; modules under `pts:internal/` may be imported only by the library (PTS1002 otherwise).
- **Reason:** Running the same synchronous libuv calls on the threadpool keeps one implementation for both APIs and Node's results; doing the system calls apart from making values keeps the collector out of the pool's threads, which aren't registered with it.
- **Alternatives rejected:** libuv's asynchronous fs requests chained per step (open, read, close; several callbacks per call, and buffers that must outlive them). Registering the pool's threads with the collector. Exporting the shared helpers from `pts:fs` (they would be importable names Node doesn't have).
- **Known gaps:** `FileHandle` (`open`), `opendir`, `watch`, `cp`, streams; Node runs more steps per call, so how a call's settling interleaves with other promises' microtasks differs (both settle after the current microtasks; I/O completion order is not deterministic in either).

### D-119 · Functions start on 32-byte boundaries (M4.6)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** Programs and the runtime compile with `-falign-functions=32` (release profile).
- **Reason:** At the M4 benchmarks fib_int had become 35% slower than at M3 (132 → 182 ms) with byte-for-byte the same generated C and the same machine code: only the runtime around it had grown, which moved `pts_fn_fib` from a 64-byte boundary to 48 bytes past one. Clang aligns functions to 16 bytes; the CPU's decoded-instruction cache works in 32-byte windows, so a hot function's speed depended on where the linker put it. With 32-byte alignment (Go's linker's) fib_int is 125–135 ms whatever the layout, async_fib 213 → 183 ms, and the other benchmarks are unchanged within noise.
- **Alternatives rejected:** 64-byte alignment (no faster than 32 here, more padding). Leaving it (benchmarks would move with unrelated runtime changes).

### D-120 · The `parallel` keyword: a marked call of the prelude's `parallel`, typed by tsc (M5.1)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** (1) The fork's parser recognises D-O05's operator: `parallel` followed on the same line by a token that starts an expression, other than `(`, `[`, `.`, `?.`, a template, `,`, `;`, `)`, `}`, an assignment or binary operator, and also `=>`, `:`, `?`, `!`, `++`, `--`, `as`, `satisfies` and `of` (which keep `parallel => x`, `c ? parallel : y`, `parallel!`, `parallel++`, `parallel as T` and `for (parallel of xs)` meaning the identifier). Its operand is a unary expression, as `await`'s is (`parallel f(x) + 1` is `(parallel f(x)) + 1`), or an arrow function. It parses as a call of the identifier `parallel` with the operand as its one argument, marked `NodeFlagsPtsParallel` (a new node flag, bit 29); `parallel for (…)` is the for statement marked the same way, its range starting at `parallel`. No new node kinds. (2) The prelude declares `parallel<T>(task: T): Promise<Awaited<T>>`, so tsc types `parallel e` and `parallel(e)` as `Promise<Awaited<T>>` with no checker change, and a program's own binding named `parallel` shadows it, which is D-O05's rule for `parallel(e)`. (3) The PTS check reports (PTS1002) the operator where a binding named `parallel` is in scope (the call would call the binding) and a function as the operand (`parallel async () => …` would make a promise of the function; the error says to pass the call). (4) Until M5.2 and M5.5 the lowering reports both forms as not supported yet.
- **Reason:** A call node needs no new AST kind, visitor or emitter work, keeps positions and tooling (the LSP sees an ordinary call), and lets the checker type the operator through an ordinary generic signature; the flag keeps the operator form distinguishable. The excluded tokens beyond D-O05's list are those that can follow an identifier in valid TypeScript, so no valid TypeScript program parses differently.
- **Alternatives rejected:** New `ParallelExpression`/`ParallelForStatement` node kinds (generated factory, visitor and checker code to edit across the fork). A checker edit to type the operator (the prelude declaration does it). Typing `parallel async () => …` as running the function (contradicts the brief's `Promise<Awaited<T>>` of e's type).
- **Known gaps:** `parallel e` where a binding named `parallel` is in scope is an error rather than the operator.

### D-121 · The multi-loop runtime: inboxes, home loops for promises, sleeping loops, least-loaded placement (M5.2)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Loops.** The main thread's loop runs the modules; the first `parallel` starts the others (`PTS_THREADS` loops in all if it is a positive number, at most 256, else as many as the cores the process may run on), each a detached thread (bdwgc registers it through `GC_THREADS`' `pthread_create`) with the main thread's stack limit and its own libuv loop, timers, immediates, microtask queue and stack guard. A program that never runs `parallel` keeps one thread.
  - **Messages.** Each loop has an inbox (mutex and condition variable) of messages and of tasks, and a `uv_async_t` (unreferenced) that other loops send to. Promises never change on two threads: a promise belongs to the loop that made it (its home) and settles only there, and a reaction runs on the loop that added it (its owner). Settling or resolving a promise of another loop, and adding a reaction to one, is a message to its home; a reaction of another loop's is a message to its owner, which queues it as a microtask. Each message is a macrotask followed by a microtask drain. Resolving functions decide "already resolved" with an atomic exchange, since two loops may call them.
  - **Tasks.** `parallel e` lowers to `pts_parallel(closure)`, where the closure (no parameters) returns e's value boxed; the call makes the promise on the calling loop and posts a task message. The loop that runs the task calls the closure; a throw rejects the promise; otherwise the value goes through PromiseResolve and its outcome settles the task's promise (on its home). An await or yield in the operand is PTS1001: whether it would suspend the task or the caller is unclear, and the caller's hoisting would see it.
  - **Placement.** A task goes to the loop other than the caller with the lowest load (tasks placed and not finished), ties round-robin. A loop that runs out of work (its libuv loop has nothing referenced left) first takes a task another loop has queued but not started (tasks never move once started), then sleeps. With one loop the task goes to the caller's own inbox, so it still runs later, as a new task (D-O06).
  - **Ending.** A count of loops that aren't sleeping, raised by whoever wakes a sleeping loop before it lets it go and lowered by a loop when it falls asleep with an empty inbox: when it reaches 0, no loop can wake another, so the program is done; the last loop to sleep wakes the main thread's, whose `pts_run_loop` returns (then the top-level await check and exit). `process.exit`, an uncaught exception, an unhandled rejection, a panic and the main thread's normal exit take one process-wide lock first, so only one thread ends the process.
- **Reason:** Settling only on the home loop keeps every promise single-threaded (no locks on the hot single-loop paths, only a thread-local compare), keeps Node's unhandled-rejection rule meaningful (a task's promise rejects in a macrotask of the loop that awaits it, as an I/O promise does), and makes a task's async function resume where it started (D-L14). The sleeping count ends the program exactly when Node would for one loop: nothing referenced anywhere, nothing in flight. Least-loaded placement plus stealing at idle is the brief's option and needs no global queue lock on the common path. Running the closure's code directly needs no dynamic entries.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A lock per promise (a cost on every await of every program, and rejection tracking across threads). A global run queue (one lock for every task; stealing gives the balance). Starting the loops at the program's start (threads for programs that never use them). Settling a task's promise on the loop that ran it (an unhandled rejection would race the caller's await). Running `parallel e` with an await inside as an async task (the caller's statement hoisting would hoist the await out of the task).
- **Known gaps:** a loop with something referenced (a long timer, a server) doesn't take tasks queued on others until it runs dry; a loop that sleeps doesn't run its unreferenced timers until it wakes; awaiting a promise of another loop that rejects races with the rejection check of its home loop (if the rejection is seen first it is unhandled); an async generator driven from two loops at once runs its body on both; allocation-heavy tasks stop scaling at 4 loops on the 4-core test machine (bdwgc's parallel marking and stopping 8 threads per collection; M5.7); ThreadSanitizer can't run programs that collect on several threads (bdwgc's signals), so the TSan check (clean) ran with collection off.

### D-122 · The fork-join pool: Rayon's join and splitter over Chase–Lev deques; pool threads work for the operation's loop (M5.3)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Threads.** The pool has `PTS_THREADS` − 1 workers (the count of D-121, at most 256), started at the first operation (`pthread_once`), detached, registered with bdwgc through `GC_THREADS`' `pthread_create`, never stopped; the thread that starts an operation is the last one, so `pts_pool_threads()` is the count operations run on. A loop thread gets a deque the first time it joins and keeps it. With `PTS_THREADS=1` there are no workers: `pts_join` runs a then b and `pts_par_run` runs one piece, on the caller (D-O06).
  - **Deques.** Lê, Pop, Cohen and Zappa Nardelli's Chase–Lev deque of job pointers (one word per slot, D-L13); buffers are collected memory, so a buffer a push replaced lives while a slow thief still reads it; `steal` tells an empty deque from a lost race, and a thread looking for work retries a round in which it lost one. Deques register under a lock and are published by a release store of their count.
  - **join.** Rayon's: b's job lives on the caller's stack (join returns only once b is done), is pushed, a runs, then the caller pops b back and runs it (not stolen, the common case, with no synchronisation beyond the pop), or runs other work until the thief sets b's latch. Throws are caught around each closure and rethrown when both are done, a's first, so the leftmost piece's exception wins in an operation. b gets `migrated` true when a thief runs it.
  - **Waiting and sleeping.** A thread with nothing to run (its own deque, then stealing from a random start) yields 64 times, then counts itself sleepy, issues a fence, reads the event counter, looks once more, and sleeps on a condition variable until the counter moves or its latch is set; a push, or a thief finishing a job, fences and, if anyone is sleepy, moves the counter under the lock and broadcasts (Rayon's protocol, simplified).
  - **Splitting.** `pts_par_run(op, count, result)` runs `op->leaf` over pieces and folds results with `op->combine` (left then right), splitting with Rayon's adaptive splitter: a budget of `pts_pool_threads()` splits halved at each level, reset when a piece migrates, and pieces no shorter than `op->min_length`. The right half's result lives on the stack of the piece that split.
  - **Whose work.** Each job records the loop of the operation it belongs to. A thread running a job of a loop that isn't its own sets `pts_current_loop` to that loop plus `PTS_LOOP_FOREIGN`: promises it makes belong to that loop, every change to a promise and every reaction becomes a message to its loop (the index never matches a home), and `queueMicrotask` posts the job there; they run once the loop's thread is free again. Timers and asynchronous I/O from a pool worker throw `Error` until M5.4 decides otherwise (a worker has no loop).
  - **ThreadSanitizer.** A `ThreadSanitize` toolchain profile; the pool's and the loops' C tests run under it with `GC_DONT_GC=1`, since bdwgc's stop-the-world signals fail under TSan. It found one race (the deque count published before the deque), fixed.
- **Reason:** Rayon's design is what D-L16 asks for, and its join keeps the uncontended case nearly free; job pointers on the stack need no allocation; marking foreign threads in the one variable promise.c already compares keeps promises single-threaded (D-121) with no new check on the loop's own paths.
- **Alternatives rejected:** N workers plus the helping caller (one thread more than cores). A global injector queue for operations started on loop threads (D-L16 wants the starting thread to help, so it needs a deque anyway). Epoch reclamation for deque buffers (the collector does it). Running a pool worker's promise work on the worker (it has no loop to drain microtasks or wake on, and would race the loop that owns the promises).
- **Known gaps:** a waiting thread may run a long stolen job and return late (Rayon's leapfrogging); wake-ups broadcast to every sleeper; a loop thread helping in one operation can run pieces of another loop's operation, delaying its own return.

### D-123 · M5.4 split; `.par` and pts:parallel as a ParallelScript library over three pool intrinsics (M5.4a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Split.** M5.4 becomes M5.4a (`.par` on arrays and ranges with `map`, `filter`, `forEach`, `reduce`, `some`, `every`, `find`, `count`, `sum`, `min`, `max`, `toArray`; `pts:parallel` with `range` and `join`; the branded callback type), M5.4b (`flatMap`, `sort`/`sortBy` as a parallel merge sort) and M5.4c (typed arrays and `.par` on them). `.par` comes first because it is M5's headline and needs nothing from typed arrays.
  - **Library.** `pts/lib/parallel.pts` (`pts:parallel`) holds generic classes `ParallelArray<T>` and `ParallelRange`, the class `Range` and the functions `range(start, end?)` and `join(a, b)`, written in ParallelScript over three intrinsics: `pts_parallel_for(leaf, count)` runs `leaf(from, to)` over pieces covering 0..count (D-122's `pts_par_run`), `pts_parallel_reduce(leaf, combine, count)` folds the pieces' values (boxed, one per piece) left to right, and `pts_parallel_join(a, b)` is D-122's join. Monomorphized generics make each method's leaf a typed loop calling the callback directly. Results keep the elements' order; `find` gives any match (the leftmost found among the pieces that ran); `some`, `every` and `find` stop looking once a piece finds an answer.
  - **Semantics chosen.** `reduce(callback, initial)` folds elements with a callback of two elements, which must be associative, from `initial`, its identity (Rayon's reduce; JS's accumulator type can't be combined across pieces). `sum`, `min` and `max` work on any element type through `Number`, as `Math.min`/`Math.max` do (0, Infinity, -Infinity for none); `count(predicate)` counts the elements predicate holds for; predicates return `unknown`, as Array's do. `range(end)` is 0..end-1, `range(start, end)` start..end-1 (integer parts); a Range is iterable and has `length`, `toArray()` and `par`.
  - **`xs.par`.** The prelude declares `interface Array<T> { readonly par: ParallelArray<T> }` (an import type of pts:parallel); the lowering makes `xs.par` a `new` of the class instance that tsc's type of the expression names, with xs; the front end adds pts:parallel to the modules a program runs when one of its modules reads `Array.par`, as it does for the global `process`.
  - **Brand.** `ParallelCallback<F> = F & ParallelBrand`, where `ParallelBrand` is an interface with one optional property; tsc relates functions to it without the weak-type check (it is an intersection member), so any function of type F passes. The lowering takes such an intersection as F (`withoutParallelBrand`). M5.5's guardrail will find parallel bodies by it.
  - **Promise work in callbacks.** An operation started on a loop's thread first makes sure the loop's inbox exists, and before it returns (or throws) applies the messages waiting there: the promise work and `queueMicrotask` jobs of callbacks that ran on other threads (D-122) are then queued on the loop as if the callbacks had run there, in no particular order between pieces.
- **Reason:** Writing the methods in ParallelScript reuses the whole compiler (closures, generics, typed arrays of results, exceptions through join) and keeps the runtime to three thin intrinsics; a method is a few lines, so M5.4b and later helpers are cheap to add. Absorbing the messages makes a program's microtasks independent of which thread ran each callback.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Runtime-implemented methods over dynamic values (boxing and a dynamic call per element). Generated IR helpers per method, as the sequential array methods have (much more compiler code for the same loops). A conditional `par` type giving number arrays a numeric view with `sum`/`min`/`max` (fails for generic element types; methods with a `this` parameter aren't supported). Leaving callbacks' microtasks for the loop's next macrotask (their order against the caller's microtasks would depend on the threads).
- **Known gaps:** `ReadonlyArray` and tuples have no `.par`; a `.par` method called through `any` isn't supported; allocation-heavy callbacks scale poorly (D-121's collector findings, M5.7); timers and async I/O from callbacks on pool workers throw (D-122).

### D-124 · `.par.flatMap`, `sort` and `sortBy`: Array's sort in place, as a parallel merge sort (M5.4b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - `xs.par.sort(compare?)` is Array's sort: in place, stable, returning the array, by `compare` or, without one, by the elements' strings with undefined last. `xs.par.sortBy(key)` sorts in place, stably, by `key(element)` (numbers or strings, compared with `<`), computing the keys in parallel first. `xs.par.flatMap(callback)` flattens one level, in order. On a range they give new arrays.
  - The sort is Rayon's parallel merge sort, in `pts:parallel`: the array splits into one piece per pool thread (at least 2,048 elements each), Array's own sort sorts each piece, and the pieces merge through a buffer, a merge of more than 2,048 elements splitting at the longer run's middle element and a binary search in the other run (first element not before it, or first after it, which keeps equal elements in order) into two merges joined in parallel. With one thread it is Array's sort alone (D-O06). The default order and `sortBy` sort the indices and then move the elements, so the comparator compares strings or keys computed once.
- **Reason:** In-place sorting returning the array is what `sort` means in JS (tie-breaker 2). On this machine, 2M numbers: Array's sort 250–300 ms; the parallel sort 253 ms on one thread, 195 ms on two, 126–133 ms on four. More pieces per thread (2 or 4) meant more merge levels and was slower (151 and 178 ms on four).
- **Alternatives rejected:** Returning a new sorted array (that is `toSorted`). A fixed 2,048-element leaf (ten merge levels for 2M elements; 800 ms on one thread). Sorting the elements with a comparator that converts to strings at every comparison (two conversions per comparison).
- **Known gaps:** merges copy back from the buffer at every level instead of alternating buffers; a comparator that isn't consistent gives some order (never out-of-bounds access), as in JS.

### D-125 · M5.4c split; typed arrays and ArrayBuffer as builtin classes over a view of a buffer (M5.4c1)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Split.** M5.4c becomes M5.4c1 (typed arrays and ArrayBuffer themselves) and M5.4c2 (`.par` on typed arrays), so the arrays land, with their tests and the two corpus programs, before the parallel view over them.
  - **Which.** `Int8Array`, `Uint8Array`, `Uint8ClampedArray`, `Int16Array`, `Uint16Array`, `Int32Array`, `Uint32Array`, `Float32Array`, `Float64Array` and `ArrayBuffer`. `BigInt64Array`/`BigUint64Array` (D-O08), `Float16Array`, `DataView`, `SharedArrayBuffer` (its type is ArrayBuffer's, so `ArrayBufferLike` is ArrayBuffer), resizable and transferable buffers and the base64/hex methods are PTS1001.
  - **Representation.** Builtin classes the runtime defines, as Date (D-087): a typed array is a `pts_typed_array` (its ArrayBuffer, a pointer to its first element, a fixed length, a byte offset), an ArrayBuffer a `pts_arraybuffer` (length and bytes in one allocation, which the collector never scans). `subarray` and views of a buffer share its bytes. The length never changes, so racing threads can't reach outside a view (D-L13).
  - **Elements are numbers.** A read gives a number (an `Int32Array` element is not an `i32`), so arithmetic on elements is JS's; a write takes a number and converts it as JS does (ToInt8/ToUint8/…/ToUint32 modulo 2^n, ToUint8Clamp, rounding to float). Reads and writes compile to inline functions per typed array (`pts_X_get`, `pts_X_set`). Reading an index the array doesn't have gives undefined where any value can go and throws RangeError where a number is required (as D-015 and D-038 do for strings and arrays); writing one is ignored (JS's behaviour, and nothing is lost: the array can't grow).
  - **Methods.** The ones without callbacks, the constructors' forms and `from`/`of` are runtime functions (V8's RangeError messages); the ones with callbacks reuse the array helpers (D-042), whose loops read the typed array; `map` and `filter` make typed arrays of the same kind, `sort(compare)` sorts the elements as a number[] and writes them back. A typed array is a `number[]` (a copy) wherever it is spread or iterated except `for…of`, which reads it live.
  - **Printing and JSON** are Node's: `Uint8Array(3) [ 1, 2, 3 ]` grouped in columns as number arrays are, the hidden properties under `showHidden`, `ArrayBuffer { [Uint8Contents]: <…>, [byteLength]: n }`, and JSON's object form `{"0":1}`.
- **Reason:** Typed arrays are numeric storage for performance-minded programs (and `.par` sources), so element access must be a bounds check and a load or store, which a view struct with an inline accessor per kind gives; a sieve over a 20M-element Uint8Array and a sum over a Float64Array run about as fast as Node inside a function. Builtin classes give `instanceof`, conversions from `any`, unions and fields with no new IR types.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Typed arrays as arrays of the integer types (`u8[]`): they grow, throw on writes out of range, convert losslessly and print as arrays (D-103). A ParallelScript library class per typed array: its methods would have to be found by name on tsc's lib types, and element access would still need the compiler. Elements read as their integer types: `a[i] + 1` would wrap at 255 for a Uint8Array, unlike JS.
- **Known gaps:** methods with callbacks through `any` throw TypeError; typed arrays don't take part in `Object.assign`, spreads into objects or `structuredClone`; `toLocaleString` uses the default English format (D-083).

### D-126 · `.par` on typed arrays: ParallelTypedArray, a merge sort generic over its container (M5.4c2)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **View.** The prelude gives each typed array `readonly par: ParallelTypedArray<X<TArrayBuffer>>` (`pts:parallel`), which `parallelView` makes as it makes `ParallelArray`s (D-123). `ParallelTypedArray<A extends TypedArrayLike>` has ParallelArray's methods over the elements as numbers, written in ParallelScript; each instance is compiled for its typed array, so `items[i]` is the inline accessor. The constraint is an interface (`length`, a numeric index signature, `slice`, `subarray`, `set`, `sort`), not the union of the typed arrays: tsc types a value of a type parameter whose constraint is a union as that union where it is the receiver of a property access, so the instance's typed array would be lost.
  - **Results.** `map` and `filter` make typed arrays of the view's kind, as the typed array's own `map` and `filter` do (`map` writes into `items.slice()`, `filter` into `items.slice(0, kept)` after collecting the kept numbers per piece); `toArray()` is a `number[]`, `flatMap` an array of the callback's values. `sum`, `min`, `max`, `reduce`, `some`, `every`, `find` and `count` are ParallelArray's.
  - **Sort.** `sort()` is the typed array's own sort (numeric, -0 before 0, NaN last, in place): with one thread that sort alone (D-O06); otherwise D-124's merge sort, whose pieces the typed array's own sort sorts in place and whose merges compare in the same order. `sortRun` and `mergeRuns` are generic over an `Indexed<T>` container (an array or a typed array) and `sortRun` takes the leaf sort, so the one merge sort serves both. `sort(compare)` and `sortBy(key)` sort a `number[]` copy and write it back.
- **Reason:** The typed array's own sort (a C sort per kind) is faster than a comparator called through a closure, so pieces sort with it and only the merges compare through `compareNumbers`. Measured on this machine under a load of about 3: a sqrt map over 10M Float64Array elements 106 ms on 1 thread, 78 ms on 2; `sum` 28.5, 13.9 and 6.8 ms on 1, 2 and 4; `sort()` of 2M doubles 137, 113 and 131 ms against 134–145 ms for the typed array's own sort (Node's: 223 ms).
- **Alternatives rejected:** A `ParallelTypedArray` per typed array (nine copies of the methods). Sorting every typed array as a `number[]` with `compareNumbers` (2M doubles: 228 ms on 2 threads, slower than the sequential C sort). An intrinsic that makes a typed array of A's kind (intrinsics are lowered once per declaration, not per instance).
- **Known gaps:** `sort(compare)` and `sortBy` copy through a `number[]`; the speedups above are small under the machine's background load and will be measured properly in M5.7.

### D-127 · `parallel for` as a leaf closure over pieces, cancelled by the first throw; the guardrail as a lexical check (M5.5)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Lowering.** `parallel for (const x of items) body` evaluates the items once into a local: an array or a typed array as it is, pts:parallel's `Range` as its start and end, any other iterable (strings, maps, sets, generators, `any`) as `Array.from` would copy it. The body becomes a leaf closure `(from, to)` holding a loop over those indices that binds x (or its pattern) and runs body; `continue` continues that loop, so it goes to the next index. The runtime's `pts_parallel_for_each(leaf, count)` runs it over the pool (D-122) and returns when every piece is done.
  - **Errors.** A piece calls the leaf on runs of 256 indices and starts no run once any run of the operation has thrown; the first run to throw records its error, which is rethrown after joining. Runs already going finish their current indices ("as far as possible", D-L16).
  - **Restrictions** (PTS3002, a new code): only `for…of` over a declared variable can be `parallel`; `return`, `yield`, `await`, `for await`, `break` out of the body and labelled jumps to statements outside it are errors; `continue` may name the loop's own label.
  - **Guardrail** (PTS3001, a new code; D-L17): in a parallel body (the operand of `parallel`, also written `parallel(e)`; a `parallel for` body; a function passed for a `ParallelCallback` parameter, written inline or named: a function declaration or a const's function expression), an assignment to a variable declared outside the body is an error: `=`, compound assignments, `++`/`--`, destructuring and `for…of`/`for…in` targets, to a `let` or a parameter (`var` is unsupported anyway). It is lexical: functions made inside the body are part of it, though some of them may run later on the loop (a microtask); fields and elements may be assigned. The message suggests `.par.reduce`, `.par.sum` or an `Atomic` (pts:sync).
- **Reason:** A leaf over a piece runs the body as a plain loop (no call per element, unlike a `forEach` callback), `continue` needs no translation, and the loop's captures are the closure machinery's (D-041). Runs of 256 make cancellation cheap: a setjmp per run. A lexical check is predictable and needs no escape analysis; a deferred closure can keep its state in an object.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Lowering to `.par.forEach(callback)` (a call per element, `continue` as `return`). Rethrowing the lowest piece's error (D-122's join order): with cancellation, a later piece can stop a lower one before it reaches its own error, so "lowest" isn't the first. Exempting nested functions from the guardrail (a nested function called in the body races just the same).
- **Known gaps:** a `parallel for` over a `Map` or `Set` copies its items first; the guardrail doesn't follow calls (a function called from the body that assigns an outer variable isn't reported, by D-L17's "no other checks").

### D-128 · M5.6 split; pts:sync as a ParallelScript library over atomic cells and futex locks in ArrayBuffers (M5.6a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Split.** M5.6 becomes M5.6a (`AtomicInt`/`Atomic<int>`, `Mutex<T>` with `withLock` and async `lock`, `Once`) and M5.6b (`Channel<T>`), which builds on M5.6a's locks.
  - **Library.** `pts:sync` (`pts/lib/sync.pts`) is ParallelScript over seven intrinsics: 64-bit atomic load, store, add and compare-exchange on an 8-byte cell, and lock, try-lock and unlock on a 4-byte word, each kept in the first bytes of an `ArrayBuffer` the class makes (typed arrays' buffers, D-125: aligned, never moved, never scanned). The lock is Drepper's futex mutex (0 free, 1 held, 2 held with waiters) after 100 tries, so any thread may release it.
  - **Atomics.** `AtomicInt` holds an `int` (i64) with `load`, `store`, `add` and `compareExchange`, all sequentially consistent; `add` wraps as `int` arithmetic does; `add` and `compareExchange` return the value before, as JS's `Atomics.add` and `Atomics.compareExchange` do. `Atomic<T extends int = int>` is a subclass of `AtomicInt`: the family has one member, `int`, for now.
  - **Mutex.** `new Mutex<T>(value)`. `withLock(fn)` takes the lock (blocking the thread: for short critical sections), runs `fn(value)` and releases it, also when fn throws. `lock()` returns a promise of a `MutexGuard<T>` (`value` to read and write, `unlock()`): free, it resolves at once; held, the resolver waits in a queue (behind a second lock) and a release passes the lock, still held, to the first waiter by resolving its promise, which settles on the loop that made it (D-121), so the loop never blocks. A guard used after `unlock()` throws `Error`.
  - **Once.** `run(fn)` runs fn the first time; a concurrent `run` blocks until it is done; a throw leaves it not run (the next `run` tries again); `done` tells.
- **Reason:** Writing the classes in ParallelScript keeps the runtime to small, separately testable primitives (as D-123 did for pts:parallel) and gives the async lock promises and cross-loop settling for free. A lock any thread may release is what the async hand-off needs (a pthread mutex must be unlocked by its owner). JS's Atomics fix the return values; tie-breaker 2.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A runtime object type per primitive (descriptors, printing and conversions for each). pthread mutexes (owner-only unlock). `Atomic<T>` for every integer type now (each needs its own width's wrapping and compare; nothing asks for them yet).
- **Known gaps:** a `withLock` on a loop thread while a `lock()` holder awaits on the same loop deadlocks, as any blocking lock would; no fairness between `withLock` callers; no `Atomic<i32>` etc.

### D-129 · Channel<T>: a locked queue with queues of waiting receivers and senders, whose promises settle on their loops (M5.6b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** `Channel<T>` in `pts:sync` is ParallelScript over D-128's lock: one lock (an ArrayBuffer word) guards the queued items, the resolvers of receivers waiting and the senders waiting with their items. `new Channel<T>(capacity = Infinity)`; a capacity below 0 (or NaN) is a RangeError; 0 makes every send a hand-over. `send(v)` hands v to the first waiting receiver, else queues it if fewer than `capacity` are queued, else waits (its promise resolves when a receiver takes v or room frees up). `recv()` takes the first queued item (moving the first waiting sender's item in), else the first waiting sender's item, else resolves to undefined if the channel is closed, else waits. `close()` marks it closed, resolves waiting receivers as done and rejects waiting senders; later sends reject; items already queued stay for receivers; a second close does nothing. `for await` uses an async generator over the same `#next()`, which tells done apart from an undefined item. The promises are made on the caller's loop and settled by whichever thread serves them, through D-121's messages; nothing blocks a loop but the short lock.
- **Reason:** Go's and Rust's channel semantics (FIFO, bounded back-pressure, rendezvous at 0, drain after close) in JS's promise idiom; a send to a closed channel rejects rather than panicking (tie-breaker: JS errors are exceptions). Writing it in ParallelScript reuses promises, async generators and cross-loop settling (D-123's approach).
- **Alternatives rejected:** A `recv()` that rejects once closed (a loop over `recv` would need try/catch; undefined matches iterators' end). Throwing on a second `close()` (Go panics; JS APIs mostly make it a no-op, as `AbortController.abort()` does).
- **Known gaps:** no `select` over several channels, no `trySend`/`tryRecv`; `recv()` can't tell an undefined item from the end (use `for await`, or an item type without undefined).

### D-130 · M5.7 split; stress programs repeated nightly; race tests of the invariant; races on maps and arrays made safe (M5.7a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Split.** M5.7 becomes M5.7a (stress tests, the nightly job, the race-safety tests and the runtime fixes they call for) and M5.7b (the parallel benchmarks with speedups, and the collector's scaling), so the fixes land before anything is measured.
  - **Stress tests.** `pts/tests/stress/*.pts` take the run tests' directives, with `// expect:` and `// threads:` required. `TestStress` runs each under each thread count `PTS_STRESS_REPEAT` times (default 1, so the normal suite runs each once) and reports the first run that differs; `make test-stress` runs only them; `.github/workflows/pts-nightly.yml` runs them 100 times a night (and on demand with a count). `run/parallel_stress.pts` moved there as `tasks_gc.pts`.
  - **Race tests.** The `race_*` programs race threads on arrays, maps, sets and hash-map objects, fields and variables, strings and typed arrays, and print only what holds however they interleave: every value read is one some thread wrote (a hole reads as undefined where any value can go), lengths and sizes stay within what was ever added, nothing crashes or hangs. A racing operation that throws RangeError (a `pop` of an array another thread emptied, a write past a length another thread shrank) is a surprising value, not a failure.
  - **Fixes they called for** (with an audit of the runtime by a review subagent): a map's size, a plain counter, went below zero under racing deletes and read as 1.8e19, which console.log, console.table and JSON's reviver allocate by (an abort); it is now signed, reads below zero as 0, and tables are sized from the live entries they count; appends racing on one entry each index it, so the index could fill and lookups loop forever; probes now stop after one round (the entry is then lost to lookups); an append after a replace that racing appends had filled wrote past the table; it is dropped. On arrays: `with` wrote past its copy if another thread shrank the array between its length check and the copy (it throws RangeError now); `shift` of an empty buffer published under a length of 1 published length 2^64 − 1 (it throws now); a sort with a comparator read each slot twice (a hole could reach the comparator); growth and `unshift` copied shared buffers with memcpy (D-038 says one load per element). `new Set(array)` and `filter` through `any` read holes as elements (a NULL string hashed; `undefined` stored as a pointer): they now skip them, as JS's filter skips holes, through a new `pts_array_load`. A hash-map object's key-order snapshot kept keys in malloc'd memory the collector doesn't scan. A typed array's `sort` ran qsort over elements other threads may write: it sorts a private copy.
- **Reason:** The invariant is Java's: races lose updates but never break memory. Plain stores with bounded probes and clamped sizes cost nothing on the single-threaded paths every program runs; per-element copies cost about nothing measurable (user time equal on 20 million pushes).
- **Alternatives rejected:** Entries claimed by a CAS on `used`, an atomic size and a CAS on each index slot (two or three locked instructions per new key in every program, and two threads adding the same key could still both add it). A lock per map (a cost for every program, and a lock in the hot path the invariant was designed to avoid).
- **Known gaps:** under races a map may hold a key twice, lose entries and report a size that differs from its entries; publication of new objects relies on x86's store order (an ARM port needs release stores where fresh objects are published).

### D-131 · Parallel benchmarks with sequential and goroutine twins; bdwgc marks on two threads (M5.7b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Benchmarks.** Four parallel benchmarks in `pts/bench/`, each with a `threads` file (`1 2 4 8`) that makes `run.sh` run ParallelScript once per `PTS_THREADS` value and report the speedup over one thread: `mandelbrot` (a 2400×1600 view, at most 500 iterations, `.par.forEach` over rows into a Uint16Array), `sum_reduce` (100 million doubles filled by `parallel for`, then `.par.sum`, `.par.reduce` with an addition and `.par.max`, two rounds; the values are multiples of 1/4 below 256, so every order sums exactly), `word_count` (64 files of 100,000 generated words, made once under `/tmp` by a Park–Miller generator exact in doubles, counted by `.par.map` into maps and merged) and `fan_out` (64 tasks of 1 to 8 million terms, started with `parallel` and awaited with `Promise.all`). Node and Bun run `main.ts`, the same program without `.par`/`parallel` (they have no shared-memory threads to compare), Go a goroutine twin over every core; every implementation prints the same.
  - **Collector.** bdwgc marks on 2 threads (`GC_set_markers_count(2)`; `GC_MARKERS` overrides), not one per core.
- **Reason:** Measured on this 4-core, 8-thread laptop part (i5-8250U) under a background load of 2 to 5: with one marker per core (8), 32 tasks building binary trees took 1.21 s on one thread against 0.75 s with 1 or 2 markers, json 1.18 s against 1.04 s with 2, word_freq 1.57 s against 1.55 s; binary_trees was best with 2 (1.68 s, against 1.85 s with 1); the extra markers burn the cores parallel code needs (user time 50 to 70% higher). One marker was best only for 8 threads all allocating (1.23 s against 1.85 s with 2). A larger initial heap, or one per thread, helped no case consistently. The benchmarks (`docs/BENCHMARKS.md`, M5.7b): mandelbrot 5.78× on 8 threads (6.5× Node, 0.93× Go), fan_out 3.26× (3.4× on 4), word_count 2.22×, sum_reduce 1.83× (memory-bound, with a serial zeroing of its 800 MB array).
- **Alternatives rejected:** One marker (10% slower on single-threaded binary_trees, and parallel free-list building needs two). Larger heap blocks to refill thread-local free lists less often (bdwgc needs aligned mappings for blocks above the page size; a deeper change). A minimum heap per thread (no consistent gain).
- **Known gaps:** allocation-heavy parallel code stops scaling at 4 threads here: bdwgc refills every thread's free lists under one lock, and its collections stop every thread; a mutator that allocates less (escape analysis, M8) or a collector with per-thread heaps is the fix. Double `%` calls C's fmod (sum_reduce fills with `&` instead: integer inference is M8 work); a 800 MB typed array is zeroed by one thread before the parallel fill.

### D-132 · M6.1 split; RegExp's core: JS patterns translated to PCRE2's syntax, matched in UTF-16 indices (M6.1a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Split.** M6.1 becomes M6.1a (PCRE2 vendored and built; the runtime's core: compiling JS patterns, matching, V8's SyntaxErrors), M6.1b (`RegExp` in the language: literals, the constructor, properties, `lastIndex`, `test`, `exec` and its match arrays, printing, `any`) and M6.1c (the String methods that take regexes, and `normalize`, moved from M6.1). The owner's documentation refresh, added as M6.1d while this session ran, keeps its number and place after them.
  - **PCRE2.** pcre2-10.49 as a pinned submodule (D-O09), its 8-bit library built by the toolchain from a checked-in configuration (`pts/third_party/pcre2-config`, as libbacktrace's), with Unicode support and without the JIT (whose sljit is a submodule of PCRE2's own; the interpreter first, the JIT if benchmarks ask). Patterns compile in UTF mode with Unicode properties (`UCP`) for `\s` and caseless matching, `\d`, `\w` and `\b` kept ASCII (`EXTRA_ASCII_BSD`, `EXTRA_ASCII_BSW`), invalid UTF-8 in subjects allowed (lone surrogates are WTF-8, D-014), `[]` and `[^]` as JS reads them, backreferences to groups that took no part matching empty, and, without the `u` flag, no case equivalence between ASCII and non-ASCII characters (`EXTRA_CASELESS_RESTRICT`: `/k/i` doesn't match the Kelvin sign, as in JS).
  - **Translation.** The runtime translates each JS pattern into PCRE2's syntax rather than relying on options, where the two read the same text differently: `.` excludes JS's four line terminators (`(?s:.)` with `s`), `^` and `$` with `m` are lookarounds over them, `$` without `m` is the end only, `\v` is U+000B, an escaped letter JS doesn't know is that letter (Annex B; PCRE2 gives `\a`, `\e`, `\z` … meanings), `[` in a class is literal (no POSIX classes), a class escape next to `-` makes the `-` literal (Annex B), `{` that starts no quantifier is literal, `\uXXXX` pairs join into one code point, decimal escapes are backreferences when the group exists and legacy octal escapes otherwise, `\p{Script=…}`, `gc=`, `scx=` and long category names are PCRE2's forms, and `(?ims-ims:…)` modifiers reach PCRE2 only for `i` (`m` and `s` change how `^`, `$` and `.` are written). Named groups become numbered groups and the runtime keeps the names, so any JS identifier is a name and `\k<name>` is a numbered backreference. The translation also finds the errors V8 reports (unterminated group or class, unmatched `)`, nothing to repeat, ranges and `{}` numbers out of order, invalid groups, escapes and names, duplicate names, unknown references, lone quantifier brackets) with V8's messages (`Invalid regular expression: /(/: Unterminated group`); PCRE2's message stands in for anything else it rejects.
  - **Matching.** `pts_regex_exec` matches from a UTF-16 index, anchored or not, and reports every group's start and end in UTF-16 indices; a `pts_regex_cursor` (a UTF-16 index with its byte offset) lets a loop over a string's matches convert positions in one pass (ASCII strings convert for free). Compiled code and match data are collected memory with no pointers; compiled code is read-only, so threads share a regex.
- **Reason:** PCRE2 is D-O09's choice; a translator gives exact JS meanings where options can't (PCRE2's newline conventions have no "LF, CR, LS, PS but not CRLF", and its escapes differ), and checking the syntax ourselves gives V8's messages, which programs print. Expected results in the tests are Node's.
- **Alternatives rejected:** `PCRE2_ALT_BSUX`, `PCRE2_NEWLINE_ANY` and `PCRE2_MULTILINE` instead of translating (wrong for CRLF, VT, FF and NEL, and for `\a`-style escapes). PCRE2's own named groups (PCRE2 names are ASCII words; JS's are identifiers, `$` included). Matching on a UTF-16 copy with PCRE2's 16-bit library (a copy of every subject, and a second library).
- **Known gaps:** without `u`, JS sees an astral character as two units: `.` or a class matches one of them there, while here it matches the whole character (`/a.c/` matches "a😀c" here, not in JS), and patterns written against surrogate halves (`[\uD800-\uDBFF][\uDC00-\uDFFF]`) don't match; `\s` doesn't match U+FEFF; `\w` with `iu` doesn't match U+017F and U+212A; duplicate group names in different alternatives (ES2025) are a SyntaxError; the `v` flag is an invalid flag; a match that exceeds PCRE2's backtracking limit throws `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded`.

### D-133 · RegExp in the language: a builtin class with a lastIndex field; match arrays as string[]s with their properties after them (M6.1b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **RegExp** is a builtin class the runtime defines, as Date and the typed arrays are (D-087, D-125): `pts_regexp` holds the compiled code (D-132), the pattern as written (OriginalSource, which `source` escapes) and `lastIndex`, a real field of the class, so compiled code reads and writes it (`re.lastIndex = 0`, `+=`, `++`) as any field. Its other properties (`source`, `flags`, the flag getters; `unicodeSets` is false), its methods (`exec`, `test`, `toString`), its constructor's forms (`new RegExp(p, f)` with a string or RegExp pattern, `RegExp(…)`, which returns a RegExp pattern itself when flags are undefined) and `RegExp.escape` (ES2025) are runtime functions that calls reach directly. exec and test follow RegExpBuiltinExec: lastIndex is read (ToLength) and written only with `g` or `y`, set to the match's end after a match and to 0 after none; `y` anchors the match at lastIndex.
  - **Literals.** Each evaluation of a regex literal is a new RegExp (its own lastIndex), over code compiled the first time the literal runs and kept in a static of the generated C per literal (`ir.RegExpLiteral`); racing threads may both compile it, and either result will do, since compiled code is immutable. An invalid literal tsc lets through throws its SyntaxError when it first runs.
  - **Match arrays.** exec's result (RegExpExecArray, and String.match's RegExpMatchArray in M6.1c) is a `string[]` to the program, so indexing, `length`, destructuring, `for…of`, spreads and every array method work unchanged; the runtime allocates it as a `pts_match_array` (a `pts_array` followed by `index`, `input` and `groups`) with its own descriptor, which `m.index`, `m.input` and `m.groups`, console.log (`[ 'a', undefined, index: 1, input: 'xac', groups: undefined ]`), `Object.keys` and `any` read. A group that took no part is a hole (D-038): undefined where any value can go, and, as a loose local is (D-090), a `string | undefined` where the context tests it (`m[2] || "px"`, `??`, `=== undefined`, conditions, a parameter that takes undefined) or makes a string of it (templates); elsewhere a checked string (`TypeError: Expected string, got undefined (group 2 of the match took no part in it)`). Copies (`slice`, `filter`) are string arrays whose holes print as undefined (`pts_type_match_copy`). `RegExpExecArray | []` (from `re.exec(s) ?? []`) is a `string[]` too.
  - **groups** is a hash-map object of strings (D-059) printed as Node prints a null-prototype object; a named group that took no part keeps its key with a zero value, which `pts_map_get` treats as a hole of a hash-map object of pointers: listed by `Object.keys` and printed as undefined, undefined where any value goes, `RangeError` where a string is required.
  - **Printing and conversions** are Node's: `/a\/b/gi` for console.log, String, templates, `+` and `join`; JSON `{}`; `typeof` "object"; `o.constructor.name` "RegExp". Through `any`: the properties, lastIndex writes (other properties: V8's TypeError), `exec`, `test`, `toString`, and a match array's `index`, `input` and `groups`. String's `split`, `replace` and `replaceAll` through `any` throw TypeError for a RegExp argument until M6.1c (they would have split on its string).
  - **Left out** (PTS1001): `compile`, the legacy statics (`RegExp.$1` …), a match's `indices` (the `d` flag is accepted and reported), classes extending RegExp.
- **Reason:** A builtin class reuses everything classes already have (instanceof, conversions from `any`, unions, fields, printing hooks) and a real field gives lastIndex its JS semantics, including compound assignments, with no new IR. Match arrays as `string[]` keep regex results first-class arrays, which is how programs use them (`const [, y, m] = …`, `m.slice(1).map(Number)`); a trailing struct gives them their extra properties without a new IR type. Groups that took no part are rare but real (`(px|em)?`), and the idioms that handle them (`||`, `??`, tests) are the ones D-090 already makes work for loose locals. Caching compiled code per literal keeps `/\d+/.test(s)` in a loop from compiling on every iteration (as V8 also caches).
- **Alternatives rejected:** A RegExp in ParallelScript over intrinsics (lastIndex and the properties would still need a builtin layout, and console.log of matches the runtime's inspector). A match array as a builtin class of its own (every array method, destructuring and iteration would need a second implementation). Elements of match arrays as dynamic values (`m[1]` would need a check everywhere, and `string[]` code couldn't take a match). Compiling a literal on each evaluation, or a global cache keyed by pattern (a lock on every evaluation).
- **Known gaps:** a hole passed to a typed callback of a match array's methods, or met by `for…of`, throws TypeError where JS passes undefined; named groups that took no part give `RangeError` where a string is required; no `indices` for `d`; the gaps of D-132 (astral characters without `u`, `v` flag).

### D-134 · M6.1c split; the String methods with RegExps as @@match … @@replace in the runtime, loops that keep lastIndex out (M6.1c1)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Split.** M6.1c becomes M6.1c1 (`match`, `matchAll`, `search`, `split`, `replace` and `replaceAll` with RegExps) and M6.1c2 (`normalize`, which shares nothing with RegExp and needs Unicode's normalization data).
  - **Runtime functions over a boxed pattern.** Each method is a runtime function taking the pattern as a dynamic value, so one function serves a RegExp, a string and a `string | RegExp` (as JS's methods dispatch on the argument): `match`, `matchAll` and `search` make a RegExp of a non-RegExp (with `g` for matchAll); `split`, `replace` and `replaceAll` use a string as a string. A pattern whose static type is a string keeps D-082's lowering. They follow RegExp.prototype's @@match, @@matchAll, @@search, @@split and @@replace: `match` with `g` gives every match's text (null for none), without `g` exec's match array; `matchAll` requires `g` (V8's TypeError) and gives an iterator of match arrays from the RegExp's lastIndex, computed when called (no program code can run between its steps, so nothing tells); `search` ignores and keeps lastIndex; `split` cuts at the matches of a sticky splitter (found by searching from each position, which finds the same matches), splices in the captures (holes, undefined, where they took no part) and stops at ToUint32 of the limit; `replace` and `replaceAll` (TypeError without `g`) substitute GetSubstitution's references (`$$`, `$&`, `` $` ``, `$'`, `$n`/`$nn` with the two-digit fallback, `$<name>`). An empty match moves the next search on by one unit, or with `u` by a whole surrogate pair.
  - **lastIndex and threads.** The loops over every match of a global RegExp keep their position to themselves and set lastIndex to 0 when they end, which is what JS leaves; no program code runs during them, so no program can tell, and a global RegExp shared by threads (`const WORD = /\w+/g` used in `.par.map`) gives every thread the right matches. Non-global `replace`/`match` read and write lastIndex for `y` as exec does.
  - **Replacement callbacks.** As @@replace does, every match is found first (`pts_regexp_replace_matches`, match arrays), then a generated helper calls the callback for each with the match's arguments, an `any[]` whose length depends on the match (match, groups, index, input, and the groups object when the RegExp has names): each parameter takes its element converted to its type (undefined past the end), a rest parameter the elements from its position on; then `pts_regexp_replace_with` assembles the string. Through `any` the runtime calls the closure's dynamic entry. Callbacks with default parameter values, and callbacks with a pattern that may be a string, are PTS1001 for now.
  - **Positions** are converted in one pass per loop: matching and slicing carry a `pts_regex_cursor` (`pts_regex_slice` in the core).
- **Reason:** A boxed pattern mirrors JS's own dispatch and covers unions without a lowering per combination; matching in C keeps the loops off the collector's and the program's way. Keeping the loops' positions private costs nothing in JS terms and removes a race a parallel program would hit with the most common way of writing a regex (a module-level constant). Collecting matches before calling the callback is the spec's order.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Lowering the loops in IR over exec (slower, and each step would publish lastIndex to other threads). A callback helper per group count (the count isn't known for `new RegExp(s)`). A lazy matchAll iterator in the runtime (a generator object for a loop no program code can interleave with).
- **Known gaps:** console.log of matchAll's iterator prints `Object [Array Iterator] {}` (Node: `Object [RegExp String Iterator] {}`); a typed callback parameter given undefined for a group that took no part throws TypeError; without `u`, empty matches next to astral characters skip the whole character (D-132's gap).

### D-135 · normalize over tables generated from Node's ICU, with ranks for the combining classes (M6.1c2)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** `String.prototype.normalize` implements UAX #15 in the runtime (`normalize.c`): each character's full decomposition (canonical, or compatibility for NFKC and NFKD), canonical ordering (a stable sort of each run of non-starters by combining class), and for NFC and NFKC canonical composition with blocking; Hangul syllables decompose and compose by arithmetic. The tables come from this Node's ICU, as the case tables do (D-016): `normalization_tables.mjs` takes each code point's NFD and NFKD, and each primary composite's two characters (the last character of its decomposition it composes with, and the NFC of the rest). ICU doesn't expose combining classes, so the table holds their ranks: a mark is a non-starter if NFD swaps it with U+0334 (class 1) or U+0301 (class 230), and marks are sorted by whether NFD swaps a pair; canonical ordering and blocking only compare classes, so ranks normalize as the classes do. The form is NFC when undefined; another string than NFC, NFD, NFKC and NFKD is V8's RangeError. ASCII strings are their own normal forms; a lone surrogate is a starter nothing decomposes or composes with. The string corpus compares every code point of planes 0 and 1 and the CJK compatibility ideographs, in the four forms, and random sequences of marks, jamo and compatibility characters with Node.
- **Reason:** Generating from Node rather than from UCD files keeps the one source of truth the differential tests already use (Node 24's ICU is Unicode 17, ahead of the machine's Python and ICU), needs no download, and regenerates in a second when Node changes.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Vendoring ICU (tens of megabytes for one method). Python's unicodedata (Unicode 15 here: new characters would differ from Node). Real combining-class numbers from UnicodeData.txt (a download, for numbers only their order uses).
- **Known gaps:** none known; regenerate the tables when Node's Unicode version changes (the corpus fails until then).

### D-136 · M6.2 split; pts:http as Bun's serve with the Fetch standard's classes, in ParallelScript over a C HTTP/1.1 server (M6.2a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Split.** M6.2 becomes M6.2a (the server on the loop that calls `serve`: the API, HTTP/1.1, tests) and M6.2b (`reusePort`: a listener on every loop with SO_REUSEPORT, and the hello-world and JSON-echo benchmarks with `oha`).
  - **API.** Bun's `serve({ port, hostname, reusePort, fetch, error })`, whose `fetch(request, server)` returns a `Response` or a promise of one, with the Fetch standard's `Request`, `Response` and `Headers` (names without case, values combined with ", " and set-cookie apart, iteration in name order, `Response.json`, the status converted as WebIDL does and checked with undici's RangeError). Defaults: port 3000, hostname 0.0.0.0; a handler that throws gets the `error` option's response, or its error on stderr and a 500. `Server` has `port`, `hostname`, `url` and `stop()`. Bodies are text (UTF-8; invalid bytes in a request become U+FFFD). The library is ParallelScript (`pts/lib/http.pts`) over four intrinsics: listen, respond, port and stop.
  - **Server.** The runtime (`http.c`) runs HTTP/1.1 on libuv's TCP on the loop that listens: requests are read one at a time per connection (pipelined ones wait in its buffer, so responses keep their order), with Content-Length or chunked bodies (up to 64 MB; heads up to 64 KB), `Expect: 100-continue`, keep-alive as HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/1.0 say, and 400, 413 and 431 answered by the server. A request reaches the program as a callback with a connection id (a slot of the loop's table and its generation), so an answer for a connection that closed meanwhile is dropped, and a stale or forged id can't reach another connection. The response's framing (content-length, connection) is the server's; headers with line breaks are dropped. `stop()` closes the listener and the idle connections, and busy ones after their responses, so a program whose server stops ends.
  - **The loop.** A program that listens runs the event loop after its top-level code (`ir.Func.Listens`), and every loop flushes stdout before it waits for I/O, so a server's log lines appear as it runs.
  - **Compiler fixes it needed:** an object literal's method may fill an optional function-typed property (`error(e) { … }` for `error?: (e: unknown) => R`), and an object literal for a union with one hash-map object member it fits, and no other object type, is that hash-map object (`{ "content-type": "…" }` for `HeadersInit`).
- **Reason:** `fetch` (M6.3) returns a Response, so one set of Fetch classes serves both, and Bun's handler-returns-a-response shape fits async functions better than Node's streaming `res.write`. A server in C over libuv keeps parsing and I/O off the collector and the program, and ids keep the program's side memory-safe whatever it does with them. The server's tests talk raw TCP and Go's HTTP client to a ParallelScript server, since Node has no `pts:http`.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Node's `http.createServer((req, res) => …)` with streams and events (needs EventEmitter and streams for little gain). Vendoring llhttp (a parser generator's output for what a few hundred lines do). Passing connections to the program as pointers (a program could forge or reuse them).
- **Known gaps:** no TLS, no streaming bodies (requests and responses are whole strings), no WebSocket or upgrade, no request timeouts; `Request.url` comes from the Host header; console.log of these classes doesn't print as Node's.

### D-137 · pts:http on every loop: a SO_REUSEPORT socket per loop, bound by the caller and handed over by call messages; regex matches that allocate nothing; server benchmarks with oha (M6.2b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Every loop.** `serve({ reusePort: true })` starts the other loops (as the first `parallel` does) and gives each loop a listening socket of its own. The calling thread makes them all: SO_REUSEADDR and SO_REUSEPORT, the first bound to the requested port, the others to the port it got, all listening. An address in use or a bad port therefore still throws from `serve`. Then each loop takes over its socket (`uv_tcp_open`, `uv_listen`): the calling loop at once, the others through a new message kind, CALL (`pts_post_call`), which runs a runtime function on a given loop in order with its other messages. The kernel spreads new connections over the sockets. A connection stays on the loop that accepted it, and its requests' handlers start there, with the promises, timers and tasks they make. Without `reusePort`, or with `PTS_THREADS=1`, there is one socket, on the calling loop.
  - **State per loop.** A server has a listener record per loop holding that loop's connections, which only that loop touches. Connection ids carry the loop (a 24-bit generation, an 8-bit loop and a 20-bit slot: 52 bits, exact in a double), and `pts_http_respond` finds nothing for another loop's id. `stop()` sets an atomic flag once, then stops each loop's listener on that loop: directly for the caller's own, by a CALL message for the others, which arrives after the message that started it. A loop that gets its socket after the stop closes it. `stop()` from any loop (or a pool thread) is now safe; before, it touched the listener from whatever thread called it.
  - **Handlers run in parallel.** With `reusePort`, `fetch` runs on several loops at once, as a function passed to `.par` does. Captured variables and module state are shared as in any parallel code: memory-safe by D-L13, but a counter needs `AtomicInt`. D-L17 forbids new parallelism checks without the owner, so the guardrail does not extend to `fetch`; the guide and the spec say so instead (listed in `docs/agent/REVIEW_LATER.md`).
  - **Date header.** Responses carry `date` (RFC 9110 asks it of origin servers with a clock; Node, Bun and Go send it), formatted once a second per loop, unless the program set its own. The server's own error responses have it too.
  - **Faster requests.** Profiling the hello-world benchmark under load (gdb stack samples) showed four loops slower than one: every regex match allocated PCRE2's match data and its 20 KB frame vector through the collector, a large object under bdwgc's global lock each time, and `pts:http` checked every header with regexes. Now each thread keeps one malloc'd match-data block, in which PCRE2 also keeps its frames between matches, so a match allocates nothing. The request headers the C parser has checked (it now also rejects NUL and CR in values, as RFC 9112 asks, with 400) go into `Headers` unchecked, through a function that Headers' static block gives the module. Header names are checked by character codes, and a value goes through the trimming regex only when it starts or ends with whitespace. Hello world on one loop went from 26k to 54k requests/s, and on four loops from 23k to 114k.
  - **Benchmarks.** `pts/bench/http_hello` and `http_json` (an order posted as JSON, parsed into typed records and totalled), each with twins for Node (`node:http`), Bun (`Bun.serve`), both one process, and Go (`net/http` over every core). `pts/bench/serve.sh` (`make bench-http`) builds them, starts each server, checks that all answer the request the same, loads it with oha (64 connections, 2 s of warm-up, 10 s measured) and prints requests per second; ParallelScript runs under each `PTS_THREADS` in the benchmark's `threads` file. A server benchmark is a directory with a `request` file (method and path, then the body), and `run.sh` skips those.
- **Reason:** Binding on the calling thread keeps `serve`'s errors synchronous and each libuv handle on its own loop's thread (libuv isn't thread-safe). SO_REUSEPORT lets the kernel balance connections with no shared accept and no hand-off between loops, and connections that never move keep their state single-threaded. A thread-local match block is the usual way to use PCRE2 from threads; regexes are common in servers, so taking the collector's lock per match would have capped every parallel program that matches, not only `pts:http`.
- **Alternatives rejected:** One listening loop handing accepted connections to the others (a message per connection, and that loop becomes the bottleneck). Binding on each loop (bind errors would arrive after `serve` returned). Several processes like Node's cluster (no shared heap, which is the point of ParallelScript). Applying the guardrail to `fetch` (a new check; D-L17).
- **Known gaps:** connections aren't rebalanced, so a loop busy in a long synchronous handler holds up its own connections while other loops idle; with few connections the kernel's hash can leave loops unused. Other processes of the same user can bind the port too while it is open with SO_REUSEPORT, as with Bun's `reusePort`.

### D-138 · M6.0a split by the review's findings
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** M6.0a (safety stabilization, from the independent review of 2026-10-12) becomes M6.0a1 (finding 1: loop-owned handles), M6.0a2 (findings 2 and 3: narrowed captured unions, captured-cell and class TDZ, constructor-field NULL reads), M6.0a3 (finding 4: checked OrdinaryToPrimitive, no signature-blind function casts) and M6.0a4 (finding 10: the shared-memory contract, typed arrays without tearing, one async generator from several loops, the remote rejection race). The scope and the gate (no known crash path in an accepted program) are unchanged.
- **Reason:** Each finding is its own subsystem (the loops, the lowering of variables, the runtime's calls of program methods, the memory model) and needs its own repros and tests; one session can't do all four well. Finding 1 goes first because M6.2b's CALL messages are the mechanism it needs.
- **Alternatives rejected:** One task for all of M6.0a (a session would end with it half done and nothing ticked).

### D-139 · Timers and immediates belong to their loop; other threads clear them through atomic flags and reach the loop by messages (M6.0a1)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** A `Timeout` records the loop that made it, and only that loop touches its heap, its slot and the loop's count of referenced timers. `clearTimeout`/`clearInterval` from any thread (another loop, or a pool thread in a `.par` callback) exchanges the timer's atomic `active` flag, so a callback that hasn't started never starts after the call returns, then has the owning loop take the timer out of its heap: directly when the caller is that loop, else by a CALL message (D-137). `ref`/`unref` store the atomic `referenced` flag (so `hasRef` answers at once) and have the loop apply it to its count; `refresh` runs on the loop. The loop skips a due timer whose flag another thread cleared before its message came. An immediate's `active` flag is atomic too: any thread may clear it, and the loop's check phase exchanges it before running the callback. The review's finding 1 (`await parallel clearTimeout(timer)` segfaulted on the other loop's NULL timer state) is the regression test, with the other operations, from other loops and pool threads.
- **Reason:** The review asked for owner-loop dispatch rather than forbidding shared handles: a timer is a value programs pass around, and `parallel` makes any function run elsewhere. Clearing through the flag gives the call its sequential meaning (after `clearTimeout` returns, the callback won't start), which a message alone wouldn't when the timer is due before the message arrives; the heap and counts stay single-threaded.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A lock per loop's timer heap (every timer operation would pay for a case few programs have). Throwing for timers of another loop (the review rejected forbidding shared handles). Moving the timer to the caller's loop (changes which loop runs the callback, and its promises' home).
- **Known gaps:** `refresh` from another loop takes effect when the loop takes its messages, so the new due time counts from then; a callback already running on its loop when another thread clears the timer finishes (as in any threaded runtime). The audit for other loop-owned handles found `pts:http`'s (made loop-safe by D-137; `pts_http_respond` drops an answer for another loop's connection, which library code never sends) and async generators, which M6.0a4 takes.

### D-140 · Narrowed reads that a call may have invalidated are checked for every type; captured locals have a ready flag (M6.0a2a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Split.** M6.0a2 becomes M6.0a2a (variables: the review's findings 2 and 3) and M6.0a2b (classes: TDZ for classes, and object fields read before the constructor sets them), since the class cases need their own mechanism.
  - **Narrowed reads.** tsc keeps a property, and a variable that a closure assigns, narrowed across calls it doesn't follow. A pre-pass marks every variable or parameter that a function other than its own assigns (any assignment form, destructuring and `for…of` targets included). A narrowed read of such a variable, or of any property or element (D-089 checked these only for primitives and classes), is boxed and converted as a dynamic value is: primitives by tag, classes by descriptor, other object types (shapes, arrays, tuples, interfaces, records, maps, functions) by `pts_value_convert`, unions by their members. A value that is no longer of the narrowed type throws `TypeError` (`Expected string, got number`) where the read used it as one (the review's probe 1 segfaulted; a property narrowed to an array that a call made a string read the string as an array). `pts_value_convert` now accepts an object of the check's own descriptor first, so the usual case costs a call and a compare. The json, binary_trees, word_freq and string_building benchmarks compile to the same C or run at the same speed.
  - **Captured locals before their declaration.** The cell pass already knows a local captured before its declaration ran (D-041); such a local is now `Early`: its cell holds a ready flag after the value, set by the declaration (and copied with the cell for each loop iteration), and every read or write of it from a closure checks the flag and throws `ReferenceError: Cannot access 'x' before initialization`, as Node does. Uses in the declaring function itself need no check: tsc rejects them above the declaration.
- **Reason:** The brief puts memory safety first (§3); a narrowed read is an unchecked extraction only where nothing can change the value between the test and the read, which tsc can't promise for properties and closure-assigned variables. Checking all those reads rather than only primitive ones closes the same hole for object members, which was worse (wrong field offsets rather than a wrong pointer). The flag lives with the cell because closures already reach locals through their cells, so it costs nothing elsewhere.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Following calls to see what they assign (whole-program analysis, and indirect calls defeat it). Checking every narrowed read of every variable (most can't change: tsc follows assignments in their own function). A separate ready cell per local (closures would capture two pointers). Reading the changed value dynamically as JS does (it would need every member access on narrowed values to go dynamic).
- **Known gaps:** a narrowed value that changed throws TypeError where Node reads the new value (SPEC §8); a module-scope class used before its declaration ran, and object fields read before the constructor sets them, are M6.0a2b.

### D-141 · Classes have a ready binding that `new` checks; an unset object field is undefined, checked where it is used as an object (M6.0a2b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Class TDZ.** Classes are module-scope (inside functions they are PTS1001), so a class's readiness is a module-scope binding's (D-021): each non-generic class gets a hidden binding, declared after its static initializers (where JS initializes the class binding), and `new C(...)` checks its ready flag before evaluating the arguments wherever the declaration may not have run (a function, or a module of an import cycle). A use before throws `ReferenceError: Cannot access 'C' before initialization`, as static fields already did. Static methods called early and `instanceof C` still run.
  - **Unset object fields.** A class instance's field of an object type is NULL until its initializer or the constructor sets it, which a method the constructor calls (or a base constructor calling an override) can read first; using it crashed. JS sees undefined there, so: objects' truth is now tested (NULL is false; it was folded to true, which broke `if (!this.cache) this.cache = …` for fields declared with `!`); `x === undefined`/`x == null` of an object test it; `??=` and `??` (with a value of the field's type, on a field of a local's object) treat it as nullish; boxing gives undefined (`console.log`, `String`, `JSON`, `any`); identity comparisons compare it as no object. Every other read of such a field goes through `pts_check_field_set`: reading a member from it or calling its method throws Node's `TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'x')`; any other use as an object throws `TypeError: The field 'f' is undefined: it is read before the constructor sets it`. Shapes' fields are set when objects are made, and string fields start as "" (D-028), so neither is checked.
- **Reason:** Memory safety first (brief §3): the read is where the emitter still knows the value came from a possibly-unset field, so checking there needs no analysis of where the value flows. The uses exempted are those where JS's undefined has a meaning the program relies on (lazy initialization), and none of them dereferences the pointer. A check is a compare and a predicted branch, and only class fields of object types pay it.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Initializing object fields to a sentinel object (it would need every type's layout). A signal handler turning NULL dereferences into TypeError (not async-signal-safe with the collector and longjmp exceptions). Holding object fields as dynamic values (every field read would need a check and a conversion). Checking every dereference of every object (cost everywhere, for a value only fields produce).
- **Known gaps:** `typeof` of an unset field is "object"; `?.` on one throws TypeError where Node gives undefined (the chain's type has no undefined to give); `??` with a fallback of another type keeps the field; a use as an object other than a member read or method call throws earlier than Node and with another message; a static method called or `instanceof C` tested before the class's declaration ran works where Node throws.

### D-142 · toString and valueOf are called through their dynamic entries, in OrdinaryToPrimitive's order (M6.0a3)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** The runtime calls a program's `toString` or `valueOf` (of a class or an object literal's shape) only through the method's dynamic entry, with no arguments, as JS's conversions do: the entry gives each parameter undefined (optional ones are left out; one that can't take undefined throws TypeError, as calls through `any` do, D-074) and returns the result as a dynamic value whatever its declared type. A program with such a method gets dynamic entries and declared types, as programs that use `any` or the iteration protocol already did. `String(x)`, templates and `join` use OrdinaryToPrimitive with the string hint (toString, then valueOf), `+`, `==` and comparisons the default and number hints (valueOf, then toString); a class without `toString` has Object.prototype's "[object Object]", and if neither method gives a primitive the conversion throws Node's `TypeError: Cannot convert object to primitive value`. The remaining calls of program functions from the runtime were audited: getters and setters are called by their declared kind (`pts_call_getter`, `pts_call_setter`, now also for optional properties' accessors in `object.c`), `toJSON` and timers' callbacks through dynamic entries, and library closures (`pts:http`, `pts:parallel`, tasks, JSON revivers) have the function types their declarations give them.
- **Reason:** The review's probe 14 (`toString(): any { return 7 }` joined by `join`) segfaulted: `array.c` called every method named toString as returning a `pts_string *`. A method's own C signature can be anything a TypeScript method can declare, so the runtime must not call it blind; the dynamic entry is the one signature that always fits, and the emitter already knows how to write it.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Checking the method's result kind and parameter count in the method table and calling directly (parameters with defaults and Given flags make the C signature vary further). Compiling every conversion of a class instance statically (conversions through `any`, arrays' `join` and the inspector happen in the runtime).
- **Known gaps:** a parameter that can't take undefined throws where JS passes undefined; a hash-map object's `toString` property isn't called (JS would call it); `[Symbol.toPrimitive]` isn't supported.

### D-143 · The shared-memory contract: atomic typed arrays, generators one thread at a time, async generators on their loop, handlers counted when added (M6.0a4)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **The contract** (the review's finding 10, the owner's ruling 10) is written down in `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §4.13: shared slots are one aligned word or less; a listed set of runtime structures is accessed with `__atomic` builtins; generated code's object fields, cells, bindings and number arrays use plain one-word loads and stores, which ISO C calls data races, relying on clang's x86-64 code (one instruction per aligned access of up to 8 bytes, atomic on x86-64; no split or repeated loads of heap words); code reads a shared slot once before checking and using it; no `memcpy`/`memmove` of numbers other threads can read; x86 store order publishes new objects (D-130); what isn't safe by these rules belongs to one thread.
  - **Typed arrays.** Elements are read and written by relaxed atomic accesses of their C type (`PTS_ELEMENT_LOAD`, `PTS_ELEMENT_STORE`), in the inline accessors generated code calls and in the runtime; bulk copies (`set`, `slice`, `copyWithin`, `sort`'s copies, construction from a typed array, `ArrayBuffer.prototype.slice`) go through `copy_shared`, a `memmove` made of relaxed atomic units of the element's size, 8 bytes wide where both addresses agree modulo 8 (an alignment ladder at each end), and `reverse` swaps by atomic units. A double written by one thread is never seen torn by another. Views of different widths over one buffer see each other's bytes; an element is whole only to views of its width or narrower.
  - **Sync generators.** A generator's body runs on one thread at a time: `pts_iterator_resume` claims its `executing` flag and releases it on every way out (`pts_throw` releases the generators it unwinds); a resume while another thread runs it throws `TypeError: Generator is already running`, as a resume from its own body does. The claim is an atomic exchange only once `pts_threaded` is set (before the first loop or pool thread starts); until then only one thread runs the program, and a relaxed load and store do.
