Once threaded, each resume's value also goes to a per-thread slot that `pts_iterator_value` reads, so a thread gets the value its own resume made, not another thread's.
  - **Runtime iterators shared by threads** (found while testing the generators): an array or tuple iterator loads its position once (a tuple's elements aren't bounds-checked, so the second read could index past them), and a map or set iterator steps a private copy of its table and position (`follow` re-read the shared table between the bound and the index).
  - **Async generators** belong to the loop that made them (`pts_iterator.home`), as promises do (D-121): `next`/`return`/`throw` from another loop or a pool thread make the request's promise on the calling loop and post the request to the home loop with `pts_post_call`, so the queue and the body change on one thread, in the order each loop asked. One async generator drained from four loops at once segfaulted before.
  - **An audit of the runtime against rule 4** (read once, check, then use; by a review subagent, each finding checked): `console.log` of an error counted its entries from `name`, `message`, `cause` and the optional fields, then read them again to fill them, so a field another thread set in between wrote past the entries (a heap overflow): each is read once and the entries have room for every field; the inspector's slots (a hole test, then the use), errors' names and messages, and a date's time are read once (a date set to NaN between the test and the format threw from `console.log` and `JSON.stringify`); a loop's per-iteration cell copy used `memcpy` (`pts_cell_copy` copies by atomic units now: `pts_copy_shared`, moved to `shared_memory.c` with the typed arrays' helpers). Two bugs outside rule 4: `JSON.stringify`'s stack of frames and the reviver's key snapshot were `malloc`'d, which the collector doesn't scan, though a frame's key may be the only reference to its string (collected memory now); and a pool thread's `parallel` that started the loops threw the timers' error from inside `pthread_once` (the main loop's slot is made by a once of its own now, which can't throw).
  - **Handlers from other loops.** Adding a reaction to another loop's promise stores its `handled` flag (sequentially consistent) before posting the THEN message, and the home loop's unhandled-rejection check loads it sequentially consistently. Every outcome then matches an order of the two loops' steps: if the check misses the flag, the await came after the check, which a single loop would also report. A rejection in the same macrotask as another loop's await was reported unhandled before (D-121's known gap).
- **Reason:** The owner's ruling: never torn values, crashes or out-of-bounds accesses, which rules out non-atomic multi-word copies of shared numbers; the review: x86 publication isn't permission for C data races, so the C-level rules had to be explicit, and where they rely on the compiler rather than ISO C, say so. Relaxed atomics compile to the same `mov` on x86-64, so element accesses cost nothing measurable (a 20M sieve the same, a Float64Array sum 3–4% slower) and copies about 10–15% (they no longer use vector moves). Claiming generators keeps every frame single-threaded, which the narrowing and the resume points assume, without a lock: a second thread gets the error a reentrant call gets, and nothing can deadlock (a waiting resume could deadlock a body that joins work which resumes it). Owning async generators reuses D-121's messages, so single-loop programs pay nothing. Setting `handled` at the call is the only point where the caller's order is known.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Relaxed atomics for every field and element access in generated code (ISO C, but it stops clang from merging, hoisting and vectorizing those reads in every program, for a guarantee clang already gives on x86-64). Element-wise copies at the element's size only (word-wide copies are as safe and twice as fast for small elements). A lock per generator, or waiting for a running generator (a body that runs `.par` work which resumes the same generator would deadlock). Moving an async generator to the loop that resumes it (two loops' requests would still race). A lock per promise (D-121's reasons).
- **Known gaps:** generated code's plain accesses rest on clang's code generation, not on ISO C, and an ARM port needs release stores (D-130); threads racing on a sync generator or a runtime iterator may skip or repeat items, and on a generator get `TypeError` from the losing resume.

### D-144 · M6.0b split by the review's findings; thenables' then read once, its failures rejecting (M6.0b1)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Split.** M6.0b becomes M6.0b1 (finding 5: thenables), M6.0b2 (finding 6: values read before a suspension, and the exhaustive effect classification), M6.0b3 (finding 7: the iterator protocol's order), M6.0b4 (finding 8 and ruling 6: erased generic factories, type arguments in conversion messages) and M6.0b5 (the corpus skips classified, the corpus report in four categories). Scope unchanged.
  - **Thenables.** ResolvePromise reads a thenable's `then` once, inside a handler: a getter that throws rejects the promise with what it threw, where it used to throw out of `Promise.resolve` (and leave `new Promise(resolve => resolve(x))` pending, its resolving functions already used). NewPromiseResolveThenableJob calls the function that was read, not `then` looked up again by name, so a getter runs once and its result is what's called (the review's probe printed `changing 2 2`; Node `changing 1 1`). A method read through `any` is bound to its object (D-075), so the call needs no receiver.
  - **Object literals' methods through `any`** (found on the way): reading one gave its raw closure, whose first parameter is the object (D-044), so calling it plainly shifted the arguments (`TypeError: Expected object1, got function`); it is now bound, as a class's method read through `any` is.
- **Reason:** ECMAScript's algorithms (Get(resolution, "then") as an abrupt completion rejects; the job calls the captured thenJobCallback). A handler around the read costs a `_setjmp` only when an object of the program's resolves a promise.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Calling `then` through the object's method table when it has a method of that name (two paths for one algorithm, and the getter case still needs the captured value).
- **Known gaps:** a method read through `any` reports `length` 0 (the bound value's descriptor has no parameter count).

### D-145 · Operands read before a suspension keep their values; the effect classification is exhaustive (M6.0b2)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **What a statement's hoisting keeps** (D-111). An operand JS evaluates before a yield or an await in the same statement is kept in a frame local unless nothing can change it while the function is suspended: literals, function expressions, `this`, constants, functions, classes, enums and imports, a variable of the function itself that no other function assigns (D-140's pre-pass), a variable of an enclosing scope that nothing assigns after its declaration (the pre-pass now records those too), and `!`, `typeof`, `void` and binary operators of such operands. Property and element reads are always kept (a getter, or code that runs meanwhile, could change them; the review's probe 6 returned 12 and ran the getter after the resume, Node 11). Unary `+`, `-` and `~` and template substitutions count only for primitive operands, and a template substitution before a later yield is kept as its string, since JS converts it (calling an object's `toString`) when it reaches it. A name called as a function is read before the arguments.
  - **Compound assignments** (`x += yield v`, `o.n *= await p`, `a[i] -= …`) whose right side suspends read their target's value before it, for a name, a field or an element; the assignment's lowering uses that value (`keptCurrent`).
  - **Effects** (`pts/cgen/order.go`). `effectsOf` names every IR expression; an unknown one is taken to read, write and throw. Builtins are classified by what they are: String's, Number's and Math's members compute from their arguments (some throw), a short list of value tests too, `Math.random` and `performance.now` depend on the calls before them, console methods write, and every other builtin may run the program's code (a getter, `toString`, a callback) or change state; `ToString` of a dynamic value may run `toString` (D-142). Before, any builtin not listed as throwing counted as pure, so `f(o.x, g())` through `any` could run the getter after `g`.
- **Reason:** JS evaluates operands left to right, each when it reaches it; "has no side effects" isn't "can't change while suspended", since other code runs during the suspension. Keeping only what can change costs a frame local per such operand; the benchmarks' C is the same except ordering temporaries around dynamic calls, which compile to the same code.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Keeping every operand before a yield (a local per name for operands that can't change). Re-reading a getter target of a compound assignment after keeping its value (the getter would run twice).
- **Known gaps:** a compound assignment's target read through a getter or `any` is read after the yield; a method found on an object (`o.m(yield v)`) is looked up after it.

### D-146 · Iterator steps read done before value; closing an iterator after a throw keeps the throw (M6.0b3)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **IteratorStep and IteratorValue.** The runtime's wrapper of an object's iterator (D-111) reads a result's `done` first, and its `value` only if it isn't done; a done result is kept, and its `value` is read when something asks for it (`pts_iterator_value`, which `yield*` calls for its result; a `for…of` never does). The review's probe 13 read the value of a done result, whose getter threw. `for await` already read `done` first.
  - **IteratorClose.** A loop over an iterator (`for…of`, `for await`) closes it in the finally its lowering wraps the loop in (D-109); that finally now sees how the loop completed (`withFinally` passes its context): after a throw, an error from `return()` (or a non-object result) is dropped and the loop's error goes on; after a `break`, a `return` or another jump, the error from closing is the loop's, as in JS. Before, closing's error replaced the body's (probe 13 caught `close`, Node `body`).
- **Reason:** ECMAScript's IteratorStep/IteratorValue and IteratorClose (completion's precedence), and the review's finding 7.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Reading every result's value eagerly and dropping a throw from it when the result is done (a getter would still run, which is observable).
- **Known gaps:** an object literal with accessors returned as an `IteratorResult` is PTS1001 (`converting … to object1 | object2`: a literal with getters can't become the union's shapes); with `any` results, as the probe has, it works. Array destructuring's close (`IteratorTake`, in the runtime) and `yield*`'s closing of its inner iterator don't distinguish completions yet.

### D-147 · M6.0b4 split; conversion errors name generic class instances with their type arguments (M6.0b4a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Split.** M6.0b4 becomes M6.0b4a (type arguments in conversion messages) and M6.0b4b (erased generic factories whose instances convert to the specialized type: ruling 6's dispatch to monomorphized instances, or specialized construction where the call site knows the type arguments). The second is a design change of its own (erased copies would need their type arguments at run time, or a way to find the specialized constructor), the first a small one that makes the second's failures readable meanwhile.
  - **Messages.** A generic class instance's IR class carries its name with its type arguments as tsc writes them (`ir.Class.Display`, `Box<number>`); its descriptor has it as `display_name`, which `pts_value_description` prefers, and class checks and checked conversions name the expected type with it. The probe's `TypeError: Expected Box, got Box` reads `Expected Box<number>, got Box<any>`. `console.log` and `constructor.name` keep the class name alone, as Node shows it.
- **Reason:** The review asked for type arguments in mismatch messages "immediately"; a message that names the same type on both sides tells the programmer nothing.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Naming descriptors `Box<number>` (console.log and `constructor.name` would differ from Node).
- **Known gaps:** unions and interfaces that mention generic instances still print their members' plain names.

### D-148 · Typed calls of erased copies find the monomorphized instance of their signature in the value's or receiver's descriptor (M6.0b4b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** Ruling 6 of `docs/agent/OWNER_ANSWERS.md` (keep erasure as a fallback, but a value an erased copy constructs must convert back to the specialized type): *dispatch to the monomorphized instance*, by signature, at run time.
  - **Calls that look up an instance.** A call of a value of a generic function type, and a call of a dispatched generic method (D-101), whose resolved type arguments don't mention erased type parameters and whose callee's declared result may hold objects of a type that mentions its type parameters (`Box<T>`, `[Box<A>, Box<B>]`, `Box<T>[]`, not `T` or `T[]`: `holdsGenericObjects`), evaluates the callee or receiver and the arguments (typed as the resolved signature's parameters) once, then asks the runtime for the instance of the call's signature (`pts_instance_find`, by the signature's `pts_check`, and for a method by name). It calls that with the typed arguments; with none, it calls the erased copy as before (arguments boxed, result converted with a check). No rest parameters; the result's type must be the one `fromErased` converts to.
  - **Where instances are listed.** A closure's descriptor lists its function's instances (`pts_type.instances`, name NULL); a class's descriptor lists the instances of the generic methods it declares, and the search walks the class's ancestors. A method that implements the dispatched one but gets no instance of the signature has an entry without a function, which ends the search, so a receiver never finds an ancestor's instance in place of its own class's method.
  - **Which instances.** Erased values are erased copies of generic function declarations and static generic methods used as values, and the closures of module-scope generic consts (D-102), whose function makes generic class instances (`makesGenericInstances`). Once the program's bodies are lowered, `specialize` pairs every demand with every erased value of the demand's erased function type (making the instance for the type arguments tsc infers relating the generic function to the call's resolved signature, `PtsInstantiateSignatureInContextOf`), and with every class's generic method of the dispatched method's name in its class or a subclass (the method's instance for the call's type arguments, by position); the instances' bodies may add demands, values and classes, so it repeats until nothing is new. An instance is listed only if it can be called as the call's signature: the same parameters or the first of them, and the same result or a subclass's instance (an entry function adapts each to the exact C signature).
  - **Instances that wouldn't lower.** An instance whose body, or the body of a generic function it calls by name, narrows a variable to `never` for its type arguments (`typeof x === "number"` for a string `x`) isn't made for a demand (`narrowsAway`), since instances don't lower such code (D-102's known gap); the call keeps the erased copy, so no program that compiled before fails to compile now.
  - **Also:** a generic method may override a generic method in a generic class instance made after the name's erased copies joined the method tables (it was PTS1001, "overriding a method with a generic method": the base's erased copy in its table is a plain method). D-102's test of a generic const's result recurses into arrays (`Box<T>[]`).
- **Reason:** Probe 3 printed `Expected Box<number>, got Box<any>` and `oop/result_option.pts` threw where Node printed the values; both pass now, and the calls that find instances run monomorphized code with unboxed arguments. Whole-program compilation knows every call's type arguments and every value and class that could receive them, so the instances can be made ahead of time; looking them up by signature at the call keeps one representation per function value and per method table (D-078, D-079), which every other path (adapters, `any`, interfaces) relies on.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Copying a `Box<any>` into a `Box<number>` at the conversion (loses identity, which ruling 6 asks to keep; D-078 and D-102 rejected it). Hidden type-argument descriptors passed to erased copies, so that `new Box(x)` picks the class at run time (reified generics: a second calling convention for every generic function value, run-time type construction for `Box<T[]>`, and a dynamic constructor per class instance). A dispatcher keyed by the call site's generic function type (a value can flow between structurally equal types; the signature is what the call can use). Instances for every value and method regardless of `makesGenericInstances` (their bodies may not lower, D-102, for no gain: erased copies that make no generic instances return what typed code gave them).
- **Known gaps:** generic arrow functions and function expressions other than module-scope consts, values from `any`, and calls inside erased code (whose type arguments are dynamic) still take the erased copy, so `new Box(x)` there still makes `Box<any>`. Interface calls of generic methods (D-079) keep their adapters (an instance's class result isn't the interface's result type). An instance that `narrowsAway` keeps the erased copy and its run-time TypeError.

### D-149 · Corpus skips are classified as by design, unsupported or bug; the cheap bugs among them fixed; narrowed downcasts are checked (M6.0b5)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Classification.** A skipped corpus program's `// skip:` cause starts with its category: `by design: D-nnn: …` (a documented difference from Node, citing its decision), `unsupported: PTS1001: …` (a feature not built yet, rejected with a clear message) or `bug: …` (anything else: wrong output, a crash, or PTS1001 on a construct the spec says works, such as an internal conversion failing on ordinary code). `TestCorpusSkipsAreClassified` requires a category and logs the counts; `pts/tests/corpus/report.sh` prints the corpus report in four groups: Node-identical, intentional differences, unsupported, failing.
  - **The 46 skips.** Each PTS1001 was traced to its construct (subagents, each fix reviewed and retested here): 8 are by design (D-038, D-059, D-068, D-075, D-L09), 1 was stale (`errors/error_aggregation.pts`, fixed by D-113), 13 were bugs that cheap fixes remove, and 24 are unsupported (one, `functional/memo_selectors.pts`, also hit the overload bug). The report reads 267 Node-identical, 8 intentional differences, 24 unsupported and 1 failing: `algorithms/lru_cache_map.pts`, which the checked downcasts below turn from a lucky pass into a TypeError (`m instanceof MapLRU` on a `Cache<string, number>` narrows, in tsc's types, to `MapLRU<any, any>`, which the object, a `MapLRU<string, number>`, isn't; the unchecked cast read it as the other instance, which for a `number` type argument reads a double as a dynamic value).
  - **Narrowed downcasts are checked.** A class value (or an interface value) that the checker narrowed to a subclass is converted with a check (`downcast`: `TypeError: Expected Wide, got Base`), as a union narrowed to a class already was (D-067): a type guard that lies (`b is Wide` for a `Base`) made an unchecked C cast, and reading the subclass's fields read past the object (segfault, found by two subagents independently). `instanceof` narrowing pays the same descriptor test.
  - **Bugs fixed** (each with a run test): a spread property that a later property replaces may have another type (`{ ...page, items: n }`); an array spreads into an array literal or call of another element type as a checked copy (`["a", ...numbers]`, D-094), and `concat` copies an argument array of other elements likewise; a value narrowed by `instanceof` goes to a union of classes as its subclass (`e instanceof A ? e : new B()`); optional chains reach members of generic class instances (`this.head?.value`); a call of an overloaded generic function infers the implementation's type arguments from the overload (other type parameters, or another order, picked a wrong instance or failed); `Array<T>(n)` in erased code makes an `any[]` as `new Array<T>(n)` does, and methods of arrays erased code makes are found at run time; a dynamic callback (erased, or `any`) of an array method is called by the method found at run time; object patterns take `length` of arrays and strings and `size` of maps and sets; a string literal union or a string enum indexes as a string; a template or `String()` of an object literal calls a `toString` that is a function-valued property (D-142's runtime looked for methods only); `filter` with a guard that narrows a class to a subclass makes the subclass's array (checked); a conditional's branches where a `number | string` goes keep their own types; an arrow without a return type takes its context's result where that is its own result made nullable (`reduce<number | null>`); an enum read with a string key that names no member statically (`Opcode[name as keyof typeof Opcode]`) reads the enum's object (D-089); a class's method may provide an interface's method with fewer parameters, called through the method's adapter (descriptors then carry declared types).
- **Reason:** The review asked for the corpus's divergences to be told apart, so that a skip says whether the language means it, hasn't built it, or is wrong; tracing each one found that a third of the "not supported yet" skips were bugs, most of them a few lines each. Tie-breaker 1 makes the unchecked downcast the first fix.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Leaving `instanceof` narrowing unchecked (the checker's narrowing can't be told apart from a type guard's at the reference; the test is a descriptor compare). Copying objects between field representations for `parser_combinators.pts` and `super_chain.pts` (a checked copy of objects that may be shared is a policy change beyond D-094's arrays; left unsupported).
- **Known gaps:** `instanceof` narrowing to a generic class from an interface or another class, where tsc's narrowed type is the instance for `any` (a checked TypeError now, where Node goes on; the instance the object is would have to replace tsc's type at every use in the branch). The 24 unsupported programs' features (the report lists them: functions with properties, tuples at computed indices, recursive tuple and function types, `Function` and constructor values, optional methods, objects of another representation, …). A spread doesn't call an overridden getter of its source, and an absent optional property written later takes its first place. A Map's or Set's `forEach` with a dynamic callback is still PTS1001. A program with an interface method provided with fewer parameters carries declared types in every descriptor.

### D-150 · M6.0c split; a function is a valid operand of `parallel` (M6.0c1, ruling 1)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:**
  - **Split.** M6.0c becomes M6.0c1 (rulings 1, 2 and 4–8 of `docs/agent/OWNER_ANSWERS.md`: small changes and documentation, D-150 … D-156), M6.0c2 (ruling 3: enforce `Object.freeze`, a runtime and C emitter change with a cost budget) and M6.0c3 (ruling 9: check asserted union literals where it's cheap, D-104). Every ruling keeps its own D-entry citing that file.
  - **Ruling 1** (review finding 9, D-120): `parallel` followed by any expression is the operator, a function or class expression too (`await parallel function () { return 42; }` gives the function, which its task made), typed `Promise<Awaited<T>>` as before. The PTS1002 for a function operand is gone; the guardrail (D-L17) stays the only parallel-specific check of an operand. `parallel e` where a binding named `parallel` is in scope stays PTS1002: tsc types that call as the binding's, so it isn't the operator.
- **Reason:** The owner's ruling: returning a function is legitimate task evaluation (brief §2.6), and the language shouldn't add restrictions beyond the guardrail.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A warning in place of the error (the PTS checks have no warnings, and the ruling asks for no restriction).

### D-151 · `.par.reduce` equals `reduce` for any initial value (M6.0c1, ruling 2)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** Ruling 2 of `docs/agent/OWNER_ANSWERS.md` (review finding 9, probe 9): each piece of `xs.par.reduce(f, initial)` folds its own elements from its first, the pieces' results are combined with `f`, and `initial` goes in front once (`f(initial, total)`); an empty input gives `initial`. The pool never makes an empty piece of a non-empty range (it splits only when both halves get an element). Arrays, typed arrays and ranges alike (`pts/lib/parallel.pts`). `f` must be associative, not commutative, and no identity is needed; the docs say so.
- **Reason:** Simple and correct beats a documented trap (tie-breaker); probe 9's 7998100 for `reduce`'s 7998010 came from every piece starting at the initial value.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Requiring an identity (the trap the review found). Folding `initial` into the first piece only (the first piece isn't known before the split, and the result would depend on where it starts).

### D-152 · Reads past the end keep their design; their RangeError says how to read a value that may be missing (M6.0c1, ruling 4)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** Ruling 4 of `docs/agent/OWNER_ANSWERS.md`: an out-of-range read (D-015, D-038, D-089, D-090) still gives undefined wherever the context can hold it and throws `RangeError` where a non-optional type is required (SPEC §8). The message now ends with what to do: `Array index 5 is out of range (length 3); if it may be missing, read it with \`?? fallback\`, give the element type \`| undefined\`, or check the length first` (arrays, typed arrays, strings), and for hash-map objects (D-059) `…; give the value type \`| undefined\`, or check \`key in object\` first` (`PTS_MISSING_ELEMENT_HINT`, `PTS_MISSING_KEY_HINT` in `pts.h`).
- **Reason:** The ruling keeps the design and asks for a better message; a message that names the fixes turns a surprise into a one-line change.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Typing such reads `T | undefined` everywhere (the ruling rejects it).

### D-153 · Generator and async state machines stay in the C emitter until M8 (M6.0c1, ruling 5)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** Ruling 5 of `docs/agent/OWNER_ANSWERS.md` (D-109): the frame and state transform of generators and async functions stays in `pts/cgen/generators.go`, a known divergence from brief §2.3 (which wants it done on the IR before C emission), recorded in SPEC §9's notes; M8 moves it into an IR pass, when escape analysis needs one. The review's finding 6 was fixed in place (D-145).
- **Reason:** It works and is tested; the IR pass pays off with M8's analyses.

### D-154 · Generic erasure stays as the fallback, and what it breaks is fixed (M6.0c1, ruling 6)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** Ruling 6 of `docs/agent/OWNER_ANSWERS.md` (D-051, D-078, D-079, D-101): erasure stays for function values passed around generically, and a value an erased copy makes converts back to the specialized type because typed calls dispatch to the monomorphized instance of their signature (D-148, M6.0b4b); `Expected Box, got Box` is gone (D-147 names both sides). What remains (generic arrow functions other than module-scope consts, values from `any`, calls inside erased code) is listed in `PROGRESS.md`.

### D-155 · Checked copies from `any` and adapter identity stay; SPEC §8 lists identity and aliasing in one table (M6.0c1, ruling 7)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** Ruling 7 of `docs/agent/OWNER_ANSWERS.md`: the checked copies of D-071 and D-072 (dynamic data converted to typed arrays and object types), the copies of D-089 and D-094 (`as` to another array or object type, spreads and `concat` across element types), adapter identity (D-075) and the other places where `===` or aliasing differ from JS (bound methods, D-044; static closures, D-041; enum objects, D-089) are kept as documented differences, now gathered in one table, SPEC §8 "Identity and aliasing".
- **Reason:** They are the price of an honest `any` (brief §2.4); one table lets a reader see every place where an object or function isn't the one JS would give.

### D-156 · Interface merging stays (M6.0c1, ruling 8)
- **Date:** 2026-10-12
- **Decision:** Ruling 8 of `docs/agent/OWNER_ANSWERS.md` (D-020): interfaces merging with interfaces stays allowed; it changes nothing at run time (the merged interface is one object type to tsc and to the lowering) and is everywhere in real TypeScript.

### D-157 · `Object.freeze` is enforced: a frozen object points to its descriptor's frozen twin (M6.0c2, ruling 3)
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** Ruling 3 of `docs/agent/OWNER_ANSWERS.md` (review, D-105): a write to a frozen object's property, element or hash-map key throws Node's strict-mode `TypeError`, through any reference and through `any`. This replaces D-105's "writes aren't stopped"; its table stays for the kinds below that only get marked.
  - **Frozen twins.** `Object.freeze` points an object at its descriptor's frozen twin: a copy (the whole descriptor, a class's method table included: `descriptor_size`) with `frozen` set and `parent` the original, made once per descriptor and never freed. `instanceof`, field and method tables and virtual calls work unchanged; code that compares descriptors (a RegExp, a match array, a copy's descriptor, conversions from `any`, a spread's order) sees through the twin with `pts_unfrozen`. Objects (those with fields), tuples, arrays, hash-map objects and RegExps get twins. The kinds whose contents JS leaves writable (Map, Set, Date, functions, generators, promises, timers) and objects without fields are only marked in D-105's table; a typed array with elements throws `TypeError: Cannot freeze array buffer views with elements`. `frozen` and `descriptor_size` sit in padding holes, so descriptors keep their size.
  - **Where writes test it.** No store site's inline cache ever holds a twin: the slow paths of stores through interfaces (`pts_field_store_address`, `pts_field_set_slow`, `pts_property_find_slow` for setters, `pts_optional_set_slow`) throw for a twin before caching. So a store that hits its cache, the common case, tests nothing. Direct stores to class and shape fields test `type->frozen` (`PTS_WRITABLE`) only in a program that calls `Object.freeze` (the emitter sees `ir.ValueFreeze`), after evaluating the value as JS does; `#private` fields and setters stay writable, as in JS. The runtime's mutators test it: every array method throws at the first write JS's algorithm makes (`push` → `Cannot add property 3, object is not extensible`, `pop` → `Cannot delete property '2' of [object Array]`, `shift` → `'0'`, `sort` after the comparator's calls, `length`, …); hash-map objects' `pts_record_set` and `pts_record_delete` (a Map's or Set's `pts_map_set` tests nothing); writes through `any` and `Object.assign`; and every write of a RegExp's `lastIndex` (`exec`, `test`, `replace`, `match` and `search` with `g` or `y`), which `search` now makes only when JS does. Messages are V8's, naming the object `#<Point>`, `#<Object>`, `[object Array]`, `[object RegExp]` or an error's string.
  - **`Readonly<X>`** of a class's instance type, of `this` or of a library type (Object.freeze's result) lowers as X (`instantiate`), its members as X's, so `Object.freeze(this)` in a constructor, `Readonly<Point>` parameters and frozen RegExps, Maps and Dates compile; before, a class with methods was PTS1001 (`using a Point as a {…}`).
  - **Cost.** No benchmark calls `Object.freeze`, so their generated code is unchanged apart from function names, and they execute the same instructions (callgrind, before and after, `PTS_THREADS=1`: `word_count` 9,797,201,685 and 9,797,217,068; `binary_trees` 13,855,082,515 and 13,854,923,805; `sum_reduce` 28,249,675,328 and 28,249,676,852). Timings of the ten sequential and parallel benchmarks (`PTS_THREADS=1`, 3 rounds of 5 runs, old and new alternating) moved by −3% to +12% between runs of the same two binaries on this busy host; code placement alone does that much (two control builds that only add unused code to the runtime: +1.5% and +3.7% on `word_count`'s best time). Tables in `docs/BENCHMARKS.md` are unchanged.
- **Reason:** The owner's ruling: writes must never silently succeed, at no measurable cost. Twins make the test one load of a flag where a test is needed, and nothing where a store hits a cache that can't hold a twin.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A test of D-105's table on every write (a locked hash lookup per store). A header bit (a word more per object). A frozen bit in the descriptor pointer's low bits (every read of an object's descriptor would mask it, and bdwgc's gcj marker reads the pointer as it is). Testing in every program (a load and a branch per field store in programs that never freeze).
- **Known gaps:** A frozen error with an empty message says `Cannot assign to read only property 'message'`, where V8, which gives such an error no own `message`, says it can't add one. A frozen timer, generator or promise is only marked (Node's timers would throw from their internals). A JSON reviver's writes into a holder it froze aren't checked (JS's CreateDataProperty fails silently there).

### D-158 · A member an asserted object literal lacks throws a TypeError (M6.0c3, ruling 9)
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** Ruling 9 of `docs/agent/OWNER_ANSWERS.md` (D-104 (5)): an object literal asserted to a union of object types that it fits no member of stays the object it is, as in JS, and is now checked where it is used. A read of a property or a call of a method that it lacks, through the union or after narrowing, throws a catchable `TypeError: Cannot read property 'n' of #<Object>: it has none, though a type assertion says it has` (or `Cannot call method 'm' …`); a write of one throws `Cannot add property n, object is not extensible`, as other writes that would add a property do (D-086). Before, the lookup's slow path ended the program (`pts_panic`, uncatchable). A property held in another representation (`{ type: "rename", name: 5 } as unknown as Action`) was already a TypeError, because every read through a union of object types passes the declared type (D-072). The checks are on the slow paths only (a site never caches a member the object lacks); generated code is unchanged.
- **Reason:** The ruling asks for the check if it's cheap; it is free where the object has the member. JS reads undefined there, which the member's type (a number, a string) can't hold, so a TypeError that names the assertion is the honest result; tie-breaker 3 prefers a run-time throw to a compile error for a program tsc accepts and Node runs.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Rejecting the assertion at compile time when the literal's discriminant selects a member it doesn't fit (it blocks a program Node runs; tie-breaker 3). A checked conversion at the assertion (it would throw for `{ type: "@@init" } as Action`, the Redux idiom D-104 keeps working). Reading undefined where the context can hold it, as D-015 does for indices (each site would need to know its context; the throw is rarer and clearer).

### D-159 · M6.3 split: the client first, then the review's acceptance items
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** M6.3 becomes M6.3a (the `fetch` client of D-O10 with global `fetch`, `Request`, `Response` and `Headers`, tested against a `pts:http` server and a Go server) and M6.3b (the review's acceptance items for the client and the server: cancellation, disconnects, backpressure, cleanup, cross-loop use, pending requests and rejected promises). No scope is dropped.
- **Reason:** Each half is a session's work, and the acceptance items need the client to exist.

### D-160 · fetch: one connection per request, the body read whole, Node's errors (M6.3a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** The global `fetch` (D-O10) is pts:http's, written in ParallelScript over one C intrinsic (`pts_http_fetch`, `fetch.c`); `fetch`, `Request`, `Response` and `Headers` are globals through `GlobalModules`, as `process` is. Each request is one connection on the calling loop: `getaddrinfo`, each address tried in order (so `localhost` reaches a server on 127.0.0.1 when ::1 refuses), the request written with `connection: close`, and the response read whole: framed by Content-Length, chunks or the connection's end, 1xx responses skipped, decoded as UTF-8, up to 64 MB. The library parses the URL (http and https schemes with an authority; the path and query percent-encoded as the URL standard does), drops the request headers the client writes itself (`host`, `connection`, `content-length`, `transfer-encoding`, …), adds `accept: */*`, `user-agent: ParallelScript` and the string body's content type, and follows redirects as the Fetch standard does (20 at most; 303, and 301 and 302 of a POST, become a GET without a body; `redirect: "manual"` returns them, `"error"` fails). Failures are Node's: `TypeError: fetch failed` with a `cause` (Node's system error for `connect` and `getaddrinfo`, `ENOTFOUND` for a name that doesn't resolve; an Error for a protocol failure, `other side closed`, `bad port` (the Fetch standard's list), `unknown scheme`), and `Failed to parse URL from …` for a URL it can't parse. `https://` rejects with `fetch failed` and a cause saying TLS isn't supported yet.
- **Reason:** One connection per request and whole bodies are simple and enough for M6's example apps; the body type the library has is a string (D-136), which text() and json() read. Node's error shapes let code written against Node's fetch handle failures unchanged.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Keep-alive pooling (state shared by requests on a loop, for a gain the examples don't need yet). Streamed bodies (`ReadableStream` isn't built). Parsing URLs in C (the library's string functions do it in a few lines, and a `URL` class is its natural home later).
- **Known gaps:** no `AbortSignal`, no `fetch` from a pool task, no streamed bodies or `arrayBuffer()` (M6.3b and later); the system error has no `address` or `port` property; bodies are decoded as UTF-8 whatever their charset; relative redirect locations don't resolve `.` and `..` segments; `new Request(request)` doesn't take over the source's body as Node's does.

### D-161 · M6.3b split: the client's acceptance items, then the server's
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** M6.3b becomes M6.3b1 (the client: `AbortController` and its globals, aborting fetch at every stage, fetch from other loops and pool threads, failures mid-response, large bodies, cleanup under many requests, pending and unhandled requests) and M6.3b2 (the server: disconnects while a request is handled, slow readers, `stop()` with requests in flight, its handles used from other threads). No scope is dropped.
- **Reason:** The client's half needs the abort classes, which the server's `request.signal` then reuses; each half is a session's work with its tests.

### D-162 · AbortController and its globals in ParallelScript; fetch requests have ids any thread may abort (M6.3b1)
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** `AbortController`, `AbortSignal`, `DOMException`, `Event` and `EventTarget` are globals (`GlobalModules`) from a library module, `pts:internal/abort`, written in ParallelScript as the DOM standard describes them: listeners by type, each added once, called in the order added (`once`, `signal` and `stopImmediatePropagation`; a listener's throw is reported as uncaught after the others run, as Node does); `onabort` added as a listener the first time it is set; `AbortSignal.abort`, `timeout` (its timer unref'd, as Node's) and `any`; `new AbortSignal()` is Node's `TypeError: Illegal constructor`; a signal's `aborted` is a readonly field, so `console.log` shows `AbortSignal { aborted: false }` as Node does; DOMException's `code` comes from WebIDL's table of names. A `Request` has its own signal that follows the one given (`AbortSignal.any([signal])`, so `request.signal !== init.signal`, as in Node), or a never-aborting one made when first read. fetch checks the signal before it starts, after each response, and listens for its abort while a request is in flight: `pts_http_fetch` now returns a request id and calls a done callback instead of returning a promise, and `pts_http_fetch_abort(id)` from any thread ends the request on its loop (a CALL message from elsewhere), closing its connection; fetch then rejects with the signal's reason. Ids are slot and generation in one table under a mutex, the only state threads share; the client stays the loop's: while the resolver or a closing connection still holds an ended client, that callback frees it. A fetch on a pool thread (a `.par` callback) starts its request on the operation's loop by a CALL message, where its callback runs. Microtasks run after the libuv callbacks that end requests, never inside an abort the program calls (they did, inside `finish`, which ran promise reactions in the middle of synchronous code). A response declaring a Content-Length over 64 MB fails with `Response body is larger than 64 MB` (it was a protocol error), unless it has no body (HEAD, 204, 304).
- **Reason:** Programs written against Node's fetch pass `signal`s and expect `AbortError`s; the classes are plain library code with no runtime support needed, and Node's behaviour is easy to compare (`run/abort_signals.pts` matches it). Ids keep a freed client out of reach of an abort from another thread without reference counts; the loop that owns libuv's handles is the only one that touches them, as for timers (D-139).
- **Alternatives rejected:** The abort in C, with a GC-allocated control object shared by the signal and the client (the client would need a collected pointer, and the signal's listeners are what Node's undici uses anyway). Reference-counted clients (every callback would need to count). Getters for all of Event's attributes, as the DOM declares them (console.log would then show `Event {}`, no closer to Node's custom `Event { type: … }` than fields are).
- **Known gaps:** listeners are functions only (no `handleEvent` objects); the `capture` option is ignored (no tree to capture in); a dispatched event's `this` isn't the target; `DOMException` takes a name, not an options object, and has no static code constants; Node's DOMException prints as `DOMException [AbortError]: …`, ours as `AbortError: …`; an abort right after `fetch()` returns can't overtake a failure fetch finds synchronously (a bad port, an unknown scheme), where Node's abort wins; a chunked body is decoded again on every read (a 60 MB body takes about twice Node's time).

### D-163 · The server reads on while a request is handled; a client that leaves closes its connection and aborts `request.signal`; slow readers stop taking requests (M6.3b2)
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** A connection keeps reading while the program has its request (it stopped before), buffering at most `MAX_HEAD` bytes of what follows, so the end of the client's side or a read error is seen at once: the connection closes then, freeing its descriptor whatever the handler does, and the request's `signal` aborts (an AbortError DOMException), as Bun's `request.signal` does; a late answer finds no connection and is dropped. A client that half-closes its side and waits for the answer is taken as gone, as nginx takes it. The request callback returns the `Request` (as `unknown`, so the C side calls it through its exact type), which an uncollectable cell per connection keeps while the program has the request; a second callback given to `pts_http_listen` gets it back on a disconnect, and the library marks it gone and aborts its signal (made aborted if it is first read afterwards). Once more than `MAX_QUEUED` (1 MB) of responses wait in libuv's write queue, the connection reads and processes nothing more until the queue falls below half of it, so a client that pipelines requests and doesn't read costs about a megabyte; the body is copied once into the write (it was copied twice). `stop(closeActiveConnections = false)` follows Bun's: with true, connections whose requests are with the program close at once and their signals abort; those writing their last response finish it. `stop()` works from any thread (from a pool thread it posts to every listener's loop); `serve()` from a pool task throws, as timers do there.
- **Reason:** The review asked for the server's behaviour under disconnects, slow readers and `stop()` with requests in flight. Reading on is the only way to see a client leave before writing to it; aborting the request's signal lets long handlers stop work, with the classes D-162 brought; the bound on queued responses keeps one client from growing the server without limit (64 unread 1 MB responses grew it by 62 MB, now under 24 MB in the test, usually a few MB). Hello world keeps its throughput (about 60,000 requests a second on one loop, the same as before within this machine's noise).
- **Alternatives rejected:** Detecting disconnects only when writing (the descriptor and the handler's work live until the answer, which may never come). A per-server map from connection ids to requests in the library (loops with `reusePort` would share it without a lock). Zero-copy bodies (the write would need to keep the string alive in uncollectable memory per response; one copy is enough until bodies stream). A `Promise<void>` from `stop()` as Bun's (no caller needs it yet; awaiting the void result works).
- **Known gaps:** a client's half-close ends its request (HTTP/1.1 clients don't half-close to wait for an answer, but some tools do); the server notices a client that leaves while its responses are queued only when writing fails; `serve` from a pool task throws.

### D-164 · M6.4 split: bytes and the hasher, the PNG renderer, then the server and the log analyzer
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** M6.4 becomes M6.4a (bytes in `pts:fs`, which the hasher and the PNG writer need, and the parallel file hasher), M6.4b (the mandelbrot renderer with its stored-deflate PNG writer) and M6.4c (the JSON API server on every core, the parallel log analyzer, `examples/README.md` and the CI smoke runs). No example is dropped.
- **Reason:** The examples need a missing piece first (binary files), and each is a program with its own test; three parts are each a session's work.

### D-165 · pts:fs reads bytes as a Uint8Array for Node's Buffer, with a Buffer's text a compile error (M6.4a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** `readFileSync(path)` and `readFile(path)` without an encoding (`undefined`, `null`, `{ encoding: null }`, or options with only a flag) give the file's bytes; `writeFileSync`, `appendFileSync`, `writeFile` and `appendFile` take a string or a `Uint8Array` (a view writes only its bytes, copied as D-143 copies shared typed arrays). pts:fs's `Buffer` type is `{ toString(encoding?): string; slice(start?, end?): Buffer } & Uint8Array`: an intersection that `Uint8Array` covers, so its values are `Uint8Array`s (D-086; typed arrays' members are found through it), whose `toString` and `slice` are declared with `@notSupportedYet`. tsc resolves `buffer.toString()` and `buffer.slice()` to those declarations, and any conversion of a Buffer-typed value to a string (template substitutions, `String(b)`, `+`) checks its type's `toString` for the tag, so a Buffer's text is PTS1001 with a message saying to read the file with `"utf8"`, rather than the `"104,101,…"` a Uint8Array's toString gives where Node gives the text. The runtime reads into a malloc'd buffer as before and copies it into a new Uint8Array (`pts_fs_read_file_bytes`, `_async`).
- **Reason:** The examples (a file hasher, a PNG writer) and real programs need binary files, and typed arrays have been there since M5.4. A Uint8Array is what most code does with a Buffer (index, length, subarray, pass it to a function taking `Uint8Array`), and Node's Buffer is one, so `buffer instanceof Uint8Array` holds in both. Making the cases where Node's Buffer behaves differently compile errors keeps D-L02: a program prints what Node prints, or doesn't compile.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A Buffer class of our own extending Uint8Array (classes can't extend typed arrays yet, and an interface extending one isn't lowerable). `type Buffer = Uint8Array` (a Buffer's text would silently be numbers joined by commas). Keeping unencoded reads a compile error (the hasher couldn't exist).
- **Known gaps:** `console.log` of a Buffer shows `Uint8Array(n) [ … ]` where Node shows `<Buffer 68 65 …>`; `JSON.stringify` of one gives a typed array's `{"0":104,…}` where Node gives `{"type":"Buffer","data":[…]}`; Buffer's own methods (`equals`, `readUInt32BE`, `toString("hex")`, `Buffer.from`, …) don't exist; writes take a `Uint8Array`, not another typed array or a DataView.

### D-166 · The API server and the log analyzer; regexes skip PCRE2's UTF check on well-formed strings, and split is linear (M6.4c)
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:**
  - **Examples.** `examples/api-server`: a JSON API on every loop (`serve({ reusePort: true })`) whose loops share one `Mutex<Map<number, Item>>` and `AtomicInt` counters; routes for items (list, add, read, delete), a prime count by `range(…).par.count`, a wait that `request.signal` cuts short, stats and shutdown; handlers throw an `HttpError` and `serve`'s `error` option turns it into a JSON error with its status (anything else is a 500). `examples/log-analyzer`: files read by `.par.map`, cut into pieces of about 1M characters at line ends, each piece split into lines and parsed by one RegExp per line into a `Summary` of Maps and a Set, `.par.map` over the pieces, the summaries added up and printed as a report (status shares, the ten busiest paths, requests and bytes per hour). `examples/README.md` lists the four examples with their numbers. Their Go tests drive them as a user would (`TestExampleAPIServer` over HTTP; `TestExampleLogAnalyzer` against the counts of a Go log generator), under 1 and 4 threads; `make test` (CI) runs them.
  - **Strings known to be well-formed.** A string has a third flag, `PTS_STRING_WELL_FORMED`: no lone surrogate, so its WTF-8 bytes are valid UTF-8 (ASCII strings may carry only `PTS_STRING_ASCII`; `pts_string_known_well_formed` asks either). `pts_string_measure` sets it in the same pass, concatenation when both sides are known, `repeat` from its string, `pts_string_from_utf8_lossy` always (its output has no surrogates), cgen for literals that are valid UTF-8. Absent means unknown, so constructors that don't set it are still correct.
  - **Regexes.** Each RegExp is compiled twice: as before with `PCRE2_MATCH_INVALID_UTF`, for subjects that may hold lone surrogates, and without it, used with `PCRE2_NO_UTF_CHECK` for subjects known to be well-formed. With `MATCH_INVALID_UTF`, PCRE2 validates the subject from the start offset to its end on every match call, ignoring `NO_UTF_CHECK`, so every loop over a long string's matches (`replace`/`matchAll`/`split` with a global RegExp, `exec` loops) was quadratic: `"ab ".repeat(100000).replace(/b/g, "c")` took 6.7 s (Node: 31 ms), now 17 ms.
  - **split.** `String.prototype.split` by a string that can't match half of a surrogate pair (every separator but one starting with a lone low surrogate or ending with a lone high one) finds its matches with `memmem` over the bytes and copies each piece's bytes: matches start and end on whole characters. On a non-ASCII string it found each match, and each piece's start, by scanning from the beginning: 100,000 pieces of a 200,000-character string took 22 s, now 5 ms.
  - **Smaller things on the way.** A slice of an ASCII string (`bytes_between`, regex captures) is made without measuring its bytes (`pts_string_from_ascii`); `pts_string_from_utf8_lossy` validates and measures in one pass; both scans skip 8 ASCII bytes at a time.
- **Reason:** The examples are M6.4's acceptance: programs a TypeScript developer would write, tested against independent answers. Writing and profiling the log analyzer found the three performance bugs: one line of non-ASCII text in a log made it 60× slower, and any global regex over a large text was quadratic. Each fix is local to the runtime, keeps every result the same and has a regression test that times out with the old code. Two compiled codes per RegExp cost a second `pcre2_compile` per RegExp made; a lazily compiled second code would need an atomic publication for threads sharing a regex, for a cost (a few microseconds per pattern) that only `new RegExp` in a loop would notice.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Validating a subject once per loop and passing a flag down (the subject's own flag serves every caller, `exec` loops in the program included). Caching well-formedness in the string lazily (strings are shared between threads, and their flags are read with plain loads). Splitting the analyzer's text into lines once and chunking the array (its `split` would run on one thread). A larger heap block for bdwgc so threads refill free lists less often: past four threads the analyzer stops gaining because every line allocates about ten objects under bdwgc's allocation lock, which D-131 already leaves to M8.

### D-167 · The benchmark audit: spectral-norm and startup, twins that do equal work, interleaved rounds with their raw times kept (M6.5a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:**
  - **New benchmarks.** `spectral_norm` (the Benchmarks Game's, n = 4000, brief §5): plain TypeScript over Float64Arrays that Node and Bun run as it is, with a Go twin computing the matrix's entries in float64 as JS does; all four print the same unrounded norm. `startup` prints one line, so the table shows what starting and ending a process costs each implementation (every time in the tables includes it).
  - **Equal work.** A subagent audited every twin against its `main.pts` (each finding checked here). Fixed: `sum_reduce/main.ts` fused the sum and the max into one pass (the review's finding) and now makes three passes as `main.pts` and `main.go` do; `mandelbrot/main.ts` counted the inside pixels with `filter(…).length`, building a typed array, and `main.go` fused the sum and the count; `word_count/main.go` and `fan_out/main.go` fused a total into another loop; `file_io/main.go` listed the directory sorted (`os.ReadDir`), where `readdir` doesn't sort, and counted bytes without making strings, where `main.pts` reads text; `word_freq/main.go` made its text with integer arithmetic and the `json` and `string_building` checksums were integer, where JS's are doubles (Go twins compute in float64 where JS does, as in fib and nbody). Kept as differences of the languages, not of the work: Go's `m[k]++` and `m[k] += n` look a key up once where a JS Map takes a `get` and a `set`; Go's substrings share their string's bytes; `encoding/json` decodes into structs or sorts map keys (D-081); Go's `strings.Builder` in string_building (D-061); integer ids and tag names in json's `makeItems`.
  - **Runner.** `run.sh` runs each benchmark in `ROUNDS` (default 2) rounds of `RUNS / ROUNDS` runs, every implementation in turn within a round, so a change in the machine's load (it runs other services) reaches them all; it keeps hyperfine's JSON of every round and a merged `<name>.json` with every run's time, mean, standard deviation, min and max in `RESULTS` (default `pts/bench/results/latest`, ignored by git; a milestone's runs are kept as `pts/bench/results/mN/`), and its tables give mean ± standard deviation and say how many runs and rounds. A benchmark's `label` file is a note after its name: `warm cache` for `file_io` and `word_count`, whose files are in the page cache (written by the same run, or generated once and read on every run), `process start only` for `startup`. `serve.sh` keeps oha's JSON reports there too.
- **Reason:** The review (`docs/reviews/2026-10-12-gpt-6.1-sol/REVIEW.md`, M6.5a) found output parity standing in for equal work, the raw times and their spread thrown away, and warm-cache and startup-inclusive results unlabelled; the brief's §5 lists spectral-norm. Benchmarks are evidence only if each implementation does the same work and the reader can see the noise.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Making Go's map increments take two lookups (a pessimized Go program is no fairer than an idiomatic one; the difference is the language's). Interleaving every single run (hyperfine runs one command's runs together; rounds of a few runs each get most of the benefit with hyperfine's own statistics). Subtracting the startup time from the other rows (it is part of what a user waits for; the startup row shows it instead).
- **Found on the way, for M8:** spectral_norm runs in 2.7 s against Node's 1.9 s; with the module-level `const N` written as a literal in its loops it runs in 2.0 s. A module-scope binding read in a function is checked for readiness and reloaded on every read (D-021), and clang can't hoist either out of a loop whose typed-array reads are relaxed atomics (D-143). A literal const declared before any code that can call a function could be emitted as a C constant.

### D-168 · `pts lsp` is typescript-go's language server with two hooks: the compiler's programs and ParallelScript's diagnostics (M7.1)
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** `pts lsp [--stdio]` runs upstream's language server (`internal/lsp`) in the `pts` binary (`pts/lsp`), over the library's file system (`lib.WrapFS`, serving `ptslib:///`), with automatic type acquisition off. Two package-level hooks, set by `pts/lsp` and nil in upstream's own use, adapt it: `project.ProgramCommandLine` makes every project's program (inferred or from a tsconfig) the compiler's: the project's root files plus the prelude, with the compiler's fixed options (D-005) in place of the project's, memoized so that an edit reuses the program; `ls.ExtraDiagnostics` adds ParallelScript's semantic checks (`pts/check`: the numeric rules, the guardrail, the features left out) to a file's pulled diagnostics, as errors whose code is the PTS code and whose source is `pts`, ranged by the node they are about (diagnostics now carry start and end offsets). The checks run on the modules reachable from the file and only when none of them has a tsc error, as the compiler runs them only on programs tsc accepts; a panic in them becomes one warning on the file. `ptslib:///` paths are kept as URIs, as `bundled:///` ones are, so a definition in the library is a location the client can ask for; `.pts` files count for the project service's file watching and its globs. Errors that only lowering finds (most PTS1001s) aren't shown yet.
- **Reason:** M7.1, brief §6 (M7): the editor must see the program the compiler sees, or every `parallel`, integer type and `pts:` import is a red squiggle. Hooks in the two places where upstream builds a program's command line and a file's diagnostics are the smallest change (eight upstream files, each a line or a hook, `docs/FORK.md`), and the rest of the server (completions, hover, rename, code actions) comes for free.
- **Alternatives rejected:** A tsconfig.json written for the user (the options would be theirs to break, and the prelude isn't a file on disk). A server of our own wrapping upstream's over JSON-RPC to add diagnostics (a second process and protocol plumbing for what one hook does). Pushing PTS diagnostics with `publishDiagnostics` (upstream pushes only project-level diagnostics; files are pulled). Running the lowering for its PTS1001s on every request (it compiles the whole program; M7.3 can revisit with a cache).

### D-169 · The VS Code extension: a grammar generated from TypeScript's, library files through `workspace/textDocumentContent`, no semantic token for the `parallel` keyword (M7.2)
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** (1) `pts/editors/vscode` is a VS Code extension (VS Code 1.91 or later; vscode-languageclient 10, bundled with its dependencies into one minified `dist/extension.js` by esbuild, whose `dist/ThirdPartyNotices.txt` lists the bundle's packages' licenses). It contributes the language `parallelscript` for `.pts` (TypeScript's language configuration, from VS Code 1.141.0) and starts `<parallelscript.server.path> lsp --stdio` (default `pts`, looked up on PATH; `~/…` and paths relative to the first workspace folder are resolved) for `file:` and `untitled:` documents, pulling diagnostics on change and on save; **ParallelScript: Restart Language Server**, and a restart when the path setting changes. `make vscode` runs its tests and packages `dist/parallelscript.vsix` (version 0.7.0, publisher id `parallelscript`), installed with `code --install-extension`; it isn't published to the Marketplace. (2) The grammar, `source.pts`, is generated by `scripts/grammar.mjs` from VS Code's TypeScript grammar (vendored unchanged from VS Code 1.141.0, MIT): every scope's `.ts` suffix becomes `.pts`, and a rule first in `statements` and `expressionWithoutIdentifiers` scopes `parallel` as `keyword.control.parallel.pts` where D-120's parser reads the keyword: before `for` on the same line, or before, on the same line, an identifier, a number, a string, `{` or `~`, where the word isn't `as`, `satisfies`, `of`, `in` or `instanceof`. `test/parallel_samples.json` lists programs and where `parallel` is the keyword in them; the grammar's test (vscode-textmate and Oniguruma, as VS Code tokenizes) and a Go test of the parser (`pts/lsp/grammar_test.go`) check the same samples, so the two can't drift apart unnoticed. (3) Library files: the server answers LSP 3.18's `workspace/textDocumentContent` for the schemes in a new hook, `lsp.ContentSchemes` (none in upstream's own use; `pts lsp` sets `ptslib`, and `bundled` when TypeScript's libraries are embedded), reading the file from its file system and accepting the URI without its empty authority, as VS Code writes it (`ptslib:/parallel.pts`). vscode-languageclient registers a content provider for the schemes the server declares, so go to definition into `ptslib:///parallel.pts` or `bundled:///libs/lib.es5.d.ts` opens the file read-only, with no code in the extension. (4) The semantic tokens leave out the identifier of `parallel e` (the callee of a call marked `NodeFlagsPtsParallel`): VS Code's semantic highlighting, on in its default themes, would otherwise color the keyword as the prelude's function `parallel`. `parallel(e)` is a call of a function in both. (5) An end-to-end suite runs the extension in a real VS Code 1.141.0 (`@vscode/test-electron`, headless through Electron's ozone headless platform with `DISPLAY` unset, so no window opens): `npm run test:vscode`, and a CI job.
- **Reason:** M7.2; the brief's M7: "A VS Code extension that runs the forked language server for `.pts` files", "Syntax highlighting, including `parallel`", "PTS semantic diagnostics shown in the editor". Generating the grammar keeps TypeScript's highlighting exactly, follows upstream's fixes by replacing one vendored file, and gives every scope ParallelScript's suffix, as TypeScriptReact's grammar is TypeScript's with `.tsx`. A standard request for the library's text works in any client that implements LSP 3.18, and costs the extension nothing. The end-to-end suite found a bug no unit test had (VS Code drops the empty authority from `ptslib:///` URIs).
- **Alternatives rejected:** A grammar that includes `source.ts` and injects the keyword (it needs VS Code's built-in TypeScript extension enabled, and its scopes would end in `.ts`). A copy of TypeScript's grammar edited by hand (upstream's fixes would have to be merged by hand). A custom request or a `pts` subcommand for the library's text, or writing the library to disk for `file:` definitions (a protocol or files of our own for what LSP 3.18 standardizes; stale copies). The integer types as primitive scopes (semantic tokens color them as type aliases anyway, so they would change color once the server answers).
- **Known gaps:** A comment between `parallel` and its operand (`parallel /* c */ f()`) shows `parallel` as a name; the parser reads the keyword.

### D-170 · M7.3 split; `.par`'s `map`/`filter` keep returning arrays, with hints on tsc's errors; a literal with a decimal point is never an integer (M7.3a)
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** (1) M7.3 is split: M7.3a the smoke test's papercuts, `pts --help` and the docs refresh's papercuts; M7.3b `pts test`; M7.3c two programs written from scratch, the papercuts they find, and the guide's tutorial. (2) `.par.map`, `.par.filter` and `.par.flatMap` keep returning arrays (typed arrays for typed arrays), as D-L16's list, SPEC §5.5, the guide, the examples and the tests use them; their parallel methods are on `.par` again (`xs.par.map(f).par.sum()`), and `xs.par.count(predicate)` counts without the filtered array. Instead, tsc's errors that ParallelScript's features lead to get a hint (`frontend.Hint`): TS2339 for a view-only method (`count`, `sum`, `min`, `max`, `sortBy`, `toArray`) on an array or typed array says that `.par.m` returns an array and what to write (`.par.count(predicate)` or `.length` after `.par.filter`); TS2349 on `parallel (f)(x)`, where `parallel` is the prelude's, says that `(` after `parallel` starts a call of `parallel` (D-O05) and to write `parallel((async () => { … })())` or `parallel f()`. The CLI prints the hint on the line after tsc's message; the language server adds it as information, source `pts`, at the error's range (`pts lsp` now returns hints for a file with tsc errors, and ParallelScript's checks otherwise). (3) A numeric literal written with a decimal point (`2.0`, not `1e6` or `0x10`) whose context makes it an integer type is PTS2002: `seven / 2.0` with `seven: int` would have divided as integers and printed 3, where TypeScript prints 3.5. The message says to write the integer or to convert the other operand with `as number`. Literals still take their type from context (D-L07); this rejects the one case where that silently changed a program's arithmetic. (4) The type `Function` is PTS1001 with its own message suggesting a function type; `new Function` stays PTS1002. (5) `pts help <command>` and `pts <command> --help` print the command's usage (a usage error prints its command's), `pts version` prints `pts 0.7.0 (commit …; typescript-go 7.0.2)`, and the general usage lists the environment variables and exit codes. `u8[]`'s wrapping `reduce` stays as documented in the guide (valid code; checksums want it).
- **Reason:** M7.3 holds three features of different sizes; each part is a session's work. Returning views from `map`/`filter` would break every program that uses their results as arrays, and an array type with the views' methods would need extension methods the language doesn't have; tsc's message names the problem and a hint names the fix (the plan allowed either). Making `parallel (…)(…)` the operator would reopen D-O05's rule that `parallel(` is a call. The decimal-point literal was the papercut most likely to give a wrong answer silently.
- **Alternatives rejected:** `.par.map` returning `ParallelArray<U>` with `toArray()` (Rayon's and the brief's example's model, which REVIEW_LATER notes, but it breaks plain-array use). A parser rule for `parallel (function)(…)`. Typing `2.0` as `number` in an integer context (contradicts D-L07's literal rule rather than rejecting its surprising case). An error for `reduce`'s narrow accumulator.

### D-171 · `pts test`: `pts:test` and `pts:assert` as Node's `node:test` and `node:assert/strict`, a file a program, results through a file (M7.3b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** (1) Tests are written as for Node's built-in runner: `pts:test` has the common part of `node:test` (`test`/`it`, `describe`/`suite`, `before`, `after`, `beforeEach`, `afterEach`, `skip` and `todo` options, async tests, a `TestContext` with `skip()`, `todo()` and `diagnostic()`) and `pts:assert` is `node:assert/strict`'s common part, with Node's messages and `AssertionError`. Both are ParallelScript sources in `pts/lib`; the differential tests map `pts:assert` to `node:assert/strict`, so a program that uses it is compared with Node. (2) A test file is an ordinary program: registering a test schedules the run after the top-level code (`setImmediate`), tests run one at a time in order, results print in the format of Node's spec reporter, and a failure sets the exit code; `pts run x.test.pts` prints its own totals. (3) `pts test [paths]` finds Node's test file names with the `.pts` extension (`name.test.pts`, `name-test.pts`, `name_test.pts`, `test-name.pts`, `test.pts`), past `node_modules` and dot directories, builds them a few at a time, runs each in turn with `PTS_TEST_RESULTS` naming a file where `pts:test` writes its totals and failures as JSON instead of printing them, and prints the totals and every failure of all files; a file that doesn't compile, or ends without reporting, counts as one failed test. No test files is an error, so a mistyped path doesn't pass. (4) What ParallelScript can't express is left out and documented (SPEC §8): `test.skip(…)` (functions have no properties; a hint says to pass `{ skip: true }`), `assert(x)` (the default export is an object), an error class as `throws`'s matcher (classes aren't values; the error says to match `{ name: … }`); and subtests, `only`, mocks, snapshots and reporters aren't there yet. (5) Writing the two modules found three compiler gaps, fixed here: `instanceof Map`/`Set`, a crash in `for…of` over a dynamic value tsc narrowed to a Map, and the message for an error class used as a value.
- **Reason:** M7.3b; the brief's CLI table (§2.3) lists `pts test`, a test runner. TypeScript developers know `node:test` and `node:assert` (and Bun's and Deno's runners mirror them), so tests read as they would in Node and the assertion module can be held to Node's behavior by the differential tests. One process per file is Node's model and keeps a file's crash or `process.exit` from taking the others down. Results through a file keep the human-readable output the same whether a file runs alone or under `pts test`.
- **Alternatives rejected:** One binary importing every test file (one build, but one file's crash ends all, and failures can't be traced to files). A runner in Go that parses the programs' output (the output format would become a protocol). Running files concurrently (outputs interleave; tests that use every core compete). A Jest-style `expect` API (not what Node has; it could be a module later).

### D-172 · Papercuts found by two programs written from scratch, fixed where they gave wrong results or blocked common idioms (M7.3c)
- **Date:** 2026-10-13
- **Decision:** Two subagents wrote a program each from scratch as TypeScript developers new to ParallelScript (an inventory CLI over pts:fs with tests; a parallel text indexer with `.par`, `parallel`, `parallel for`, pts:sync and tests), without reading the tests or the compiler. Of their findings, these are fixed: (1) a crash: a local declared `T | undefined` and set from an element read is narrowed by tsc to `T`, and `raw?.length` segfaulted on its undefined (an unchecked cast). Such locals are now loose (D-090) whether or not the program tests them, so reads as a `T` are checked (TypeError, as Node's) and `?.`, `??` and tests see the union; an optional chain on a loose local may be undefined whatever tsc's type says. (2) Destructuring past the end of a `(T | undefined)[]` threw RangeError; the binding is loose too, so it is undefined. (3) Reading a missing key of a hash-map object whose entry type holds undefined (`process.env.X`, `{ [k: string]: string | undefined }`) through a property threw RangeError; it reads undefined, as Node's does. (4) `??` keeps its left operand's integer type without null and undefined (`counts.get(k) ?? 0` on a `Map<string, int>` is an `int`, not PTS2001), an expression typed `int | undefined` is typed by its `int`, and `c ? 7 : undefined` where an `int | undefined` goes makes an int. (5) `c ? {} : options` (the empty literal takes the other branch's object type when it fits) and `c ? [] : counts` where a `Map<…> | number[]` goes were PTS1001. (6) An `await` anywhere in an async arrow's expression body (`async (f) => await read(f)`) was PTS1001 with a message about loop conditions; the body is lowered as a return's value, its awaits hoisted first (D-111). (7) Messages: a class used as a value (the error classes and the program's) says to test with `instanceof` (in `assert.throws`, `(e) => e instanceof C`, replacing D-171's `{ name: … }` advice, which a subclass that doesn't set its name defeats); PTS3001 suggests an `AtomicInt` for an integer count and a `Mutex` for anything else instead of "an `Atomic`"; PTS3002 for `await` says to await `parallel` tasks together; PTS2001 and PTS2002 say "an int", "an i8", "a u8"; `.par` on a readonly array, a Map or a Set gets a hint. (8) Stack traces leave out assembly frames (`clone3.S` ended every trace from a task). (9) The guide said that allocation-heavy `.par` code "stops gaining" past four threads and that racing writes to a Map "lose entries"; it now says that such code can be several times slower on eight threads than on one (with an example and `PTS_THREADS=4`) and that a Map can hold a key twice, as SPEC §4.1 says. The rest is recorded in PROGRESS.md for later.
- **Reason:** M7.3c. A crash on an accepted program breaks the race-safety and no-crash promises (M6.0a closed every known one); the other fixes are idioms a TypeScript developer writes in the first hour (`process.env.X`, the counting idiom, `async (x) => await f(x)`, `const [a, b] = parts`), and the messages are where they met ParallelScript's rules.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Checking every narrowed read of a nullable union (correct, but a check in hot loops for the one unsound source, element reads, that loose locals already cover). A default of four threads, or a GC change, for the allocation slowdown (four threads cost CPU-bound code half its speedup; the collector is M8's). A guardrail for `push`/`set` on captured collections in parallel bodies (the owner's call, D-L17; REVIEW_LATER).

### D-173 · H01 review statuses and execution evidence are separate from fixes
- **Date:** 2026-10-14
- **Decision:** The three final reviews are deduplicated in `docs/reviews/2026-10-13-final/CONSOLIDATED.md`, with separate verification and resolution states, source-item references, minimal inputs, acceptance and H01–H34 ownership. A source-review claim is unverified until fresh parent execution confirms it; a documented historical residual remains open when the approved hardening plan calls for its correction. H01 retains expected-Node smoke probes and RED outputs outside the default passing suite; the owning implementation task must turn them into RED→GREEN regression tests. Classified corpus counts and actual comparisons are reported separately. No compiler/runtime fix, new parallel guardrail or fixed-layout semantic change is part of H01.
- **Reason:** The reports mix reproduced bugs, intentional numeric/object/race behavior, unmeasured optimization ideas, unsupported coverage and historical partial fixes. Treating all of them as either fixed or proven would conceal remaining scope. A green suite can still skip compatible cases and fail independent probes.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Implementing every recommendation without reproduction; weakening expected output to match a bug; adding expected-panic passing tests that freeze defects; counting skipped programs as executed; changing D-L10/D-L17 in response to review recommendations.

### D-174 · H01 split after a content refusal: baseline and consolidation do not close blocked reproductions
- **Date:** 2026-10-15
- **Decision:** H01a owns the permitted baseline, corpus-count verification and review-consolidation checkpoint; H01b retains the entire independent defect-reproduction acceptance. H01 and H01b remain unchecked. The previous gpt-reproducer assignment (agent `ad8d8d17371a988bc`, 2026-10-14) was refused upstream; record its exact scope and message, preserve its isolated worktree, and do not retry/rephrase the assignment or route it to another model/provider. New read-only GPT children may audit unrelated baseline arithmetic and documentation; they do not rerun the refused assignment. Keep inherited execution evidence dated and SHA-qualified, and distinguish configured model/effort from an actual backend receipt. A green baseline/documentation checkpoint is not proof that independent defect probes passed or were completed.
- **Reason:** The owner explicitly requires refusals to remain blockers, while permitting unrelated work to continue. Splitting the task allows a verified checkpoint without silently dropping the blocked smoke programs, RED outputs or parent verification.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Retrying individual pieces of the refused assignment under a new prompt; declaring H01 done from the old reports; deleting the failed child's worktree; proceeding to H02 while calling H01 complete; claiming an executed inference model from configuration alone.

### D-175 · H01b redone under claude-opus-5-5; reproductions are retained evidence probes, and new findings join their fix tasks
- **Date:** 2026-10-15
- **Decision:** After the owner's 2026-10-15 switch of all remaining work to claude-opus-5-5, H01b was done fresh with its original, unreworded scope. The GPT refusal stays on record (D-174), and Opus did not refuse. The reproductions are retained as evidence probes under `docs/agent/hardening-evidence/probes/h01b/`, not added to `pts/tests` as skipped tests: each program carries a machine-readable header (row, red/control, Node oracle, threads, runs, expected and baseline behaviour, and for a red probe a `failure:` regex for how it fails today), and `run_probes.py` exits 0 only when every red probe fails as stated and every control matches Node (with a healthy oracle and no timeout), so a fix that turns a red probe green, a red probe failing some other way, or a broken control is visible. Each fix task (H02, H03, H05, H06, H07) promotes its row's red probes to RED→GREEN regressions in the suite. Probe binaries run with a 4 GiB address-space cap, and the infinite-iterator case only by hand under a 1 GiB cap. Defects the reproduction found beyond the reviews become ledger rows C18 (eager drain of `any` iterables, and of typed iterables by array patterns, in H03), C19 (erased-callback record keys, in H02) and C20 (`.par.flatMap` type argument, in H05); their plan entries name them. A killed predecessor child's drafts (session 13, cut off when its parent ended its turn, not refused) were handed over as drafts only; nothing from its raw outputs counts as evidence, and its worktree is left intact.
- **Reason:** The owner's switch removed the provider block while keeping the "no rewording" rule. Probes outside the suite keep RED evidence without adding skipped or expected-failure tests to the suite (V01 and H18: skips must not hide crashes, and a skip must not count as a pass), and without changing the test harness in a reproduction-only slice.
