Attaching new defects to the task that touches the same code keeps every finding owned without inventing new plan items.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Skipped tests in `pts/tests` now (a harness edit in a no-fix slice, and skips hide crashes, as V01 says); new plan items H03b etc. for C18–C20 (the existing tasks own the same code paths); reusing the killed child's raw outputs; running the infinite-iterator probe uncapped on the shared host.

### D-176 · Logical assignments on dynamic places, `any` record entries and keys, narrowed results, and a dynamic write's value (H02)
- **Date:** 2026-10-15
- **Decision:** (1) `??=` and `||=` on a dynamic keyed place (a property or element of a value of type `any`, which includes erased generic code's records, shapes, arrays and generic classes' mapped-type fields, D-078) read the place once and store through the dynamic path, as `&&=` already did, instead of the hash-map path that panicked (C01). (2) `??=` tests a place of type `any` or `unknown` for null and undefined; it never stored there (C21). (3) An entry of a hash-map object whose type is `any` or `unknown` is tested for truth by `||` and `||=` and for null and undefined by `??` and `??=`, as a union entry is; a present falsy or nullish entry was kept (C21). (4) A compound assignment reads a missing entry whose type is `any` or holds undefined as undefined, as a plain read does since D-172 (`r.missing &&= v`, `r.missing += 1` give JS's results, not RangeError); entries of other types keep D-059's RangeError. (5) A record key of type `any` (an erased call's result, `k: any`) is converted to a string as JS converts property keys (`String(k)`; symbols are not values here), so it indexes concrete records for reads, writes, compound assignments, `in`, `delete` and `Object.hasOwn` (C19). (6) The value of `??=`, `||=` and `&&=` has the type tsc gives the expression (C22): on a union place, the member or smaller union tsc narrows it to, converted with a check as a narrowed property is (D-089, D-140), since `&&=` gives whatever falsy value the place holds and a variable or field without a value holds undefined outside its type (D-141); on a dynamic place that tsc types (a keyed element, D-089, or a value a type guard narrowed), a checked conversion, as a read of it has. Where tsc's type is neither the place's type nor a narrowing of it (`(cache ??= new Map())` is a `Map<any, any>` to tsc, `(lists[k] ??= new Array())` an `any[]`), the value has the place's type, without null and undefined for `??=` and `||=` whose right side can't be either: the value is the place's or was stored there, converted to its type. Inside `||=` and `&&=`, the stored value is converted to the type of the place's narrowed read, so both operands have one type (a keyed element's, a guarded value's or a loose local's mixed them, which printed a double's bits as a number). A value nothing uses (`x &&= v;`, a comma's left, `void`, a `for` update) isn't converted, and `||=`/`&&=` there test the place's value as it is, so a statement never throws where JS doesn't. It had the place's type, which was PTS1001, C that clang rejected, or a panic in the C emitter. A type-guarded `unknown` (JSON data) keeps SPEC §8's copies: `(raw.list ??= new Array()).push(x)` pushes onto a copy, as `raw.list!.push(x)` already did. (7) The value of a write through `any` or in erased code (`pts_dynamic_set`) is the value as stored: the checked copy where converting dynamic data to the place's type made one (D-072), and otherwise the right side itself, as before. So `(rec[k] ??= []).push(x)` and `(o.p = []).push(x)` push onto what was stored, as in JS (C24); a separate reference to the right side still holds the original (SPEC §8, identity). Typed arrays, `length` and `lastIndex` still give the right side as written, as JS does.
- **Reason:** H02 (C01, C19). The panic hid three silent wrong results on the same operators: `??=` on `any` places never stored, `any` record entries were never tested, and the idiom's push went to an unstored copy in erased code; fixing only the panic would have turned a crash into wrong output. The reproducer's C22 is the same operators' result typing and broke the lazy-initialization idiom in plain code (D-175: findings join the task that owns the code). Making a write's value the stored value is what typed code already does (a typed store's value is the converted operand) and is the one choice under D-072's copies that keeps `(o.p = v) === o.p`, which the common idioms rely on, rather than `(o.p = v) === v`. A record key is not a typed destination, so converting it as JS does loses no check; reads and writes of the entry keep their checks.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Reading the place back after a store to get its value (calls a getter JS doesn't call). A checked conversion of `any` keys to `string` (throws where JS converts, e.g. a number from an erased callback). Narrowing without a check (the slice's first version: the independent review showed `&&=` on a variable or field without a value segfaulting). Leaving C22 to H04 (H04 contains compiler failures; it doesn't fix the operators' typing). Changing D-072's copies so that both identities hold (a representation change the locked rulings exclude). `{} as Record<K, …>` with `K` a union of literals (C23, PTS1001 before and after) is a `{}`-as-T builder, which H29 owns. Plain `=` on a keyed element whose value is used as an array (`(lists[k] = ["z"]).length`, C26) panics the C emitter before and after; it is H04's, with a skipped test (`run/keyed_assign_value.pts`). An `await` or `yield` on the right side of these operators is hoisted before the test (C25, before and after), which H08 owns.

### D-177 · Lazy iteration through `any` and typed iterables, element-by-element array patterns, void getters and bare `return;` (H03a); H03 split
- **Date:** 2026-10-15
- **Decision:**
  - **Split.** H03 is split into H03a (this decision: C02, C27, C03's typed half, C18 and the related C29, C31, C36, C37 and C38) and H03b (C28, C30, C32, found by H03's reproducer: methods of generator objects and runtime iterators through `any`; `it.next()` on a typed iterator building its result at the call; `yield*` not handing the inner result through; and C33 and C35, found by the review). The parent task stays open until both are done (D-175's rule: findings join the task that owns the code).
  - **Void and never getters (C02).** A getter typed `void` (a body that only throws or returns nothing) or `never` has the IR result `undefined`: it is a property's value, which descriptors, `any` and interfaces read as one. With the result `void` there was no run-time check and no field kind for it (the C emitter's panic), and it wasn't the interface property's type (`undefined`, PTS1001).
  - **Bare `return;` (C27).** In a function, arrow or method whose result isn't `void`, `return;` returns undefined converted to the result type (`number | undefined`, `any`, `unknown`, a getter's). It was a C `return;` that clang rejects in a function that returns a value. Generators and async functions return through their frames, as before.
  - **`for…of` (C18, C29).** A loop over a dynamic value (`any`, a boxed union) or over an iterator or iterable type steps through `pts_iteration_open`'s source: the iterator of a generator, a runtime iterator, a map or set (live, as JS's) or an object with `[Symbol.iterator]`, resumed one `next()` per iteration and closed when the loop ends early, as typed iterators already were (D-109, D-146); or an array's, tuple's or typed array's items read live by index, and a string's code points as a new array, which JS's iterators over them can't be told apart from. Before, a loop over `any` drained a generator or an object's iterator into an array first (no `return()`, wrong interleaving, unbounded memory for an infinite one) and iterated maps, sets and typed arrays as snapshots.
  - **Array patterns (C18, C31).** A pattern over a dynamic value, a class, shape or interface with `[Symbol.iterator]`, or an iterator or iterable type binds element by element, as JS's IteratorBindingInitialization does: one `next()` per element and per elision, each element bound (its default applied to undefined, its nested pattern destructured) before the next step, undefined after the iterator is done, a rest element taking what is left; the iterator is closed at the end unless it is done, and when binding an element throws (a default, a nested pattern, a checked conversion) it is closed before the error goes on, an error from closing dropped, as a loop's after a throw; one whose `next()` threw isn't closed. The protection (a handler around the bindings, whose names are declared ahead) is emitted only when an element can throw, so `const [k, v] = pair` of `any` names pays nothing. Names a declaration's own pattern binds without a default are loose locals (D-172) for every source but a tuple, whose length tsc knows (`readsElement`; a type parameter counts as its constraint): arrays as before, and now strings, sets, maps, typed arrays, iterators, iterables (an interface extending `Iterable` too) and dynamic values; past the end they hold undefined, not RangeError. Any name a pattern binds from a dynamic item (an `any` value's element, an iterator's item, a set's element, a map's entry) whose type has no in-place run-time check (a tuple, array, shape or interface, or a union with one: not `isCheckable`) holds the item converted to that type once, at the binding (`patternItem`; a loose name keeps undefined as undefined): converting a loose name's item at every read copied a map's entry (an array) into a new tuple each time, losing writes and identity, and a name of a union type with a tuple member held the array itself, which a narrowed read took for a tuple's fields (a segfault for a parameter pattern on the base, C37). An element read from an array with a declared element type (not `any[]`) is one of that type already and is kept as it is. So a name of such a type in a parameter pattern, a `for…of` binding, a nested or a rest element's pattern is checked at the binding, as D-070 checks any dynamic value entering a typed place and as names of other types already were: type-unsound `any` data throws TypeError there (after the iterator is closed; an async function rejects), and past the end of an iterable such a name throws TypeError (it was RangeError, H11's). A union member dynamic data can't be converted to yet (a generator, an iterator) keeps the item as it is. A loose 64-bit integer (`int`, `i64`, `u64`) is read exactly, not through a double (D-012); `x as i64` keeps `as`'s conversion. `+=` concatenates onto a loose local whose narrowed read is a string (it was PTS1001, also for the arrays loose before this slice: C36), and onto a string calls a right side's `valueOf` first when the right side is of a class type with `valueOf`, as `+` does (it called `toString`, C38); with an `any`, union or interface right side, `+=`, like `+`, still calls `toString` (C40, H19). A pattern over a typed array has no close-on-throw handler: its items are read by index and there is nothing to close. A `let` pattern in a `for` initializer gives only its names per-iteration copies (`declaredNames`), not the locals its lowering adds. Typed `Map`/`Set` values, tuples, arrays and strings keep their paths. Spread, `Array.from`, `new Set`/`new Map` and rest elements still take everything, as in JS.
  - **Getter results (C03, typed half).** An object literal with getters or setters as a typed iterator result (`IteratorResult<T>`, `IteratorYieldResult<T>`) stays unsupported: D-109's result shapes hold `done` and `value` in fields, so making one from the literal would run its getters at the `return`, a done result's value getter included (D-146). It is now a source-located PTS1001 in the program's types that names the alternative (``an object literal with getters or setters as an iterator result (`IteratorResult<number, any>`) is not supported yet; a `next(): any` can return one``), where it said `converting object4 to object1 | object2`, reported only where converting a literal with a `done` or `value` accessor to the result fails: a literal that tsc merely types in that context (an unannotated `next()`'s result, which the runtime reads as a dynamic object) or whose accessors are other properties builds as before. A `next(): any` result is read lazily by `for…of`, patterns, spread and the rest. Those consumers read a result's `done` and `value` by name, so for them a getter literal would be representable; it is typed reads after narrowing on `done` that use the fields. An assertion `as IteratorResult<T>` of a literal of another layout (getters, or properties beyond `done` and `value`) compiled before this slice and still does, and such a read then takes the field layout: a getter's closure, or a SIGSEGV past the object (C35, found by the review; H03b's). The diagnostic doesn't suggest `as`.
- **Reason:** H03's acceptance (CONSOLIDATED C02, C03, C18): valid code runs without a panic, `done` is read before an unneeded `value`, iterators step lazily and close, infinite ones work, and an unsupported form gets an honest diagnostic. C27 shares C02's fix (a `void` getter's `return;`) and is everyday TypeScript. Measured on this host (hyperfine, interleaved base/new binaries of the same programs; scratch `/tmp/pts-h03/bench`; these first figures on the slice's first version, confirmed by the review's round 1 and not re-measured after): array patterns over `any` arrays 2.08× faster (no copy of the whole array), over `any` map entries 1.60× faster, `for…of` over a typed `Iterable<number>` holding an array 1.54× faster (no iterator object); `for…of` over `any` arrays 1.09× slower for one 10-million-element array and 1.18× slower for tiny nested loops, from the close-on-throw handler JS's IteratorClose requires. Names that are now loose are boxed and checked at each use as D-172 says; with the checked conversion to a number made inline (`pts_value_to_number_checked` in `pts.h`, its slow path out of line), 100 million reads of a name destructured once cost, against the base: a `Set<number>`'s 1.12×, a `Float64Array`'s 1.05×, an array's 0.35× (loose before this slice too, now inline), and 30 million of a string's 1.25×; destructuring itself costs 1.04× for a set, 1.13× for a string and 0.19× for a `Float64Array` (no copy). Without the inline check the set and typed-array reads cost 2.9× and 2.8× (H03 review round 3). Before the stack fix below, converting a pattern's dynamic item once at the binding (and not an element of a `T[]`, which is kept as it is) left destructure-and-read loops at 1.03× the base for `const [r] = rows` of `number[][]`, 1.00× for shapes and 1.73× for a typed `Map`'s entry. Every dynamic conversion zero-filled a 4 KB cycle stack (`pts_value_try_convert`, `object_fits`, `pts_check_implies`), which the binding-time conversions made show (union-typed names 1.4–5.2× the base, H03 review round 5); the stacks are now only initialised by their depth, since only entries below it are read. With that, against the base: `const [e] = m` of a typed `Map` in a loop 0.91×, a generator of `[number, number] | null` 1.03×, a parameter pattern over `Iterable<P | null>` 0.61×, typed `number[][]` rows 0.34× and shapes 0.14× (the base paid the memset at each read); a `Set<Circle | Square>`'s loose union names 1.81×, since each read checks the union again.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Draining only generators and keeping the index loop for objects (objects' iterators can be infinite and have `return()`). Opening a runtime iterator per loop for `any` values too, as typed iterables did (1.77× slower on the tiny nested loops; the index cursor needs no allocation). Taking a pattern's values first and binding after (`pts_iterator_take`, D-109): it closed the outer iterator before a nested pattern ran and stepped before defaults ran. A handler around every iterable pattern (a `_setjmp` for each `[k, v]` of `any`). A dynamic result type for a `next()` that returns a getter literal (typed callers would get a run-time TypeError instead of the compile-time diagnostic). A `void` result with an undefined check for getters (descriptors call a getter for a value; a `void` C function has none).
- **Known gaps:** names that parameter patterns, `for…of` bindings, nested patterns and patterns inside a rest element bind past the end throw if typed (not `any`), a checked TypeError for iterables and RangeError for arrays, since D-172's loose locals cover a declaration's own pattern (H11); destructuring assignment over iterables (`[a, b] = it`) stays PTS1001; C28, C30, C32, C33 (an elision reads the result's `value`, where JS's IteratorStep reads only `done`) and C35 (`as IteratorResult<T>` of another layout, above) are H03b's; C34 (an object literal's getter named `[Symbol.iterator]` panics `accessorFields`, on the base too) and C39 (clang rejects `await` of a narrowed `Promise<number> | [number]` and narrowing a union with a `Generator` member to a tuple, on the base too) are H04's; C40 (`+` and `+=` with an `any`, union or interface operand call `toString` where JS calls `valueOf`, on the base too) is H19's. A generator's or async function's parameter patterns still run at the first `next()` or later (D-109's known gap).

### D-178 · Iterator results are the objects iterators hand back, read by name off any other layout; iterators' methods through `any`; `yield*` hands inner results through (H03b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-16
- **Decision:**
  - **Results as objects (C30, C35, C03's typed half).** A value of an iterator result type (D-109's result shapes for `IteratorYieldResult<T>`, `IteratorReturnResult<R>` and unions of them) is whatever object the iterator handed back: one of D-109's result shapes, which typed code reads by field, or any other object of the program's (an object's own `next()`'s result, an object literal with getters or more properties, an asserted literal, a class's instance). Such a shape is marked `ir.Class.Result`. A read or write of its `done` or `value` tests the object's descriptor against those of the shape's property orders (`isShapeObject`: a literal written `{ done, value }` is the shape's second order, D-108), and on any other object finds the property by name: a field, a getter, a class's method (read as through `any`, bound as D-144 binds it), or undefined if there is none (`pts_result_get`, whose site caches fields that hold a `pts_value`; a missing property costs a look at the object's members, no allocation). The property is converted to its type with a check as D-070 converts dynamic values (through one shape a `done` that isn't a boolean reads as its truth, as IteratorComplete reads it; through a union result type it fails the check, `Expected boolean | undefined`), and a write goes through `any`'s path (`pts_result_set`); a read-modify-write (`r.value++`, `r.value += x`, `??=`) reads the property once, also when its right side yields: the read D-145 keeps from before the `yield` is the one stored to, converted to the place's type (`readKeptAndStore`; it read the property again after the resume, which an interface's receiver did on the base too, and until review round 4 still did for a union-typed value and for element access, `r["value"] += yield v`, on results and interfaces). A property of a union with an iterator result among its members (`IteratorResult<T, R>`, or one with another object type) is read the same way, through a `Result` interface. The types of results' properties are readied for dynamic conversion (`prepareDynamicTypes`; an interface gets its copy shape), so a class instance whose `value: any` holds parsed JSON reads as an `IteratorResult<Row>`'s `value`. Objects of another layout therefore convert to a result type as they are (`asResult`): a literal with getters returned from `next(): IteratorResult<T>` (the PTS1001 of D-177 is gone), `x as IteratorResult<T>` of a literal or class instance (C35: a narrowed `.value` read the field offset, a getter's closure, or memory past the object), and a union of result shapes into another result union (`IteratorResult<T, undefined>` where an `IteratorResult<T>` goes, PTS1001 before). A conversion of a dynamic value to a result type, or to an array, tuple, union, map value or field that holds results, keeps any of the program's objects as it is (`fromValue`; the `result` flag of the shape's `pts_check`, which the runtime's conversions honour) and copies other dynamic data with a check (D-071). In a union of a result type and another object type, the other members take an object first, as it is or by a copy, and a result member takes an object of another layout only after they have all failed (`results_last` in `convert.c`): otherwise an object that only a copy makes a `Point` would stay a result, and reads through `Point` would find a field of another kind. So a `next(): IteratorResult<T>` returning an `any` getter object, an `await` of a typed `AsyncIterator`'s `next()`, `Promise.all` of them, an annotated local and an `any` array of results read nothing of them until the program does (C42; they ran both getters, a done result's value one too).
  - **Leaving the result types.** A result converted to an interface (a parameter `{ value: number | undefined }`, a conditional's branch) is the interface's object as it is if it is one of its shape's, and otherwise converted with the interface's check (`ir.ClassCast.FromResult`): kept if its declared types fit, copied otherwise, as D-071 converts dynamic data, so a copy's identity, writes through the interface and its getters' timing (read at the conversion) differ from JS (pinned by `run/iterator_results_copied.pts`). Reads through the interface then always find the representation the interface expects (they threw a TypeError that only a compiler bug makes when a literal's `value: number` field was read as a `pts_value`). This holds for every route to an interface: a parameter, an interface with optional properties the result lacks (D-066's relaxed casts), a conditional's branches, and a conversion from `any` of an object that holds a result: a result type's check implies no other object type's (`implies` in `convert.c`), so a class whose `IteratorResult<T>` field holds an object of another layout is copied where an interface with an object-typed property goes, and that field is converted with the property's check.
  - **Typed `next()` (C30).** `it.next(v)`, `it.return!(v)` and `it.throw!(e)` on an iterator type (the `!` form reaches the lowering now; `Iterator<T>`'s `return` and `throw` are optional under strict TypeScript) call the iterator (`IteratorCall`, `pts_iterator_call`) and give the object its method returned as it is: an object's own `next()`'s, after a check that it is an object, nothing of which is read at the call, or what a generator's `yield*` handed back. A generator or runtime iterator that makes its result itself still gets a D-109 shape made at the call. An object without the method, or one of the runtime's iterators over arrays, strings, maps and sets (which have only `next()`), throws JS's TypeError (`it.return is not a function`), named by the call's source text.
  - **`yield*` (C32).** A `yield*` whose inner iterator's step handed back a result object (an object's `next()`'s, or an inner generator's own `yield*`) yields that object as it is, as JS's GeneratorYield(innerResult) does (`ir.Yield.Delegate`, `pts_generator_delegate`): a consumer that steps the generator reads its `done` again (`pts_iterator_resume`) and its `value` when it asks for it, and a call of the generator's `next()` gets the object itself. Forwarded `return(v)` and `throw(e)` results that aren't done are handed through the same way. Once the inner iterator is done, the `yield*` reads its last result's `value`, also when the expression's value is unused (it read it only where the value was used; C55).
  - **Lazy values and closing (C33, C41).** An object's iterator keeps each result object and reads its `value` only when a consumer asks for it (`pts_iterator_value`), as JS's IteratorStep leaves it for IteratorValue: an elision in an array pattern reads `done` only. Closing an iterator (`pts_iterator_close`, IteratorClose) calls `return()` and checks that its result is an object, reading nothing of it (it read `done`, and `value` when not done).
  - **Methods through `any` (C28, C45).** A generator object, an async generator object and the runtime's iterators held as `any` have their methods: `next(v)`, `return(v)` and `throw(e)` for generators and objects' iterators, an async generator's giving promises, and `next()` for the iterators of arrays, strings, maps and sets, which have no `return` or `throw` (`TypeError: h.return is not a function`, as Node). A result the iterator makes is a plain object `{ value, done }` (the hash-map object async results already were); one it handed back is that object. Maps', sets' and arrays' `keys()`, `values()` and `entries()` through `any` give their runtime iterators (an `any` array had none). As for every builtin method through `any`, they are found by calls only: `typeof h.next` is `"undefined"`, as `typeof a.push` is.
- **Reason:** H03b's acceptance (CONSOLIDATED C28, C30, C32, C33, C35): getters on a typed `next()`'s result run when the caller reads them, `yield*` passes inner results through, an elision reads only `done`, and no narrowed read of a result takes another layout's offset. One rule covers them: D-109's shapes stay the fast path for the results the runtime makes and for literals typed as results, and any other object is read by name, which is what JS does for every result. With reads by name the reason D-177 gave for C03's diagnostic is gone, so the typed getter literal works, as H03 asked "where feasible". Callgrind instruction counts (`PTS_THREADS=1`, 1,000,000 steps, base `a65a75d62`, the final tree; programs in `h03b-review-probes.tar.gz` under `session4/bench/`): a typed `next()` loop over a class's iterator 0.55× (its result is no longer rebuilt at the call; 0.53× written done first), over a `Generator<number, void>` (a union, read by name) 0.85×, over a `Generator<number, number>` (one shape, a descriptor test per read) 1.11×; `for…of` over a generator 1.03×, over a class's iterable 1.03× (also done first; an earlier 1.06× didn't reproduce), `yield*` 1.04×; 2,000,000 reads of stored results: a union's 0.72×, one shape's made by the runtime 1.21×, one shape's written done first 1.28× (3.56× before the order descriptors were tested), a user type with the result shape's fields 1.01×. Review round 1 found that the method test `pts_iterator_call` gained for its TypeError cost about 500 instructions a step (0.94× instead of 0.57×); the method's call names it instead. Review round 2 found three defects of the intermediate tree, fixed and pinned: typed `return!()`/`throw!()` on runtime iterators finished them silently, two routes to an interface skipped the conversion (a regression for asserted literals), and `r.value++` read a getter twice. Review round 3 found three more, fixed and pinned: a union of a result and another object type kept an object the other member needed copied (hence `results_last`), `r.value += yield v` read the getter again after the resume, and a result's check implied an interface's by declared types (hence `implies`). Review round 4 found that the second fix missed union-typed values and element access, and that reads by name missed class methods; both fixed and pinned. Review round 5 found that the method fallback made a key string and a dynamic lookup for every read of a missing property (2.72× for a typed iterator whose results leave out `done`, which TypeScript allows); it now looks for a method first (1.03×; 0.75× with `done` written).
- **Alternatives rejected:** Iterator result types as dynamic values (`Value`): every typed read would be a dynamic get and a check, and literals typed as results would lose D-109's shapes. Interfaces instead of shapes for the result types (the same cost at every read, and conversions from dynamic results would copy into the interface's shape, reading getters). Rebuilding a D-109 shape from a handed object at the call (it reads `done` and `value` there: C30). An honest diagnostic for `as IteratorResult<T>` of another layout (it rejects code that is safe once reads go by name, and leaves the `any` door open). Converting a handed object at the moment it enters a result type (reads its getters). Values for builtin methods through `any` (`typeof h.next === "function"`): every builtin method would need a bound function value; it isn't H03b's.
- **Supersedes:** D-177's honest PTS1001 for an object literal with getters as an `IteratorResult` (C03's typed half), and the hazard D-177 left for `as IteratorResult<T>` of another layout (C35): both work now.
- **Known gaps:** a hand-written result without `done` reads `done` as `false` through a one-shape result type (`Iterator<T, T>`), where JS gives `undefined` (a union result type gives `undefined`); a handed object without `value` can't gain it by a write (`TypeError: Cannot add property value, object is not extensible`, the brief's fixed layouts, D-158); a handed result's `value` holding dynamic data that its type converts by copying (parsed JSON in a class's `any` field where an interface goes, an `any[]` where a tuple goes) is converted at each read, so writes through `r.value` are lost and `r.value === r.value` is false (D-071's copy, which typed code makes once, C51); spreading a handed result or taking its object rest copies only the result type's properties, as spreading an interface's value does; destructuring a result (`const { value } = it.next()`; C44, H29) and a union of results converted to an interface stay PTS1001, as do `it.return?.(v)` and `if (it.return)` on an `Iterator<T>` (C43, H29); a typed `return()` whose result isn't an object fails its checked conversion with internal shape names (`Expected object3 | object4, got number`; Node: `Iterator result 5 is not an object`; C52, H22); arrays' `keys()` and `entries()`, typed or through `any`, are snapshots made at the call (D-110); `h[Symbol.iterator]()` through `any` is PTS1001 (D-111; C48); calling a result's function-typed `value` (`r.value()`) is PTS1001, as on the base (read it into a local first; C62, H29), and so is narrowing a result with `instanceof` to a class (`converting object1 | object2 to Entry`; C61, H29); TypeScript's `in` narrowing across a result and another object type can reach a read that the object's actual property fails, which throws D-072's or D-158's TypeError where JS reads the value, as for interfaces (C59, intentional).

### D-179 · Internal compiler errors are contained and reported at their source (PTS9001, exit code 3); assignment values and narrowed dynamic values take tsc's types (H04)
- **Date:** 2026-10-16
- **Decision:**
  - **Containment.** A bug the compiler runs into is an *internal compiler error*: a `*diagnostic.InternalError` with code `PTS9001`, reported where the compiler was working, never a Go panic trace or the C compiler's raw output. Each of pts's phases records what it works on: the check pass the innermost node it visits (`pass.at`); the lowering the innermost statement or expression it lowers (`lowerer.current`, set by `lowering` and restored when the node is done, so that a panic leaves the innermost), and, while it declares classes, functions, module bindings and class members, the statement or class it declares; the C emitter the IR position of what it writes (`out.source`, set by `at`). A phase defers `diagnostic.Locate`, which turns a panic into the internal error at that position (the lowering's and the checks' earlier errors kept in `Earlier`, since an earlier PTS1001 may explain the failure) and panics with it; `driver.EmitC` defers `diagnostic.Contain`, which returns it, or one without a position for a panic outside the phases. Only panics are turned into errors, at that one boundary, with what a report needs kept; nothing is retried, and the compilation stops. C that the C compiler rejects is an internal error too (`generatedCFailure`): at the `.pts` line its first `error:` has through `#line` (no column: it is C's), or without a position for an error in the declarations before the first `#line`; so is an `undefined reference` from the linker in the generated program's own object. Other toolchain failures (clang missing or killed, a full disk, the runtime's build, an undefined reference in a runtime archive's member, which a broken or mismatched runtime makes) stay as they were; `toolchain.CommandError` carries a command's arguments, status and output. The CLI prints the earlier errors, then the internal error and `This is a bug in pts, not in the program. Please report it with the program; PTS_DEBUG=1 adds the details a report needs.`; with `PTS_DEBUG=1`, the Go stack of the panic or the C compiler's command line and output follow. It exits with 3, a code of its own (D-006 had 0, 1 and 2, and a Go panic exited with 2, the usage error's). `pts test` reports a test file the compiler failed on as one that didn't compile, with the same message, and exits with 1 as for any file that didn't compile (D-171); its concurrent builds no longer die with one file's panic; nor does the test binary that builds every run test in process. The language server shows an internal error of its checks at its node when that is in the file, with its message and `(a bug in pts)`. `diagnostic.WithInjectedFailure` (a context value) makes the check pass or the lowering panic at chosen nodes, on entering them or (phase `after …`) on leaving them once their children are done, which tests that each node's position is restored; nothing else sets it. `pts/tests`' `TestCompilerFailsCleanly` compiles every test, corpus and docs program and one fixed-seed mutant of each (`PTS_SMOKE_MUTANTS` for more, `PTS_SMOKE_CLANG=1` to check the C with clang) and fails on an internal error, a panic or a hang.
  - **Assignment values (C26).** The value of `x = value` (and of `x op= value`), where the program uses it, is the value the store gives, narrowed to tsc's type of the assignment, value's, as a read of the place is (`assignmentValue` through `narrowed`): a union-typed place's (a local, field or element of `string[] | undefined`) and a dynamic place's (an `any` object's property, a keyed element, D-089, a recursive union's entry) are narrower to tsc than the place, and code using the value went by tsc's type (`.length` of `(lists[k] = ["z"])` was an array operation on a `pts_value` or a union: a C emitter panic or C that clang rejected). An array or map whose tsc type isn't the place's or a narrowing of it (`[]` is a `never[]`, `new Map()` a `Map<any, any>`) is the place's type, without null and undefined when value can't be either, as the value of `??=` is (D-176's helper, now `assignedValue`/`assignedValueType`, with `storesNonNullish` covering `=`); code using an array or a map goes by its IR type, and an object type keeps tsc's (its layout matters to the code using it). A place whose type mentions an erased type parameter (D-078) keeps the value dynamic, since it holds what the caller passed (narrowing to `[]`'s `any[]` would copy a caller's `number[]`); so does the value of `=` on an `any`'s property (`storedDynamic`), whose members are then found at run time (`(o.m = new Map()).size`, below).
  - **Narrowed dynamic values (C39, C66).** A typed value held as a dynamic value, of a union with a type only dynamic values hold (a generator, a promise: `Promise<number> | [number]`, `[string, number] | Generator<number>`), narrowed to an array, tuple, record, map or set type, is converted with a check where it is read (`narrowedValue` through `dynamicConversion`), which keeps the program's own object as it is. It stayed a `pts_value`, which typed code took for a tuple or array: C that clang rejected, or a compiler panic. A value kept dynamic (`storedDynamic`: declared `any` or `unknown`, a member of one, what one's methods return, the value of `=` on one, `x!` of one) that a type guard, `asserts` or `instanceof` narrows to an object type a conversion doesn't just check (an array, tuple, record, map, set or interface; not a class, which a check converts as it is, or a function type) stays the dynamic value instead (`isNarrowedDynamic`): its members are read, written and called as a dynamic value's are (property and element reads and method calls, whose values are then narrowed to tsc's type, `t[0]` of a `[string, number]` a string, `u.slice(1)` dynamic again; writes; `Object.keys`; array iteration), and `isArrayExpression` and `isRecordExpression` don't take it for an array or record. An element past the end or a missing key reads as undefined where undefined can go (`console.log`, `=== undefined`, `??`, a context that takes it), and is checked against the member's type elsewhere, as typed elements are (D-015), though with the conversion's `TypeError: Expected number, got undefined` rather than D-015's RangeError and hint (C72, H22). Members are checked against tsc's types as converting dynamic data is, numeric types included (a guard-narrowed `{ alpha: u8 }` from JSON holding 300 throws, as a typed conversion of it does; the base truncated it). So writes through the narrowed name reach the value, identity holds and each read is a lookup, as in JS, and as the base did for method calls; reads of `length`, elements and records' entries panicked the compiler there. A conversion at each read, which an intermediate version of this slice made, lost writes and made a loop over a narrowed array quadratic (review finding 1); the next one gave method calls' results as raw dynamic values that code using them took for arrays or strings (review round 2, R1), and checked reads past the end strictly (R2). `Array.isArray` of an `any` keeps D-089's `any[]`, the array itself; a map or set of `any`s stays dynamic (`isDynamicCollection`). Converting the narrowed value to a typed place (a typed local or parameter) copies dynamic data, as converting it always does (D-071). `delete` of a dynamic value's property, an `any`'s or a narrowed one's, is PTS1001 (there is no dynamic delete), not D-L10's PTS1002 for fixed layouts.
  - **Well-known-symbol accessors of object literals (C34).** An object literal's getter or setter named `[Symbol.iterator]` or `[Symbol.asyncIterator]` is the accessor of its `@@iterator`/`@@asyncIterator` property (`literalMemberName`; `accessorFields` and `accessorsKey` by `propertyName`), as such methods are (D-111); the lowering looked the field up by tsc's internal name (`__@iterator@NN`) and panicked. The runtime opening an iteration through `any` reads `@@iterator` once, as GetMethod does (`pts_iterator_method`, which replaces `pts_value_has_iterator_method`), and calls the function it read; it read it up to three times (a getter ran three times). `@@asyncIterator` likewise. Writing `o[Symbol.iterator]` stays PTS1001, as reading it is (D-111).
  - **Object rest, spreads and patterns (C50).** `spreadNames` tells an object without properties (none to copy) from one that can't be spread (reported); an empty list was taken for the latter, so `const { a, ...rest } = i` of an interface with only `a` was dropped without a diagnostic (no locals: C that clang rejected at module scope, a PTS1001 on `rest` in a function), and so were spreads and `Object.assign` of such objects. A binding pattern that fails without a diagnostic is reported as an unexplained failure, as an expression is, never dropped. Object rest in a destructuring assignment (`({ a, ...rest } = o)`) assigns the rest's target, the spread's expression (`restTarget`; it panicked). As before, a rest of an interface's value copies the properties of its static type (D-058, SPEC §8).
  - **More panics found on the way.** An object literal with a getter alone and one with a getter and a setter of the same property had one shape (`accessorsKey` didn't tell them apart), so the second's setter had no field; and an interface that only such literals' types give took one of their shapes for its copies of dynamic data (`copyShape`), whose fields are the accessors' closures, not values (a nil field in its order: a C emitter panic; now another type's shape, or PTS1001). A method of an interface or object type, or a generic method's erased signature, with a rest parameter of a tuple type (`log(...args: [string, number])`) takes the tuple's elements, one parameter each, as a function's does (`methodParamTypes`, shared by both); any other rest type is PTS1001 instead of a type assertion. An `@@iterator` getter that gives no function makes iterating a TypeError after one read (`pts_iterator_method` returns the value it read).
- **Reason:** H04's acceptance: a clean, source-located internal compiler error at the CLI boundary that swallows nothing, and the panics and clang rejections the reviews found fixed (C26, C34, C39, C50: compiles and runs, or an honest diagnostic). A panic value that carries its position (re-panicked by the phase's `Locate`, returned by the driver's `Contain`) keeps the phases' signatures, which tests and the language server call, as they were. A separate exit code lets scripts and `pts test` tell a compiler bug from an error in the program. Narrowing assignment values and dynamic values is what reads of narrowed places already do (D-089, D-140, D-176); the value of `x = v` is v's in JS and in tsc. The reproducer's mutation harness found nothing new on the base by itself, but it compiles every program the suite has, with mutants that send typed code down dynamic paths, so a later panic shows as one failing test rather than as a dead test binary.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Returning errors from every phase (changes `check.Program`, `lower.Program` and `cgen.Emit` and all their callers for no gain over the located panic value). Recovering in the CLI only (no position). An upstream edit to recover panics on typescript-go's parser and checker goroutines (CLAUDE.md's minimal upstream edits; no such panic is known). Exit code 2 for internal errors (the usage error's). Rejecting `=`'s value on union and dynamic places with PTS1001 (valid TypeScript that the narrowing handles). Narrowing dynamic values to `any[]` instead of the typed array (the program's own `string[]` in a union isn't an `any[]`: a copy, or a failed check). Converting a value declared `any` or `unknown` to the type a guard narrowed it to at each read (writes lost, identity broken, a loop over it quadratic: review finding 1; the base dispatched its methods dynamically and matched Node). Narrowing `=`'s value to the place's member for object types (code using it goes by tsc's type, the right side's: C that clang rejected for an object literal assigned to an `Interface | undefined`).
- **Known gaps:** a panic on one of typescript-go's own worker goroutines (its parser and checker run in parallel) still ends `pts` with Go's trace and exit code 2, since those goroutines don't recover; a `pts` whose runtime sources (`PTS_HOME`) are from another version can make C that doesn't compile, which is reported as an internal error (nothing checks that the two match); an internal error in the C emitter's preparation of declarations, before it writes a function, has no position; a narrowed dynamic value converted to a typed place is a copy (D-071; pinned by `run/narrowed_dynamic_data_copies.pts`), and so are its elements as the typed parameters of a callback passed to one of its methods (`raw.forEach((u) => u.tags.push("x"))` on `raw` narrowed to `User[]`); adding a property to an object literal narrowed to a record throws, as writing one through `any` does (D-158); `Array.isArray` of an `any` or `unknown` holding the program's own typed array converts it to D-089's `any[]`, a copy, so writes through the narrowed name are lost there (pre-existing; JSON data is kept); a type that `in` narrows to an intersection (`"tags" in e`) stays PTS1001; the value of `x = value` where tsc's type of value is an object type that isn't one of the place's union's members (an object literal assigned to an `Interface | undefined`) stays PTS1001 as on the base; writing a property named by a well-known symbol (`o[Symbol.iterator] = f`) is PTS1001; an erased generic method returning its tuple rest parameter as `T[]` (`pair<T>(...items: [T, T]): T[] { return items; }` through an interface) throws `TypeError: Expected number[], got Array` at the typed call (it panicked; the tuple isn't copied into an array in erased code, H09).

### D-180 · flat and flatMap flatten what is an array at run time into tsc's result type; odd literal depths are PTS1001; new arrays take their context's union array type (H05)
- **Date:** 2026-10-17
- **Decision:**
  - **Flattening (C05).** `flat(depth)` and `flatMap` follow JS's FlattenIntoArray. An element that is an array or a tuple at run time (its descriptor's kind, which is JS's IsArray: not a typed array, a string or another object) is spread while the depth lasts, whatever slot holds it: a union's or `any`'s dynamic value, an object pointer, a tuple's field. Holes (zero words in slots of strings, objects and dynamic values, D-085) are skipped at every level; a group that took no part in a match is an undefined element of exec's and split's arrays (D-133), not a hole, so it is kept, and a `string[]` result holding one or more is a copy of a match's (`pts_type_match_copy`), whose zero words are undefined. Every other element, a leaf, is stored as the result's element type holds it: as it is from slots of that type and kind, else converted from its own slot's kind through a dynamic value and, unless its slot's declared type implies the result's element type, checked against that type (`pts_value_convert` with the result descriptor's element check, D-072); a leaf of another type throws `TypeError: Expected Point[], got Point` instead of being stored, and one the element kind has no representation for is never reinterpreted. A boxed 64-bit integer stored where a number goes (a `number[]`, or a union without a 64-bit integer type) converts to the nearest number, as any integer does (D-012); a union whose element type has `number` kept it boxed, which `typeof` took for a number and `+` for NaN. Dynamic data stored where an interface goes is copied into the interface's shape, as every conversion of dynamic data to an object type is (D-071): the result's element type is readied for dynamic values (`prepareDynamicTarget`), which before needed another conversion in the program. A leaf found to be of an object type as it is records the pair of the check and its descriptor on its thread, so the leaves of one class aren't each checked structurally (callgrind: 200,000 records of a six-property interface through `flatMap`, 1,275M → 755M instructions). The C emitter sets `declaredTypes` for any program that flattens into an array of objects or of unions (`ArrayFlat`, or flatMap's step `ArrayPushFlat`) or calls pts:parallel's `pts_parallel_push_flat`, so such results always carry their element check. The checks are needed because tsc's types for the result can be wrong: `flatMap<number[]>((x) => [x])` and `.par.flatMap<Point[]>(() => points)` hold numbers and Points in JS, which typed code would read as arrays (a segfault, found in this slice's first version). One runtime walk (`array.c`: `slots_of`, `flatten_element`, `flatten_into`, with bulk copies where a source's elements are the result's as they are) serves typed and dynamic `flat`, and typed, dynamic and `.par` `flatMap` (`pts_array_push_flat`: a callback value's elements one level down, else the value). The result has tsc's type for the call (`FlatArray<A, D>[]` as tsc resolves it, `flatMap<U>`'s `U[]`, without the undefined an optional chain adds to `xs?.flat()`), not a type the lowering derived by peeling array layers, which stopped at the first union, tuple or `any`: union, tuple and heterogeneous arrays came back unflattened, and typed code using the result failed its checks. The depth is evaluated at run time (ToNumber, then ToIntegerOrInfinity: NaN is 0, a negative depth flattens nothing; 1 when left out or `undefined`), so a depth in a `const`, an enum member, an `as const` property, a `number` variable, an optional parameter (`depth?: number`) or an `any` works, and the PTS1001 for a depth that isn't a constant is gone; one that isn't a number is converted by the runtime after it reads the array's length, as JS's flat does (`pts_array_flat_value`), so a `valueOf` that changes the array doesn't change what is flattened. A depth that tsc types as a negative (other than `-0`) or fractional number literal, or a union with one, is PTS1001, with the depth JS uses in the message: tsc's FlatArray types `cube.flat(-1)` as an array of cube's own type and `cube.flat(1.5)` as a `number[]`, where JS copies cube and flattens one level, so typed code would read the elements as what they aren't. The base compiled an enum member or literal of -1 and printed the right copy where the result went to `any`; that is now this diagnostic. Likewise a `flatMap` callback whose result type has an array or tuple member with an element type that isn't assignable to U (`flatMap<[number, string]>((x) => [x, "a"])`, `flatMap<number[]>((x) => [x])`) is PTS1001: JS spreads it, and tsc types the result as if it didn't (the base gave such results unflattened, wrong). And a typed leaf that would be copied into the result's element type, which tsc allows, is PTS1001 with the message the same conversion gets elsewhere (`keepsLeaves`, a check on types that adds nothing to the program but the D-066 registration below): a typed object where an interface goes whose members it doesn't provide as they are (`flatMap<J>(() => cs)` with a `C[]` whose `x: number` isn't J's `x: number | string`, as `const js: J[] = [c]` is), or where a union of interfaces goes none of which it provides; an instance where a class goes that it isn't one of; a tuple where a tuple of another layout goes; or a typed array that may be kept elsewhere where an array of other elements goes (D-094). Each member of a union-typed leaf is checked; an object that an interface holds only because its missing properties are optional is registered for D-066's check against subclasses that hold them as other types, as `objectCast` registers it. The arrays of a map the callback returns whose own callback returns array literals (`ns.map((m) => [m])`) are new at each call and may be copied; a `const`'s arrays, even one used once, are the same at each call, so they are kept arrays (`r[0] === r[2]` holds in JS). A program object where a union of iterator results goes is held as it is, as iterators hand results back (D-178). A generic function passed as the callback (`xs.flatMap(dup)`) is left to the runtime's checks, its result type being its type parameters' until the call instantiates it. JS stores those objects as they are; the base copied such a C silently, and the slice's round-2 version threw a TypeError at run time. Functions of another function type are adapted (D-075), and numbers and dynamic data converted, at run time. An annotated callback's values, and a named function's, are checked by its result type, since its returns convert to it where they are; otherwise the elements of a returned array literal are checked one by one (each branch of a conditional element as an element, a nested array literal's elements against the array or tuple type its context gives it, an object literal made as its context's type), and other values by their types' elements. (A first version probed the conversions themselves, which made adapters for function leaves that were then dropped, PTS9001, and rejected annotated callbacks returning new arrays of narrower objects: review round 3; review round 4 found the union, tuple, class, nested-literal and generic cases, round 5 the iterator-result, conditional-element, nested-tuple and kept-const ones.) `flat` on a tuple receiver flattens the tuple's elements, as the other methods that don't change an array do (D-089). `flatMap` visits the elements the array has when it starts (its length read once) and skips holes, through `any` too, where the callback was called with undefined at holes and the holes of its arrays became undefined; a typed callback over a match array is called at an undefined group, and throws there as `map`'s does (D-133).
  - **`.par.flatMap` (C20).** pts:parallel's `flatMap` skips holes and flattens each callback value through the same runtime step (the intrinsics `pts_parallel_has` and `pts_parallel_push_flat`), so it gives Array's result; it read every index (RangeError at a hole) and spread the callback's arrays holes and all. Its callback converts to its parameter's type `(…) => U | U[]`. An unannotated arrow or function expression whose own result has an array or tuple type that its context's union lacks (`(x) => (x === 1 ? x : [[x]])`, a `number | number[][]`, for `U = number | number[]`) now takes the context's union as its result type, so each array it returns converts where it is made: a new array, copied into the union's member that holds its elements (D-094). Before, an adapter converted the result after the call, which can't copy an array (identity), so the call was PTS1001. Callbacks of Array's own methods keep their own types, since their helpers call them as they are (D-042). A conditional with an array or tuple literal branch takes its contextual union, as one with `[]` did (D-172), when a member of that union holds the literal as it is made; a literal no member holds so (an array of arrays of other element types, `[[x, true]]` for a `(number | boolean | string)[]` member) keeps tsc's type for the conditional, as on the base. An array converted to a union with several array members goes to the member whose element type holds its elements as they are (its element type, a member of it, or a union of its members), and a tuple of another layout than the union's tuples goes to the array member that holds its elements, copied where new. A `.par.flatMap` callback declared to return a tuple, returning a tuple or array it didn't make whose type isn't the result's (`pairs.par.flatMap((p) => p)`, an existing `number[]` for a `.par.flatMap<number | string>`), or a named function whose result type has such an array (`function nested(x: number): number | number[][]` for `.par.flatMap<number | number[]>`), stays PTS1001 with D-094's message or `converting …`; `(p) => [...p]` works. A `.par.flatMap` callback that returns an array whose elements aren't of U throws the leaf check's TypeError (library code isn't checked like Array's flatMap at compile time). Its pieces are joined by the same runtime step (`pts_parallel_push_flat` of each piece onto the first), not by `push(...piece)`, which turns a match's undefined groups into holes (C78), so the result is the same at every thread count.
  - **Messages.** An IR array of a union prints as `(A | B)[]` in messages and in runtime type names: C20's message read `number | number | number[][] | number[]` for `number | (number | number[])[] | number[]`.
- **Reason:** H05's acceptance: Node's results for union, tuple and heterogeneous arrays on the typed and dynamic paths, at each depth, with holes, and `.par.flatMap` accepting the type arguments Array's flatMap does. Deciding by descriptors at run time is what JS's IsArray does, and the only way elements of union, `any` and `unknown` types can be right. tsc's type for the result is the type every use of it is lowered against, so the result must have it. The declared-type check keeps a flatten that tsc's types don't describe from storing an element typed code would misread (a kind check alone let a Point stand for a Point[]); slots whose declared type implies the result's skip it, so homogeneous arrays cost less than on the base (callgrind: `Point[][]` `flat` 233M → 127M instructions, `number[][]` 130M → 75M, `flatMap` of pairs 710M → 660M). Keeping tsc's type for a conditional whose literal no member holds avoids a PTS1001 where the base compiled. Typed `flatMap` over numbers and booleans, which have no holes, tests indices against the length as before (callgrind: `flatMap` of pairs over a `number[]`, 710M on the base → 622M).
- **Alternatives rejected:** Generated flattening helpers per array type (union and `any` levels need the run-time test anyway; one walk is simpler and as fast for nested arrays). Computing the result type in the lowering (tsc's FlatArray resolves it, and uses of the result are typed by tsc's type). Supporting negative and fractional literal depths, or flatMap callbacks returning arrays of other elements than U, with a type the lowering computes (the call's type would differ from tsc's at every use of the result). Checking leaves only by kind (memory-unsafe for objects, above) or checking every leaf (a full check per element of a homogeneous array). Storing undefined groups in a `string[]` as holes (they would be skipped or print as empty items, where JS has undefined). Copying `.par.flatMap` callbacks' tuple results in an adapter (a copy of an array the caller may keep, against D-094), or a pts:parallel signature typed by the callback's own result (an explicit type argument would then mean something other than Array's `flatMap<U>`).
- **Known gaps:** number, boolean and integer arrays have no holes (D-085), so `new Array<number>(3)` flattens as zeros; tuples held as `any` or through a union with arrays have no array methods (`a.flat is not a function`, `v.push is not a function`, C75, H29); `map`, `forEach`, `filter` and the other callback methods still throw RangeError at the holes of string, object and dynamic arrays, where JS skips them (C74, H06); arrays can't be held as interface or `ArrayLike` values (C76, H29); the `.par.flatMap` callbacks above (C73, H29); a leaf converted from dynamic data to an object type is a copy, as every such conversion is (D-071); a union result can't hold a match's undefined group unless the union has undefined (a TypeError, where JS stores undefined); nesting deeper than Node's stack allows flattens where Node throws RangeError; whether a conditional's array literal branch fits its context's union is decided by lowering that union, which can add a PTS1001 before the error of a program that fails anyway (`[1, 2].concat(c ? [3] : 4)`), and a `flatMap` callback whose value doesn't convert gets a second PTS1001 at the call (`flatMap<{ c: J }>(() => ({ c: cobj }))`); converting a `(boolean | i64)[]` to a `(number | boolean)[]` keeps its 64-bit integers boxed (pre-existing, C77, H22); `push(...m)` and other spreads of a match array into a `string[]` make its undefined groups holes (pre-existing, C78, H06); an interface leaf where a class goes is checked at run time only (a TypeError unless it is an instance, as on the base); a relaxed cast (D-066) from the result's interface to another doesn't see the classes flatMap stored in it, so a subclass holding a property as another type fails at the read, with a TypeError (memory-safe; the regular conversion reports it); a nested array literal where a union with two array members goes isn't checked (copied as on the base); a tuple leaf where an array goes is PTS1001 even when it is new, since the runtime's conversion makes arrays of arrays only (the base kept such tuples in a result of the callback's own type); copies the regular conversions make where D-094 allows a copy (a new array's elements, spreads) lose identity as they do elsewhere.

### D-181 · flatMap's leaf check follows where a callback's values are made; arrays shorter by optional elements convert to tuples (H05 follow-up)
- **Date:** 2026-10-17
- **Decision:**
  - **Why a follow-up.** H05's fix commit `c158778d4` (D-180) included round 5's fixes without another review. A confirmation round (6) after the commit found silent identity loss and false PTS1001s in the compile-time leaf check, and rounds 7–12 reviewed each rework (`docs/agent/hardening-evidence/h05-review.md`). This decision replaces D-180's description of that check; D-180's runtime flattening, depths, holes and `.par.flatMap` stand.
  - **Leaves by where they are made.** `keepsLeaves` reads the values an unannotated callback returns (`keepsLeavesOf(value, elem, made, fn)`): the elements of a returned array literal; of `xs.map(f)` and `Array.from(xs, f)` with a function, the values `f` returns; of `sort`, `reverse`, `filter`, `slice`, `toSorted` and `toReversed`, the receiver's (when the call's type is the receiver's, not a type guard's); of `concat`, the receiver's and the arguments'; of `Array.of`, its arguments; of a nested `flatMap` with a function, the leaves of its values; of a literal's `flat()`, its elements' leaves; of a const the callback makes at each call and doesn't annotate, its initializer's (an annotated one's elements are converted to its type where it is declared, so its type is checked; an `any[]` or `unknown[]` annotation holds them as they are, and flatMap's conversion of dynamic data copies them, D-071, a known gap below). Each leaf is checked as a value stored where the result's element type goes (`keepsValue`): a literal the callback returns directly, and the literals nested in it, are made as that type (`made`); a literal reached through a mapper, a const or a cast is made as its own type (`typeAt`, which reflects casts and annotations); a new array or tuple of another type is copied, so its elements are checked the same way; a tuple where an array goes is PTS1001 (the runtime converts arrays to arrays only), new or not, with a plain "using a `[…]` as a `…[]`" message; a spread's leaves are an array's or a tuple's elements, or an iterable's values, of the literal's element type; nested tuple literals are checked by position, a spread of a tuple moving the later elements (after a spread of an array, or of a tuple with optional elements, a later element is checked against every field it may reach).
  - **Consts new at each call.** Inside flatMap's check a const counts as a new array only if the callback (or the mapper) declares it, uses it once, and no loop or nested function or class between the declaration and the use runs it again (a for statement's initializer, a for-in or for-of statement's expression and a use under `return` aren't repeated): a const of the enclosing code is the same array at each call, even if the callback is its only use. D-094's regular conversions keep the source-use rule (narrowing it broke static initializers, for-of heads and callbacks' returned arrays that flatMap spreads; round 7).
  - **Object literals.** A literal reached through a mapper, a const or a cast is made as the type its own context gives it: a hash-map object type if its contextual type, its context's one hash-map member or its own type is one (`recordContext`, which `objectLiteral` uses too, so the check sees the type the literal is made as; round 11), else `literalType`'s. If that is the result's element type, or a member of it, it is that object. A new literal going where a hash-map object goes (the element type, or a union's one hash-map member when no member holds it as it is) is copied into one, so each of its values is checked where the hash map's values go, unless its type has optional properties or it has a spread, a method or an accessor (checked by its type then). Any other is checked by its type, as on the commit: rounds 7–10 approved copies of fresh literals into interfaces property by property, and each round found a copy the shape doesn't survive as it is (extra, misordered or left-out optional properties, hash maps, maps and sets, a copy-shape order taken from a Go map), so that approval was reverted.
  - **Hash maps, maps and sets.** A leaf of a hash-map object, map or set type where a hash-map object, map or set of other types goes is PTS1001 (`converting Record<string, number> to Record<string, number | string>`), as is one where a union goes none of whose members holds it as it is (`Record<string, number | string> | null`, `Map<string, number | string> | undefined`; round 11), and an object of another type where a hash-map object goes (`using a {a: number} as a hash-map object`): the runtime copied a hash map or an object into one silently and threw a TypeError for maps and sets.
  - **Single-use consts (D-094).** A shorthand property (`{ a }`) and an export specifier (`export { a }`) are uses of the const they name, and a shorthand's own default (`{ a = ns } = o`) is a use of its default's const; an exported const isn't single-use (its other uses may be in other files). Each was copied silently before.
  - **Optional tuple elements.** The runtime converts an array or a tuple shorter than a tuple type by its optional elements to it, leaving them out (D-100): flatMap's leaves (`flatMap<[number, string?]>((i) => [[i], [i, "s"]])`), dynamic data and map results threw `TypeError: Expected [number, string | undefined], got Array`. Array sources no longer compute `&fields[i]` on a NULL fields pointer (C60's `convert_tuple` report).
- **Reason:** H05's acceptance (results as JS's, never a value its type doesn't hold) and D-094's rule that a copy is allowed only where no one can tell it from the array. Each rule follows where a value is made: a type alone can't tell a new array from a kept one, and the commit's "made arrays pass" let `[cs.slice()]` copy the Cs. Time-boxing the object-literal approval (four rounds, each with new holes, then reverted) follows CLAUDE.md's rabbit-hole rule; the remaining false PTS1001s are honest, and most have a workaround (annotate the mapper or the callback; not a function's result or a `Record<string, any>`). Round 12, the time-boxed last round, found no regression against the commit; its findings are recorded below and in the ledger.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Narrowing D-094's single-use rule for all conversions (round 6's first fix; false PTS1001s on common code). Treating new arrays' elements as new (the commit; silent identity loss). Approving copies of fresh object literals into interfaces property by property (rounds 7–10; the copy shape loses extra, misordered and left-out properties and copies hash maps, and its order comes from a Go map). Making the runtime's copies keep extra properties and absent optional ones (a change to D-071/D-094's copies for every conversion, beyond H05; ledger rows C80–C83). Checking a hash-map literal by its own type (round 10's version; false PTS1001s for literals with computed or numeric keys made as the target, through every workaround).
- **Known gaps:** D-180's, but for the ones this decision fixes (map-made tuples' elements, nested tuple literals under `as const`/`satisfies`, spreads in nested literals); a fresh object literal with narrower property types reached through a mapper, a const or a cast is PTS1001 where an interface goes (C84; `xs.map((n) => ({ name, size: n }))` into `flatMap<Named>`; annotate the mapper or the callback); a hash-map literal reached through a const used more than once, a function's result or a `Record<string, any>` is checked by its type (PTS1001, as D-094's regular rule has it); `.flat(depth)` with an argument or on another receiver than a literal, an iterable's spread of new arrays (`[...new Set([[1]])]`) and an annotated callback's values are checked by type (possible false PTS1001); after an inexact spread in a nested tuple literal, a later element is checked against every later field (possible false PTS1001); a union of two array or two tuple members isn't checked for nested literals (copied as on the base); a shorthand property whose value is a new single-use const still goes through D-094's regular rule (PTS1001 for `return { arr }` into a wider interface, as on the commit); D-094's regular conversions keep their pre-existing gaps: a new array's elements are converted when it is copied, now also tuples shorter by optional elements (C80), a const used once in a loop or a function that runs more than once is copied at each pass (C81), copies into an interface take its copy shape, dropping extra properties, reordering and making left-out optional properties undefined (C82), a literal fitting two union members is made as its own type (C83), and an interface's copy shape is taken from whichever structurally identical type a Go map yields first, so twins declared in other orders copy dynamic data in an order that can change between builds (C85). From review round 12, each the same on the commit unless noted: an annotated const's kept elements are copied silently where the annotation converts them (C80) or, for an `any[]` annotation, where flatMap converts dynamic data (D-071); a hash-map object, map or array leaf where an interface, a class or a numeric-key hash map goes isn't reported by flatMap's check, so it is copied silently or throws a TypeError at run time, where the regular conversion reports it (C86); a new literal made as an alias type through a cast or an annotated const, or with a spread, going where a hash-map object goes is PTS1001 (the commit ran it as Node does; annotate the mapper); a tuple literal shorter by optional elements in a conditional element is PTS1001 (C87); flatMap's hash-map message lacks the regular conversion's hint (`{ ...o }`).

### D-182 · Sort follows SortCompare on every path; the copying methods read holes as undefined where the elements may be; `.par.sort` sorts a copy (H06a); H06 split
- **Date:** 2026-10-17
- **Decision:**
  - **Split.** H06 is split into H06a (C06: `sort`, `toSorted` and `.par.sort`, with and without a comparator, typed and through `any`; the copying methods, whose copy `toSorted` shares; an undefined comparator, C95) and H06b (the audit's other built-ins at holes: C74, C78, C88–C92). The reproducer's generated matrix (240 programs, 17,748 cases against Node) found more than one session can fix and review; H06b's RED tests are committed as skipped known issues, and the matrix is retained for it.
  - **Sort.** `sort()` and `sort(compare)` follow SortIndexedProperties and SortCompare: the elements other than undefined and holes are sorted stably, without a comparator by their strings (ToString, so `null` sorts as `"null"`; this supersedes D-039's "holes `""`" for sort), with one by its numbers; then come the undefineds, then the holes, which `sort` leaves as holes. A comparator is never called with undefined or a hole. A match array's group that took no part (a zero string slot, undefined to JS, D-133) goes last and stays undefined. Each slot is read once; the result is written to the array's buffer as it is after the comparator's or the elements' toString calls, as far as the array reaches. A frozen array throws at index 0 after the comparisons, as JS's first write does, unless it has fewer than two slots (V8 returns at once) or holes only (JS only deletes absent properties).
  - **Copying methods.** `toSorted`, `toReversed`, `toSpliced` and `with` read every index (JS's Get), so a hole becomes undefined in the copy of an array of dynamic values (any union, `(string | null)[]` included, `any[]`, `unknown[]`): `pts_array_copy_as_read`, typed and through `any`. A copy of a `string[]` or of an array of one object type keeps the hole, since its element type can't hold undefined (by design, extending D-085): it prints as `<1 empty item>` where Node prints undefined, reads as undefined where any value goes and throws RangeError where an element is required. `toSorted` and `toSpliced` evaluate their arguments before copying, as JS's methods do, and a typed array's `sort(compare)` (its elements sorted as a `number[]` and written back) evaluates compare before it copies them.
  - **An undefined comparator** is the default order: decided by the argument's lowered type, so `sort(undefined)`, `(undefined)`, `void 0` and a const holding undefined are the default order (after the argument is evaluated), a parameter named `undefined` is a comparator, and a comparator whose type is a union with undefined (`compare?: …`) or a dynamic value (`any`, `Map.get`) is tested at run time after the receiver and the comparator are evaluated. Typed arrays use their own (numeric) default order there. A dynamic comparator that is neither undefined nor a function throws the checked conversion's TypeError (`Expected (number, number) => number, got null`), where V8 says `The comparison function must be either a function or undefined`.
  - **`.par.sort`** is Array's sort on one thread. On more, it sorts a copy: the elements it compares are gathered at the copy's front (`pts_array_gather_sortable`; arrays of numbers, booleans and the integer kinds, which hold neither, skip the pass), only those are sorted (the merge calls the comparator itself, so it never sees undefined or a hole), and the copy is written back once (`pts_array_write_sorted`, with sort's frozen rule). A comparator or toString that throws leaves the array as it was, and a comparator that reads the array sees it as it was, at every thread count; before H06a an array longer than one piece was written back piece by piece.
- **Reason:** C06's acceptance (SortCompare's order, no undefined passed to a comparator, toSorted without holes, the dynamic and parallel paths) with one placement rule in the runtime for the typed, dynamic, copying and parallel paths, so `.par.sort` equals `sort` at every thread count. Review round 1 found the first version's in-place gather observable (F2) and its undefined-comparator lowering wrong for typed arrays (F1); sorting a private copy gives JS's all-or-nothing write-back.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Giving copies of `string[]` and object arrays with holes a "zero slot is undefined" twin descriptor, as match copies have for strings (D-133), so they print as Node does: each array descriptor (one per array type, with its element checks) would need a twin, and code reading such arrays a second representation of undefined, for programs that leave `new Array<T>(n)` partly unfilled and then copy it (REVIEW_LATER). Throwing when such a copy meets a hole (breaks programs that never read those slots). Gathering in place before `.par.sort`'s comparisons (H06a's first version: changes the array before a comparator that throws or reads it). Filtering undefined out in the library with a typed `filter` (C74's RangeError at holes, and two passes). Deciding an undefined comparator by the identifier's text (`isUndefined`; a parameter named `undefined` was taken for the value).
- **Known gaps:** H06b's rows (C74, C78, C88–C92); D-094's conversion copies make holes undefined (C93, H22); `copyWithin` (C94, H29); the RangeError's "out of range" wording for a hole inside the length (C97, H22); a comparator that shortens the array gets the sorted elements written back only as far as the array now reaches, where JS's writes lengthen it again; a dynamic comparator that isn't a function throws the checked conversion's message rather than V8's; the copy and its write-back make `.par.sort(compare)` of 1M numbers about 12% slower at 4 threads than before H06a (median 75 vs 67 ms over five interleaved runs; with 10% undefined about 5%), while the default order is about 13% faster (one thread is Array's sort, 2× faster than before) (`h06.md`); the other text-based `undefined` tests of the lowering (a parameter named `undefined` taken for a left-out operand of `slice`, `toSpliced` and others, C98, H29).

### D-183 · The array built-ins at holes: the callback methods skip them, reads give undefined where the element type holds it, own keys leave them out; copies of unions without undefined keep them (H06b)
- **Date:** 2026-10-17
- **Decision:**
  - **What decides.** Where JS reads an index with Get (iteration, find and its kin, includes, the copying methods, spreads, `Array.from`, `entries()`, `new Set(array)`), a hole is undefined only if the array's static element type holds undefined: `any` or `unknown`, `undefined`, or a union with undefined (`ir.HoldsUndefined`). Through `any`, the runtime asks the descriptor's check (an array of dynamic values that the program doesn't describe is an `any[]`). Elsewhere a copy keeps the hole and a typed read of it throws RangeError (D-085). This supersedes D-182's "any union, `(string | null)[]` included": H06a's copies of a `(string | null)[]` held undefined, which typed code narrowed by `x !== null` to a string and dereferenced (SIGSEGV, C99). A `(string | null)[]` is now treated like a `string[]`.
  - **The callback methods** (C74; typed, through `any` and in pts:parallel's views): `forEach`, `map`, `filter`, `some`, `every`, `reduce`, `reduceRight` and `flatMap` read the length once and visit the indices below it that the array still has (HasProperty), skipping holes. Through `any`, they used to read the length at each step, so a `forEach` that pushed never ended (C101).
    - `map`'s result has the starting length, with holes where the array had them or where a callback cut it short. A number or boolean result has 0 or false there (D-085).
    - `reduce` without an initial value starts at the first element there is, and throws TypeError if there is none.
    - `find`, `findIndex`, `findLast` and `findLastIndex` visit every index below the starting length (C88). A hole, or an index a callback cut off, is undefined where the element type holds undefined, or where the callback's element parameter takes undefined. Otherwise a hole throws D-085's RangeError.
    - A callback whose element parameter holds the elements as they are and takes undefined reads them as that type: `unknown` or `any`, or a union with undefined that has the element type among its members (`(p: P | undefined) => …` over a `P[]`). Over an array of strings or objects, find, its kin and `Array.from`'s mapping then give it a hole as undefined. Over a `string[]`, every method gives it a match's group that took no part as undefined, and `filter` keeps it as one (D-133; C104).
    - Such a read is the slot's word itself: a slot of pointers holds its element as a dynamic value or such a union holds it (D-002). A parameter that holds the elements only after a conversion gets each element converted, as before H06b, and reads a hole as D-085 says. That covers a wider array, tuple, Map or function type, or a base class. Review round 2 found H06b's second version reinterpreting those, which crashed with SIGSEGV.
    - Where the element must go back to its own type (`find(…)!`, `findLast(…)!`, the first accumulator of `reduce` without an initial value), it is converted through a dynamic value, which throws TypeError for undefined. Review round 1 found that H06b's first version converted it directly, which is PTS1001, so `ps.filter(isDefined)` stopped compiling.
    - `Array.from`'s mapper and `entries()` read every index.
  - **Search** (C89): `includes` reads a hole as undefined; `indexOf` and `lastIndexOf` skip holes. This holds typed and through `any`.
  - **`pop` and `shift`** (C108) remove a hole as they remove an element and give undefined for it where their value may be undefined (the plain calls, typed and through `any`). `pop()!` and `shift()!` still throw D-085's RangeError at a hole.
  - **Own keys** (C90): `Object.keys`, `values` and `entries`, `for…in`, `Object.hasOwn` and `in` leave holes out. A match's undefined group is an element, so it stays in.
  - **Iteration** (C91):
    - Spreads, `Array.from`, `values()`, `entries()` and `for…of` read holes as undefined where the element type holds undefined.
    - A spread of a `string[]` or an object array keeps them (D-182), and its `for…of` throws RangeError at one (D-085; H11's scope).
