    - `new Set(array)` adds undefined for a hole where the set's element type holds undefined. Otherwise it leaves holes out, as D-130 chose: a `Set<string>` can't hold undefined.
  - **`concat`** (C92) keeps holes on both paths. JS's concat defines only the indices that are there. The exception is a receiver whose zero slots read as undefined (a match array or copy, or a `string[]` that took one's descriptor): there they read as undefined (C113).
  - **A match's undefined groups** (C78): spreading, concatenating, pushing or unshifting them into a `string[]` keeps them undefined. A plain `string[]` that receives one takes the descriptor of match copies, `pts_type_match_copy`, whose zero slots are undefined, as flat's result does (D-180). The switch is decided before the elements go in, and only if the receiver has no holes of its own, which would then read as undefined too. It is a compare-and-swap from the plain descriptor, so a freeze racing with it keeps its frozen twin. Through `any`, `filter` keeps the groups.
  - **pts:parallel** (C100): ParallelArray's `map` keeps holes. `forEach`, `filter`, `some`, `every`, `reduce`, `count`, `sum`, `min` and `max` skip them. `find` skips them too: a hole and "none" both give undefined, and `.par.find` gives any match (D-123). `reduce` over an array of pointers folds per-piece results, which may be empty.
  - **Cost.** A write past the end throws (D-038), so an array gains holes only where a spread of another's holes goes into it (`push(...a)`, `unshift(...a)`) or from racing pushes.
    - The methods through `any` that visit every element look for holes once per call (`pts_array_has_holes`), and test each index only if there are some. A hole that a callback's spread adds later is then read as undefined.
    - pts:parallel's methods look in each piece's own slots (`pts_parallel_has_holes_in`), so the look runs in parallel. A hole that racing pushes leave meanwhile is read as typed code reads one (RangeError).
    - The typed callback methods test each index inline (`pts_array_has`), as do the methods that may stop early: `some`, `every`, `find`, and `indexOf`/`lastIndexOf` when searching for undefined.
    - Callgrind instruction counts are in `h06b.md`.
- **Reason:** H06b's acceptance asks that each method visit holes as JS does, against Node, or give an honest diagnostic. One static rule for when a hole may be undefined keeps every typed slot within its type, which H06a's union rule broke (C99).
- **Alternatives rejected:**
  - Reading holes as undefined in every array of dynamic values (the reproducer's DQ4). That puts undefined in a `(string | null)[]`, as C99 did.
  - Deciding from the descriptor in typed code. A program without dynamic code gives its `(string | null)[]` the descriptor of `any[]`.
  - Throwing RangeError at a hole in `new Set(stringArray)` (the reproducer's DQ7). D-130 left holes out for race safety, and a set's missing element is never read.
  - Testing each index in every method. Before the scans this cost about 11% more instructions in pts:parallel's loops and about 8% through `any`.
  - Scanning the whole array before pts:parallel's methods fork. Review round 1 measured it serial: `.par.count` over 2M strings took 11% more instructions and about 11% more time at 4 threads.
  - Scanning for holes in `some`, `every`, `find` and `indexOf`. That is O(n) where they may stop at the first index.
  - Reading widened elements through `pts_array_element` and a checked conversion, which nearly doubled such loops (review round 1).
  - Making a receiver with holes a match copy. Its own holes would then read as undefined (review round 1).
- **Known gaps:**
  - Array patterns read a hole under a default as a required element, and a rest element keeps holes (C102, H11; `run/array_patterns_read_holes.pts`).
  - `Object.values` and `entries` of a match copy throw in the checked copy into tsc's type (C105, H22; `run/match_copy_values_keep_undefined.pts`).
  - Copies, spreads and iteration of a `string[]`, an object array or a union without undefined keep holes or throw where JS reads undefined (C96, C106: by design). A typed `find` over them, whose callback's parameter doesn't take undefined, throws RangeError at a hole.
  - A `string[]` that already has holes keeps them when a match's elements go into it, and the match's undefined group becomes a hole there too, where JS has it undefined. The same holds for typed `strings.concat(m)` and spreads when `strings` has holes (`run/match_groups_into_holes.pts`). A freeze racing with such a push may also leave the groups as holes.
  - An optional parameter (`(p?: P) => …`) isn't widened, so a typed `find` over a `P[]` with holes throws RangeError at one, as before H06b (C111). `(p: P | undefined)` works.
  - A typed `find` over a `string[]` or a number array whose callback shortens the array stops at the array's current length, where JS calls the callback with undefined for the indices cut off (C112).
  - The typed callback methods over arrays of pointers pay about 8 instructions per element for the hole test. map's preallocated result offsets this.
  - pts:parallel's methods over arrays of pointers pay about 4 instructions per element for each piece's scan, and about 3 for the test in the loop. With a trivial callback over 2M strings or objects, that is +7% (`.par.count`) and +8–10% (`.par.reduce`) in instructions. On this host the wall clock of such loops is dominated by code layout (function alignment alone moves it by about ±25%, review round 3), so instruction counts are the measure. A sticky "may have holes" mark on arrays would remove both (H27).
  - Each push of a match with an undefined group into a plain `string[]` scans the receiver for holes of its own. Repeated pushes into a long array that has a hole far from its start are then quadratic: 20,000 pushes into 100,000 slots took 0.9–1.3 s against the base's 5–6 ms (C115, H27).
  - A match array or copy, or a `string[]` that took its descriptor, reads holes that `concat` adds later as undefined, where JS keeps them as holes (C113; `m.concat(holey)` did so on the base too). A spread reads them as undefined in JS too.
  - `delete a[i]`'s PTS1002 message still says arrays have no holes (C107, H22).
  - pts:parallel's views read each element as the array's element type, so a match's undefined group throws D-133's TypeError there even where the callback's parameter takes undefined (C109, H29).
  - D-094's conversion copies (C93, H22).

### D-184 · AbortSignal's constructor takes a key only pts:internal/abort has, not a flag that threads share (H07a); H07 split
- **Date:** 2026-10-17
- **Decision:**
  - **The key.** `new AbortSignal()` stays an illegal constructor for programs, as in Node, but its permission is no longer the module-level `let constructing` that `newSignal()` set around the call (C07). The constructor takes an optional `key` and throws `TypeError: Illegal constructor` unless it is the module's own `SIGNAL_KEY`, an empty object literal that nothing else can reach (pts:internal/abort is reached only as globals). `AbortSignal.abort`, `timeout`, `any` and `AbortController` pass it. Signals made on different threads share no state, so independent controllers work at any thread count, and `new AbortSignal()` always throws (on the base it sometimes succeeded while another thread held the flag).
  - **The key's type is `unknown`.** Any argument then reaches the check and gets Node's TypeError, also one passed through `any`. A key of a private class would make that a failed conversion naming the class (`Expected SignalKey, got Object`). Node's types (`new(): AbortSignal`) reject any argument at compile time; ParallelScript's accept one and throw at run time, as Node does for JavaScript.
  - **The audit** (the reproducer's table in `docs/agent/hardening-evidence/h07-reproducer.md` §C, spot-checked by the parent): the library's other module state is set once at startup or read-only. That covers the static blocks' function slots in pts:internal/abort and pts:http, which run in the module's initialisation from `main` before the program's first statement, the code table, the port and header lists, and the singletons of pts:fs, pts:path, pts:process and pts:assert. The runtime's C state that operations on independent objects touch is thread-local, behind `pthread_once`, under a lock or atomic. Two pieces of shared state remain: a signal's listener list, which the library changes from whichever threads follow a signal the program shares between them, and pts:test's registry, module state that `test` and `describe` change from whichever thread calls them.
  - **H07 split.** H07a is C07, the audit, and the regressions for independent signals: `.par` callbacks, `parallel for`, `parallel` tasks, reusePort handlers and parallel fetches with signals, at 1, 2 and 4 threads. H07b is the shared state the audit found. C116 is one signal followed from many threads (`AbortSignal.any`, and so every `Request` and `fetch` given the signal): it loses listeners, misses aborts and can make `abort()` throw RangeError at a hole that a lost push left. C117 is pts:test's registry, which `test` and `describe` called from `parallel` tasks change at once. Review round 1 added three. C119 is `AbortSignal.any`'s O(n²) cost for following one signal n times, where Node keeps a set of followers. C120 is D-121's known gap, unreferenced timers on a loop that sleeps: a `parallel` task's own unreferenced timer or `AbortSignal.timeout` never fires. C121 is a follower aborting as a listener of its source, out of the DOM standard's order. H07 stays open until both slices are done. C118 (a listener of another function type is a new adapter at each call, so `removeEventListener` can't find it, D-075) joins H29, and the suspicion that ThreadSanitizer is blind to generated code (C122) joins H20.
- **Reason:** C07's acceptance asks for many unrelated controllers at 1, 2 and 4 threads, with reusePort and parallel-fetch coverage, no shared permission state and no serializing of user objects. A key object makes construction stateless at no cost per signal, while Node's illegal constructor stays observable. A signal shared by threads is a different problem, the library's own writes into an object the program shares. It needs its own design under SPEC §4.1, which lets racing pushes on a shared array lose elements, and the acceptance forbids serializing unrelated signals. Fixing it in its own slice keeps both reviewable.
- **Alternatives rejected:**
  - An atomic or thread-local flag: thread-local module state doesn't exist in the language, and an atomic counter still lets `new AbortSignal()` slip through while another thread is constructing.
  - A lock around construction: it serializes unrelated controllers, which the acceptance forbids.
  - Making `AbortSignal`'s constructor public: Node's `new AbortSignal()` throws.
  - A key of a private class: a nominal type for tsc, but its name leaks into the TypeError through `any`, as above.

### D-185 · Signals that threads share: a lock per event target, the DOM standard's dependent signals, one lock for requests' own signals (H07b1); H07b split
- **Date:** 2026-10-17
- **Decision:**
  - **H07b split.** H07b1 is the signal library: C116 (one signal shared by threads), C119 (the cost of following a signal) and C121 (`AbortSignal.any`'s abort order), all in `pts/lib/internal/abort.pts` and the places in `pts/lib/http.pts` that use signals. H07b2 is the rest: C120, the scheduler's unreferenced timers on a loop that sleeps (D-121's known gap, runtime C), and C117, pts:test's registry. They touch different code and are reviewed apart; H07b stays open until both are done.
  - **A lock per event target.** Each `EventTarget` has its own lock (`#lock`, a futex word in a 4-byte ArrayBuffer, with pts:sync's intrinsics `pts_sync_lock` and `pts_sync_unlock`, D-128). It guards the listener list and, for an AbortSignal, `aborted`, the reason, the dependents and the flag that `onabort`'s first handler claims. It is held only while the library reads or writes them, never while a listener runs; a section that calls a function unlocks in a `finally`, so nothing can leave it held. So listeners that threads add to one target at once are all kept, and nothing reads a hole; a signal aborts once, since the check and the store are under the lock; a dispatch calls the listeners there when it begins (a snapshot under the lock); and a `once` listener is taken under the lock, so one dispatch calls it.
  - **`AbortSignal.any` is the DOM standard's dependent signal.** Its sources are the signals given, except that a signal `any` made contributes its own sources instead (flattened, kept in `#sources`), each once. The new signal goes on each source's list of dependents (an array: O(1) per follower, as Node's set), under the source's lock, which also checks whether the source has aborted: a source that aborted on another thread after `any`'s first check aborts the new signal with its reason. **Signal abort**: under the signal's lock the abort is decided, the reason set and the dependents handed over; then each dependent that hasn't aborted takes the same reason under its own lock; then the signal's abort event fires, then the dependents' events, in the order they followed. Locks are never nested. With the order, the reasons are the standard's too: a listener of the source that aborts another source can no longer give a follower of both its reason (the reproducer's matrix).
  - **The library's check-then-act sites** add first, then check. `addEventListener`'s `{ signal }` option adds the signal's listener that removes the new one, then the new listener, and then removes it itself if the signal has aborted meanwhile (`abortedNow`, a read that tsc's narrowing of the first check doesn't cover); fetch's `exchange` adds its abort listener after starting the request and then aborts the request itself if the signal has aborted (`pts_http_fetch_abort` ignores an id that has ended). The lock orders the add against the abort's snapshot of listeners, so either the abort calls the listener or the check that follows sees `aborted`. `exchange`'s listener is typed `EventListener`, so its `removeEventListener` finds it (C118's case in the library).
  - **The `{ signal }` option in Node's order** (review round 1): the signal's listener goes in before the new listener, and also for a listener that is there already. So `signal.addEventListener("abort", l, { signal })` doesn't call `l` (C124), and the signal's abort removes a listener added twice, the second time with the signal (C125); both differed from Node before. As Node's, that listener is used up by its first run (an untrusted `abort` event the program dispatches uses it up) and runs even after an earlier listener stopped the event's propagation (`resists`, a flag only the library sets; review round 2). It is used up by a flag of its own rather than by leaving the signal's list, since an abort taking n of them out one by one costs O(n²) (review round 3: 23 s for 50,000), and it is appended without the duplicate scan, since it is a new function: adding n listeners with one signal and aborting it are O(n), where the base's adds were O(n²) (Node's too).
  - **Frozen signals** (review round 1): a frozen signal's `abort()` throws the frozen field's TypeError, as Node's does, before anything changes (`Object.isFrozen` first), so it can still be listened to and followed; a signal another thread freezes meanwhile throws at the write under the lock, which puts the reason back and unlocks. The first version left the lock held and the next use hung, and a reader without the lock could see the reason the failed abort tried to set (review round 2). A frozen follower can't abort: its source's other followers and listeners still do, and the source's `abort()` throws the TypeError after (Node throws too, part-way).
  - **A request's own signal.** `Request.signal`, for a request given no signal, is made when first read. It is installed under one lock for all requests, `OWN_SIGNAL_LOCK`, which the server's disconnect takes too: a read on another thread than the disconnect ends with a signal that aborts if the client has gone, and threads reading at once get the same signal. One lock rather than one per request, because a lock each would allocate for every request the server receives; the section is a few stores, and only first reads and disconnects take it, never a signal's own operations.
- **Reason:** C116's acceptance asks that every follower and listener of a shared signal abort with it at 1, 2 and 4 threads and that `abort()` never throw for the library's own state, without serializing unrelated signals; a lock per target serializes only the threads that use one target, and an uncontended futex lock is an atomic exchange. The DOM standard's dependent signals give C119's O(1) and C121's order and reasons at once, and the reproducer's matrix (`run/abort_signal_any_dependents.pts`) shows where the listener design also gave wrong reasons. The language has no compare-and-swap on fields, so a lock-free list isn't available to library code. Measured (one thread): 400,000 followers of one signal take 268 ms to make and 236 ms to abort, against Node's 842 ms and 2 ms; the base took 21.5 s for 40,000 (`h07b-reproducer.md`).
- **Alternatives rejected:**
  - One lock for every signal: serializes unrelated signals, which C116's acceptance forbids.
  - Followers as listeners of their sources, made safe by the lock: still O(n) per follower (the duplicate scan) and the wrong order.
  - Dependents held weakly, as Node does: needs weak references in the runtime (bdwgc's disappearing links); the retention it would fix is pre-existing and is recorded as C123 instead.
  - Declaring shared signals unsupported: one signal given to many fetches on several threads is the common way to cancel a group of requests.
  - A lock per request for the lazily made signal: an allocation on every request the server receives.
- **Known gaps:** Node 24.21's `AbortSignal.any` follows lazily: it adds a follower to its sources only when the follower gets its first abort listener, and otherwise works out `aborted` and `reason` when they are read. Where that differs from the standard, ParallelScript follows the standard: a listener or `onabort` added to a follower during its source's abort event runs (Node: not if the follower had no listener before), and a follower of two sources that abort in turn keeps the first one's reason (Node: the earlier source in the list, if the follower has no listener) (review round 1, F4). Dependents are held strongly, so a long-lived signal that many short-lived signals follow (every `fetch` given an application-wide signal makes one) keeps them all until it aborts: about 300 bytes per follower and 0.6–0.9 KB per finished fetch, where Node's stay flat; likewise a signal keeps the listener that each `{ signal }` option added to it, and through it the target, until the signal goes, also after it has aborted (review round 4; the base alike) (C123); a dispatch already under way on one thread may still call a listener that another thread removes meanwhile (the two have no order); an `Event` object dispatched from two threads at once isn't guarded (one dispatch of an event at a time, as before).

### D-186 · Loops that sleep wake for their unreferenced timers while the program runs; timers count from when they are set; pts:test registers tests on the main thread only (H07b2)
- **Date:** 2026-10-18
- **Decision:**
  - **Sleeping loops' timers (C120).** A loop with nothing referenced left sleeps (D-121) in a timed wait on its condition variable (`CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, libuv's clock) until a message or the first timer of its heap, where only unreferenced timers can be left by then. When a timer is due, the loop counts itself awake again only if some loop still is: a compare-and-swap that raises a count above zero, under the slot's lock with `sleeping`. At zero the program is ending, which an unreferenced timer doesn't hold up, so D-121's ending rule is unchanged. libuv runs no timers while nothing referenced is left, so a loop that wakes runs its due timers itself, before its messages. After that work, or a task taken from another loop, libuv's phases go on from poll, as after I/O: one `UV_RUN_NOWAIT` pass, the loop's own run of its due timers if that pass leaves nothing referenced, then `UV_RUN_ONCE` passes while something is (`UV_RUN_DEFAULT` without its first timers phase; only the loop's first run starts with timers). So the immediates that the work queued run before the next timers (review rounds 1 and 2: an immediate ran after a timer set beside it, also after messages and stolen tasks on the base). A loop flushes stdout before it sleeps, as before it polls (D-136).
  - **Timers count from when they are set (the reproducer's finding A, C127).** `setTimeout`, `setInterval` and `refresh` read the loop's time afresh, as Node's getLibuvNow does; they counted from the time libuv cached at the loop's last turn, which is stale after synchronous work and after a sleep, so a timer set then fired early, by up to the whole stale time (D-114 (2) meant getLibuvNow's time, which updates the clock). An interval's next run counts from just before its callback, as Node's does (review round 1, C130: from the batch's start, which the fresh clock made fire an interval at once after a long callback beside it), and the wait for the next timer counts from after the callbacks that ran (review round 2, C136: a 10 ms interval whose callback took 25 ms ticked again after 35 ms, Node's after 25).
  - **The loop a handle records (the reproducer's finding B, C128; review round 1, C134).** A timer records the loop whose heap holds it, and a server's listeners count from the loop whose thread starts the first: the thread's own loop (`pts_own_loop`), not the loop it works for. A loop's thread helping another loop's parallel operation set a timer or started a server in a `.par` callback on its own loop but recorded the operation's loop: a timer's clear, ref, unref or refresh then indexed past the loops and crashed (marked foreign), and a server's stop reached the wrong loop, so the listener stayed open and the program never ended. Which threads may set timers and start servers in `.par` callbacks is unchanged (pool threads refuse, loop threads use their own loop), recorded as C129. A timer refused on a pool thread no longer leaks the loop state made before the refusal (review round 1, C132: about 330 bytes each).
  - **pts:test's registry (C117).** `test`, `it`, `describe`, `suite` and the hooks throw `Error: pts:test's <name>() must be called on the main thread, outside parallel tasks and the callbacks of parallel operations` unless they run on the main thread outside a `parallel` task's closure and a parallel operation. That is the runtime's `pts_parallel_on_main_thread()`, from a thread-local count that a task's closure and each pts:parallel operation raise and lower, also when they throw. So they throw in the synchronous code of every task and every `.par`, `parallel for` and `join` callback at every thread count, also where it runs on the main thread, and register nothing. Registration on the main thread is unchanged. `it` and `suite` are functions of their own, so the error names the one called.
- **Reason:** C120's acceptance: unreferenced timers on any loop fire at their time while the program runs, as Node's do, and never keep it running. The awake count is the runtime's only sign that the program runs, and only a count above zero may be raised (D-121: at zero no loop can wake another). A timed wait costs nothing while no timer waits. Reading the clock afresh is what Node does and what C120's tests need: a timer that a task set on a loop just woken fired at once. C117's acceptance allows a clear rejection. The registry, the suite being described and the runner's state are module state. Tests registered from several threads at once would run in no order a program can rely on, a `describe` body on one thread filed other threads' tests in its suite, and the runner started on whichever loop registered first (on a pool thread it couldn't start at all, and the program ended without running its tests). Counting tasks and operations makes the rejection of their synchronous code independent of where it runs, so registering there doesn't pass with one thread and throw with four (code after an `await` still can, below).
- **Alternatives rejected:**
  - Counting a loop with unreferenced timers awake: the program would wait for them, which Node doesn't.
  - A timer thread that wakes sleeping loops: another thread, and the same count rule.
  - Registration from any thread under a lock, with a current suite per thread: an order no program can rely on, a runner on an arbitrary loop, and a lock held across a `describe` body whose parallel operations' callbacks register (deadlock).
  - Rejecting only off the main thread: with one thread, tasks and `.par` callbacks run on it, so a file would pass at `PTS_THREADS=1` and throw at 4.
  - Making timers and servers in `.par` callbacks follow the operation's loop, as `fetch`'s requests and promises do: a change of what such callbacks may do, not the crash's fix (C129).
- **Code after `await parallel …`:** a task's result reaches its caller's loop as a message, so the code after the `await` runs as after I/O in Node: immediates it queues run before its timers (review round 3; the base ran them after). A sequential Node twin, which awaits `Promise.resolve(…)`, runs that code in the phase it is in and can print another order.
- **Supersedes:** D-121's known gap "a loop that sleeps doesn't run its unreferenced timers until it wakes" and D-114 (2)'s timers counted from the loop's cached time.
- **Known gaps:** a task's or callback's code after an `await` runs as its loop's code, and where that is the main thread's loop (always with one thread, and for an async `.par` callback of the main thread's operation) it registers tests as the main thread's code would; the count covers the code before the first `await` (review round 1). A test registered after the runner has finished starts it again, which runs the earlier tests again and prints a second summary, where Node runs every test in one run with one summary at exit; that needs an end-of-program hook the runtime doesn't have (C126, pre-existing) → H29. Timers due at different times fire in due order, where Node's lists per duration can run a later timer of an earlier list first (C133, pre-existing, review round 1), and `refresh()` inside a timeout's own callback doesn't fire it again, where Node's does (C135, review round 2) → H19. Timers and servers in `.par` callbacks: refused on pool threads, on their own loop on loop threads (C129) → H20.

### D-187 · A suspension in an expression runs after the references, callees and operands JS evaluates before it; logical assignments suspend only when they store (H08a); H08 split
- **Date:** 2026-10-18
- **Decision:** H08 is split: H08a (this) takes the reported claims (C08, C25) and the rest of the family its reproducer found but spreads; H08b takes spreads before a suspension (C139). No scope is dropped. With a `yield` or an `await` in an expression (D-111, D-145), JS's order now holds for:
  - **Compound assignments (C08, C137).** `keepCurrent` keeps the target's value before the right side's suspension for every target: a getter's (a class's, an inherited one, `this.p`, an object literal's), a property's or element's through `any`, a field of a union of classes, as well as names, fields and elements (D-145). `readKeptAndStore` then stores without reading again: its getter branch called the getter after the resume, which is why D-145 left getters and `any` out.
  - **Logical assignments (C25).** `x ??= v`, `x ||= v` and `x &&= v` whose right side suspends are hoisted (`hoistLogicalAssignment`) to: x's object and key, kept as any reference's are; x's value, read once (`placeValue`: a variable as it is; anything else as JS reads it, undefined for an element or entry that isn't there); then an if statement whose test is the operator's (`??=` nullish; `||=` falsy; `&&=` truthy) and whose branch evaluates v, its suspensions included, and stores it as `x = v` does (a setter, a checked write, a record's entry) without reading x again. The expression's value is a local both branches assign. A class's field that the constructor hasn't set (D-141) reads as undefined for `??=` and `||=`, which store over it: a field with `FieldRef.Raw`, a keyed element (D-072) as its dynamic value, unconverted, which the branch that keeps it converts with a check (review rounds 1 and 2: the hoisted read had the unset field's check, or the keyed read's conversion, which threw before the test); for `&&=`, whose value it would be, the read keeps that check, as in code without a suspension.
  - **Callees (C08's method half).** For a call whose callee is a property or element access, the function found there is an operand after its object and key (`evaluationOrder`), kept before a suspension in the arguments unless it can't change once the object is kept (`isSettledCallee`): a method (methods can't be assigned, PTS1001; `o["m"]` with a literal key as `o.m`), a function, class, enum or namespace (also re-exported, review round 2), a library's member, or a member of a union of objects (the call calls only methods there, and reports any other member as before; review round 1). A member of `any`, of a narrowed dynamic value or of a union of objects and other values is found by `DynamicMethodOf` (`pts_dynamic_method_of`: a TypeError for null and undefined then, as reading the property is; a property's function or a getter's result; a method of the program's object or a builtin's, which nothing can change, is left to the call to find by name) and called by `DynamicCallFound`.
  - **Operands (C138, C140, C142).** An object literal's shorthand values (`{ x }`) and computed keys are operands in JS's order, before each property's value (review round 1: they were left out, so `f({ x }, await p)` read x after the resume and `{ [key()]: 1 }` called `key` after it). `isSettled` no longer counts as unchanging: postfix `++`/`--` (it ran after the suspension, and twice in an assignment's target: `arr[i++] -= yield 1` wrote `arr[1]` and left `i` at 2), `in` (it reads the keys an object has then), and a binary operator other than `===`, `!==`, `instanceof`, `,`, `&&`, `||` and `??` unless both operands are primitive, since converting an object calls its valueOf or toString. `isPrimitive` takes unions of primitives as primitive (for unary operators and templates too).
- **Reason:** C08's and C25's acceptance: values and the side effects before the suspension match Node for yield and await; the reproducer's matrix (2,310 cases) found the same causes for every place, callee and operand kind. Keeping what JS reads first, once, is D-145's rule; these were its known gaps and misclassifications. Testing a logical assignment's place before the suspension and storing after it without a second read is the only order that matches JS when the place changes meanwhile or has a getter. Methods, functions and the libraries' members can't change while a function is suspended, so only properties, elements, getters and `any` members cost a frame local.
- **Alternatives rejected:**
  - Lowering the logical assignment after the hoisted test with the existing code: it tests the place again, after the resume.
  - Keeping every callee as a bound closure: an allocation per call for methods, which can't change.
  - `DynamicGet` then `DynamicCall` for `any` callees: a builtin's method (an array's `push`) is no property value, and a method would be bound per call.
  - Copying spreads' elements in this slice: every spread site and element kind (arrays, tuples, sets, maps, iterables, object spreads with getters) needs its own snapshot (H08b).
  - Taking a keyed element's unconverted value in the branch that keeps it with `fromUnion` (review round 2's experiment): that reinterprets a dynamic value without a check; `checkedConversion` does what the keyed read did.
- **Supersedes:** D-145's known gaps ("a compound assignment's target read through a getter or `any` is read after the yield; a method found on an object (`o.m(yield v)`) is looked up after it") and D-111's known gap "`x += yield v` reads x after the yield".
- **Known gaps:** a spread before a suspension in the same call, `new` or array or object literal is iterated after it, so changes made meanwhile show and a getter or iterator runs after it (C139 → H08b; skipped test `run/suspension_spreads_evaluate_first.pts`). A callee whose read throws in PTS where JS reads undefined (a class's unset field (D-141), an element past an array's end (D-038), a hash map's missing entry) throws before the arguments are evaluated, now with a suspension in them as already without one, where Node evaluates them and then throws `TypeError: … is not a function` (C143, pre-existing; review round 1) → H22. On a class's unset field, `||` and `||=` without a suspension, `??=` on an interface's property without one, and `&&=` with or without one throw D-141's TypeError, where Node reads undefined (C144, pre-existing; review rounds 1 and 2). Without a suspension, `??=` on an element past an array's or typed array's end throws RangeError (D-038), where Node and the suspended form store, and a static accessor's `S.p ??= v` is PTS1001, where the suspended form works (C145, pre-existing; review round 1). A shape literal's computed key whose type is a literal (`{ [kk()]: 1 }` with `kk(): "k"`) is not evaluated, so its side effects are lost, unless a later property's suspension keeps it, when it runs once before the suspension as in Node (C148, pre-existing; review round 2). All three → H29.

### D-188 · A spread before a suspension spreads what it held then: a copy of its collection, an iterable's items, an object's property values (H08b); H08 split again
- **Date:** 2026-10-18
- **Decision:** H08 is split again: H08b (this) takes spreads before a suspension (C139) and the receivers their lowering read twice; H08c takes the same order without a suspension (C152) and spreads through `any` (C153), which H08b's reproducer found. No scope is dropped. With a `yield` or an `await` after a spread in the same call, `new`, array literal or object literal (D-111, D-145, D-187), the spread is evaluated before the suspension, as JS does:
  - **Spreads of arguments and array elements** (`keepSpread`). The value kept for `...x` is what spreading x then would spread, of x's own IR type where it has one, so that every consumer (`argumentValues`, `spreadParts`, `dynamicCallWith`, console, `Math`, the string builtins) lowers the spread as before and reads it from the kept local (`spreadSnapshot`): an array's copy (`ArraySlice`, holes kept, so the consumer still reads them as D-183 says), a tuple's (a new tuple of its elements; an element left out stays out, `FieldRef.Raw`, D-100), a typed array's (`pts_typed_array_slice`), a map's or set's (`NewMap{From}`); a union's, a dynamic value's or an object's items (generators, iterables of the program, `Iterable<T>`) drained into an `any[]` by `ValueToArray`, which is what `argumentValues` spreads them as; a string or a new array or tuple of a literal as it is. `a.keys()`, `a.values()` and `a.entries()` of an array, tuple, typed array, map or set keep a copy of their receiver instead (`iterationReceiver`): `arrayIteration`, `typedArrayIteration` and `mapIterableOf` lower the receiver again, so the base evaluated a receiver that is a call twice, once before the suspension and once after it.
  - **Object spreads** (`keepObjectSpread`). `evaluationOrder` lists the `SpreadAssignment`, not its source. A hash-map object is copied (`NewMap{From}`). A class instance's, an object's or an interface's properties (`spreadNames`) are read through `propertyOf`, in that order (getters run then), into a new object whose shape holds them as values (`valuesShape`: the program's shape of the same members if there is one; a property optional where the source's is, so one the source leaves out stays out; `OrderOf` the source, so the copy takes its order where the source is of that shape, D-108), and the literal copies it by the names kept (`copiedNames`, which `spreadObjectLiteral` and `spreadIntoRecord` call, with the source's type for their diagnostics, review round 1), so a class-typed source's copy holds neither its methods nor its prototype getters, as before. A source that has a method among its properties, or that no spread of an object takes (a union, `any`), is kept as before, and its lowering reports it.
  - **Classification.** A `SpreadElement` is settled (left to be evaluated after the suspension) only when its operand is settled and is a string or an array literal; a `SpreadAssignment` never is. So `f([...xs], S)` and `f({ ...o }, S)` build the literal before the suspension too.
- **Reason:** C139's acceptance: every spread site and element kind reads what it spreads before the suspension, matching Node with yield and await. A snapshot of the spread's own type keeps each consumer's lowering (tuples to fixed parameters, rest tuples, maps and strings told apart by tsc's type, holes) without a second path through each of them; draining an iterable is what spreading it is, so it runs its iterator, or its generator's body, once, before the suspension. Reading an object's properties into a values-only copy keeps `spreadObject`'s handling of optional properties (`spreadOver`, `copiedRead`) and D-108's order unchanged, since its reads stay field reads.
- **Alternatives rejected:**
  - Keeping the parts that `argumentValues` makes, keyed on the spread: each consumer lowers spreads its own way (`spreadParts` takes maps and strings, `dynamicCallWith` hands the runtime the value), and which one will lower a spread isn't known when it is hoisted.
  - Copying every spread into an array: tuples spread element by element into typed parameters, and `spreadParts` and `mapIterableOf` decide from tsc's type, which would no longer be the kept value's.
  - Copying an object spread's source as its own class: a class instance's copy would bring its methods and prototype getters, and a getter of an object literal's shape would run after the suspension.
  - Hoisting each property read into a frame local of its own: `spreadObject`'s optional-property handling takes field reads; a local holding a read that may be absent would need a second path through it.
  - Fixing C152 here: its cause is the C that `ArraySpread` and `SpreadArg` become (an array is appended, or spread by the runtime, after the later operands run), with or without a suspension; H08c.
- **Supersedes:** D-187's known gap "a spread before a suspension in the same call, `new` or array or object literal is iterated after it".
- **Known gaps:** without a suspension, a spread array's elements are appended after the later operands run, so an operand after the spread that changes the array changes what it spreads (`sum(...xs, grow())`: `-5/2/100/10`, Node `1/2/10`; C152 → H08c; skipped test `run/spread_elements_read_before_later_arguments.pts`). A call through `any` spreads arrays, tuples and typed arrays only and throws `TypeError: Cannot spread … into the arguments of a call through any` for other iterables (D-075), where Node and SPEC §3.4 (D-078) iterate them; with a suspension after the spread, a dynamic value, an object or a generator is drained before it and the call gets its items, so the two forms differ, while a typed set, map or string throws in both (C153 → H08c). A spread of a tuple whose optional element is left out passes undefined for it (C154 → H29). With an `any` object after a property, `{ w: 0, ...anyObj }` is PTS9001 (C155 → H29). `[...m.entries(), [k, v]]` throws `TypeError: Expected number[][], got Array` (C156 → H29). `[(yield 1)[0]]` with an array `TNext` is PTS9001 (C157 → H29). A spread of an interface-typed value lists the interface's declared order, not the object's, when the literal adds properties or the object is a class instance (C158 → H22). From review round 1: without a suspension, a spread of `values()` of a union of arrays is PTS9001 (C159 → H29); an interface-typed spread of a class instance copies a prototype getter (C160 → H22); `{ ...defaults, ...o }` keeps the default for a property `o` set to undefined, as D-065 chose (C161, intentional); a class with a TS-`private` field is PTS1001 to spread (C162 → H29). All pre-existing; C152 and C153 are of H08's family.

### D-189 · A spread is read at its place: array spreads append operand by operand, console's and `any` calls' spreads are copied there when a later argument may change them, and calls through `any` spread every iterable (H08c)
- **Date:** 2026-10-18
- **Decision:** Without a suspension too, a spread's elements are read where JS iterates it, before the later operands of its call, `new` or literal run (C152), and a call through `any` spreads whatever JS spreads (C153), as D-078 and SPEC §3.4 already said. H08b (D-188) did this for spreads before a suspension; this slice closes H08.
  - **Array spreads** (`ir.ArraySpread`: the array a rest parameter gets, array literals, `push`'s and `unshift`'s values, Math's variadic functions, `Array.of`, `String.fromCharCode` and their kin). Its C (`spread`, `pts/cgen/arrays.go`) evaluates each operand and appends it (a same-typed array's elements, or the element) before it evaluates the next, as statements in order, where it evaluated every operand into a temporary first (`operands`) and appended afterwards, so a spread array was read after the later operands had run. `a.concat(b, f())` keeps that order (`concat`), since JS evaluates concat's arguments before it reads them. Nothing is copied that wasn't before.
  - **Console methods and calls through `any`** (`ir.SpreadArg`, which the runtime spreads at the call). When an argument after a spread may write (`effectsOf`), the C emitter copies the spread where it is evaluated (`copyChangedSpreads`, `spreadCopy`: `pts_array_copy` of an array, `pts_value_to_array` of what `ValueSpreadItems` would hand over), unless the argument makes a new array itself (`isNewArray`: a literal, a slice or copy, a spread, `ValueToArray`); otherwise it passes the spread as before.
  - **Calls through `any`** (`DynamicCall`, `DynamicCallMethod`, `DynamicCallFound`, and a function union's call, D-096) spread anything JS spreads (`dynamicSpread`): an array as it is; anything else through the new `ValueSpreadItems` (`pts_value_spread_items`), which hands an array, tuple or typed array to the runtime as it is (the runtime spreads those at the call) and takes a string's code points, a set's or map's items, an iterator's, a generator's or another iterable's items into a new array there (`pts_value_iterable`: its iterator or generator body runs at the spread, once), and throws D-078's `TypeError: x is not iterable` there for anything else, before the later arguments run. So the forms with and without a suspension after the spread agree (H08b drained only dynamic values, objects and generators before a suspension). `pts_value_to_array` now allocates its result at its final length.
- **Reason:** C152's and C153's acceptance: the matrix without a suspension (3,965 cases) and with one (6,326) match Node wherever their `const` twin does, with no case worse than before, and the RED tests pass. Appending operand by operand is what JS's spread does (it iterates at its place), costs nothing (the typed sites' C lost its temporaries: 0.3% fewer instructions in a spread-heavy loop), and keeps one path for every site that builds an `ArraySpread`. The runtime spreads `SpreadArg`s at the call, so their elements must be taken at the spread's place only when a later argument may change them; copying only then keeps `console.log(...xs)` and `f(...held)` through `any` as they were (`f(...held)` with an array held in `any` takes 3.3% more instructions than on the base, where review round 1's snapshot took 2.35×). Draining other iterables at the spread is what typed calls (`argumentValues`) and H08b's snapshot already do; the runtime's array-only spreading stays for `apply`, which takes array-likes, not iterables.
- **Alternatives rejected:**
  - Copying every spread array where it is evaluated (H08b's snapshot, or `...[...xs]`): a copy per spread where appending in order needs none.
  - Teaching the runtime's `spread_arguments` to iterate any value: it runs at the call, after the later arguments, so a generator's body or an iterator would run late (`f(...gen(), x)` where the generator sets `x`), out of the order the C emitter keeps; and `Function.prototype.apply` shares it with array-like semantics (`apply(null, "ab")` must throw).
  - `ValueToArray` for every non-array spread through `any` (review round 1's snapshot): a copy per call of an array, tuple or typed array held in `any` that nothing changes (F1: 2.35× the base's instructions for `f(...held)`).
  - Building the argument vectors of console's and dynamic calls in C, operand by operand: a runtime API for a case that a copy covers.
  - Matching V8's text for a spread of a non-iterable into a call: V8's own message depends on the call site and tier (`Spread syntax requires ...iterable[Symbol.iterator] to be a function`, `number 5 is not iterable (cannot read property Symbol(Symbol.iterator))`); D-078's `x is not iterable` is kept (C167 → H22).
- **Supersedes:** D-188's known gaps C152 and C153; D-075's "`SpreadArg`s whose arrays (or tuples) the runtime spreads": a call through `any` now spreads every iterable.
- **Known gaps:** the copy is made when a later argument may write anything, since the C emitter's effects don't say what a write changes: `f(...held, k++)` through `any` copies `held` though `k++` can't change it (1.75× the base's instructions in a loop of such calls, `q_writer` in review round 2). A spread kept before a suspension (D-188's copy) and followed by an argument that may write is copied once more (rare). Pre-existing, found on the way: `new Array<number>(...[3])` makes a one-element array where JS makes an empty one of length 3 (C163 → H29); a tuple held in `any`, such as an array's `entries()` pair, has no array methods (`join`, `map`: "is not a function", C164 → H29); a console method's spread of a typed set, map or string is PTS1001, as D-057 left it (C165 → H29); `f.apply(null, set)` through `any` throws where Node calls with no arguments (C166 → H29); a spread of a non-iterable into a call throws D-078's message, not V8's (C167 → H22); a method through `any` that a getter gives is found after the arguments without a suspension (`o.m(xs.length, grow())` where `get m()` pushes to `xs`: `2/10`, Node `3/10`; C168 → H22, beside C143).

### D-190 · Generic arrow functions anywhere list their instances, which run with the erased copy's closure; specialize tries each instance and a build falls back to D-148's instances; a tuple converts to an array type by a checked copy (H09a); H09 split
- **Date:** 2026-10-18
- **Decision:** H09 splits into H09a (this: generic arrow functions and function expressions wherever they are, dynamic results, trials and the fallback that keep compiling programs compiling, C70) and H09b (generic methods through interfaces, object literals and method values, `bind`, generic values where a non-generic function type goes, rest parameters, D-102's narrowed branches, speculative instances and the instance limit, a narrower fallback, type arguments of one IR type; C169–C172, C176, C177). No scope is dropped. H09a:
  - **Generic arrows and function expressions anywhere** (C09). D-148 listed instances only for generic function declarations, static methods and module-scope consts (D-102); any other generic arrow function or function expression (its own type parameters or its context's: inline arguments, function-local `const`s and `let`s, a factory's result, fields, static fields and blocks, properties, elements, map values, curried arrows, arrows in constructors, getters and instances of generic functions and classes) kept the erased copy, so its `new Box(x)` made a `Box<any>` that no typed call took. Now `functionExpression` makes it a generic function of its own (`genericFunc.closure`; not in erased code, whose type arguments would mention the erased ones), and if its erased copy makes generic class instances (`makesGenericInstances`), its value lists the instances the program's typed calls look up (D-148). The runtime value is still the erased copy's closure (one representation per function value, D-078: identity, `name`, `length`, `console.log`, adapters and `any` are unchanged), so an instance is a closure function that runs with that closure: `ir.Func.Shares` names the erased copy, whose closure struct it reads its captures from; its own name in its body is that closure (`Self`); `this` in an arrow is the enclosing method's; after lowering, the two have the same captures, all that either uses (`shareCaptures`, before `markCells`, so a variable either one writes is one cell); the instance table's entry passes the closure on. An instance's body is lowered when a demand needs it, after the code around the arrow, so it sees what the arrow's names stood for when the arrow was lowered (`scope`: the locals and nested functions its identifiers name, and the static class whose initialisers it is in; another instance of an enclosing generic function binds the same names again).
  - **Which pairs.** `specialize` pairs each demand with each erased value of its erased function type (D-148). A function-local `const` whose every use calls it by name (`calledOnlyName`) meets only the demands that call that name, since its value goes nowhere else. A pairing makes its instance whatever type arguments tsc infers for the value, `any` and `unknown` included: an `unknown` that the call's own type arguments, the demand's function type (`<T>(v: T) => Result<T, unknown>`), an enclosing generic function's instance or a type parameter the signature doesn't mention give is the one the call needs, and an instance for `any` makes what the erased copy makes (for a dynamic result, an instance made for `any` itself, below). Filters that skipped such pairings (`isUninferred`, then `uninferred`) made `Result<any, any>`s that don't convert where the base ran (review rounds 2 and 3, R1 and R1′; the phantom `f<number, string>` and `f<number, boolean>` of `Promise<Box<T>>` too); with keyed dynamic results (below) none is needed.
  - **Dynamic results.** A typed call whose result is a promise or generator of generic class instances (`Promise<Box<T>>`, `Generator<Box<T>>`) is a demand too (`demandAt` took only typed results): the instance's promise or generator object makes `Box<number>`s, which `await` and `for…of` take with their checks. Every such call has the IR signature `(…) => value` whatever its type arguments, so its table entries are keyed too (`ir.Instance.Key`, `ir.InstanceCall.Key`, `instanceKey`): by the function type's declaration and its call's type arguments, parameters and result, instantiated, which `keyPart` writes the same for types of the same IR types and literals but for dynamic values, and never the same for other IR types (a literal type by itself; a union's or an intersection's members; a type reference such as `Promise<Pair<T, U>>` by its target and type arguments, without making class instances; a class or a library interface by its IR type; any other object type, a literal's, an interface's, a mapped type's or a function type's, by its IR type and its properties, index signatures and signatures, a signature's own type parameters by their places; anything else by its IR type, or by its checker type where that is a dynamic value; a type inside itself by how far out it is; past six levels or 256 types by its IR type alone, so dynamic values deeper than that aren't told apart). The value's instance depends on all of them: a type argument can reach it from outside the call's own (a type alias's instantiation, `Loader<U>`, or an enclosing generic function's or class's instance), through the result or a parameter whose IR type is or holds a dynamic value (`Promise<U>`, `Promise<U> & { label?: string }`). Calls for the same types share an entry whatever object literals spell them; the key reads as the first such call's result type; the C emitter makes another signature check for each key, and the runtime tells them apart by address as before. Without the key, a call found the first entry of its IR signature (review round 1's F7), and `f<any>(…)` or a `T` inferred as unknown, which the base ran with the erased copy, found a typed call's instance and threw (review round 2's R2). Keyed by the result's checker type (round 2's fix), each call passing a fresh object literal had its own entry (200 such calls: 200 entries, 8,099 lines of C, base 3,264; round 3); keyed by the call's own type arguments (round 3's fix), calls through `Loader<number>` and `Loader<string>`, or in `outer<number>` and `outer<string>`, shared one and threw, `f<any>` too where the base ran it (round 4's F1). Now the 200 calls share one entry (3,323 lines). And instances are told apart by IR types (D-049), but those for `Promise<number>` and for `any`, both dynamic values, make other objects (`Box<Awaited<T>>`): a typed call tells them apart by the instance's result (`fits`), a dynamic one can't, so for it `genericInstanceFor` takes only an instance made for type arguments of the same `keyPart`s (`madeFor`: the same dynamic values, at the top or inside, as in `Promise<number>[]` and `any[]`, or interfaces, index signatures and `Record`s of them), and makes one otherwise, up to `maxDynamicVariants` (4) such instances of the same IR types and never past the instance limit; past them the call keeps the erased copy, as the base's did (round 4's F2: the `any` call found `Promise<number>`'s instance and threw where the base ran; round 5's R5-1 and R5-2, and the parent's index signatures, interfaces and `Record`s: the same, through an intersection-typed parameter and inside type arguments; round 6's R6-1 and R6-2: members alone wrote a method and a function-valued property, or the prelude's `Timeout` and a structural twin, the same, and checker ids wrote each call site's generic arrow or deep fresh literal apart, so past four of them calls threw). Typed calls' instances are as before.
  - **Trials** (`tryInstance`). An instance's code may not lower where its erased copy did (`Array.isArray(x)` narrows a number `x` to `number & any[]`; a local declared in such a branch fails with it), and pairing is speculative, so each instance specialize makes is lowered at once with the instances its code calls (`lowerPending`). If that reports, or leaves an unexplained failure, only in the trial's own code (or while its instances were made), or the instance doesn't fit the demand's signature, the reports are taken back and the trial's instances, the closures in them, their demands, erased values, relaxed casts (D-066) and the captures they added to outer closures are dropped, and the call keeps the erased copy. A failure in other code stands.
  - **The fallback** (`driver.EmitC`, `driver.Build`). A trial can't take back everything (a generic class instance an instance made, whose body is lowered later; a relaxed cast in a kept instance, checked after lowering; the instance limit), so a build whose lowering with `lower.AllInstances` fails, with compile errors or an internal error (the C included), is lowered and built again with `lower.DeclaredInstances`: D-148's erased values only (declarations, static methods, module-scope consts) and typed results only, which is what the base made. A program that compiled before H09 still compiles, and a program with an ordinary error reports the second lowering's errors, the base's. `driver.EmitCWith` emits without the fallback, for `TestCompilerFailsCleanly`, which compiles every test program with `AllInstances` alone so that the fallback hides no compiler bug.
  - **C70.** A tuple converts to an array type as an array of other elements does, by a copy with each element converted and checked (`copy_tuple_into_array`, `pts/runtime/src/convert.c`; D-071): in JS a tuple is an array, and erased code holds a rest parameter `...items: [T, T]` as a tuple, so `return items` as a `T[]` threw `Expected number[], got Array`. A tuple held in `any` converts so too (a copy, where the conversion threw); an optional element left out gives no element. Typed code keeps D-094's rule (PTS1001 where the copy could be told apart).
- **Reason:** C09's and C70's acceptance: the review's probe, the reproducer's matrix (754 programs: 37 definition forms × 12 use sites, 9 result shapes, number, string, class and array type arguments) and the C70 matrix (432) match Node where they didn't, with none worse case by case, and the checked boundaries of `any` (D-155) still throw. A program whose instance doesn't lower is the exception, and a stated one (below). Instances that run with the erased copy's closure keep D-078's single representation and D-148's run-time lookup, so nothing else changes. Pairing every demand with every value is the only whole-program rule that needs no flow analysis, and trials make its misses cheap; the fallback turns D-148's promise ("no program that compiled before fails to compile now"), which speculative instances broke in review round 1 (35 of 212 generated programs), into a property of the driver rather than of every place a trial would have to roll back.
- **Alternatives rejected:**
  - Instances only of arrows that capture nothing (the reproducer's first prototype): most factories in functions capture something (a parameter, `this`), and an instance made elsewhere can't read them.
  - A closure per instance, made where the arrow is: a second representation of one function value, which `===`, adapters and `any` would have to tell apart.
  - Speculative instances without trials (the first snapshot): `Array.isArray(x)` in an arrow's body made a compiling program PTS1001.
  - Trials that roll back everything (class instances and their member bodies, class lists, relaxed casts that stand): each kind of state a trial touches is another place to leave something behind (review round 1 found five); the fallback covers them all and costs a second lowering only for programs that fail.
  - Keeping the base's PTS1001 for an instance that doesn't lower: the compiler can't tell a pairing the program makes from one it doesn't, and the base's error was spurious for unrelated pairings (review round 1's `f04b`).
  - Making erased copies' tuple rest parameters `any[]` for C70: a tuple held in `any` would still not convert, and the calling convention of erased signatures would change.
- **Supersedes:** D-148's known gap "generic arrow functions and function expressions other than module-scope consts … still take the erased copy", its "the result's type must be the one `fromErased` converts to" for dynamic results, and its "An instance that `narrowsAway` keeps the erased copy" now for any instance that doesn't lower; D-078's "Converting a dynamic tuple to another tuple type copies it" now holds for array types too.
- **Known gaps:** H09b's scope: interface calls of generic methods, object literals' generic methods, generic methods read as values and `bind` (PTS1001 on an object type's method), generic values given where a non-generic function type goes (`[1, 2].map(mk)`, an adapter that hides the instances) (C169); rest parameters (D-148's exclusion, C170); D-102's narrowed branches: an instance whose code narrows a variable to `never` or an intersection for its type arguments doesn't lower and keeps the erased copy, and a direct call of such a generic function is PTS1001, or PTS9001 where the intersection's member reaches the C (C171); pairing is speculative, so instances no call uses count against the 64-instance limit, after which an internal error can follow, and code grows with values × demand types where values escape: 150 generic arrows pushed into an `any[]` and 50 demand types make 92,388 lines of C, a 25.7 s build (base 8,784 lines, 2.2 s; review round 2's `esc_150x50`), as 150 escaping generic declarations already did before H09, and a dynamic result's calls for other dynamic-valued checker types make up to four instances of the same IR types (70 calls of `f<Promise<"kN"> | number>`: 3,415 lines of C, round 4's fix before it 1,699, with one instance that was wrong for `Awaited<T>`), past which a call keeps the erased copy even where the shared instance would have fit (round 5's `q08`: six calls of `Promise<1>` … `Promise<6>` of `Box<Awaited<T>>`, the last two throw, as all six do on the base) (C172); type arguments of the same IR types share an instance for typed calls (D-049), so direct calls whose results differ by checker type (`Box<T extends Promise<infer U> ? U : T>` for T of `Promise<number>` and `any`) are the base's PTS1001 ("using a `Box` as a `Box` is not supported yet"; round 4's `g03`; C177); an IR interface prints its nested interfaces in full, so an instance for a wide interface graph is slow to name and check (a 10-wide, 6-deep graph: 4.7 s and 2.8 GB on the base through direct calls; through values, which made no instance before, 1.4 s → 3.3–5.2 s and 0.19 → 0.93 GB; C178, H23); the fallback is the whole program's, so one value whose instances fail in a way a trial can't take back makes every other generic arrow in the program keep its erased copy (review round 2's `c10_cliff`), and `pts emit-c`, which doesn't run clang, prints the `AllInstances` C even where clang rejects it and `pts build` falls back (C176). An instance that doesn't lower now leaves its call on the erased copy, a checked TypeError at run time where the base gave PTS1001 when the pairing was one the program makes (`f04` in review round 1; a dispatched generic method's too, `t01` in round 2; pinned by `run/generic_instances_that_dont_lower.pts`). `TestCompilerFailsCleanly` fails for a test program that compiles only with the fallback, but those built to (`needsFallback`).

### D-191 · Typed calls of generic methods through interfaces and object literals find their instances: demands by the method's name, object literals' generic methods list instances in their closures, one entry convention for methods (H09b1); H09b split
- **Date:** 2026-10-18
- **Decision:** H09b splits into H09b1 (this: generic methods through interfaces and object literals, C169's first half), H09b2 (typed reads verify the objects erased copies make: C187, C186), H09b3 (generic method values, `bind`, generic values where a non-generic function type goes and the adapters that hide instances, `?.` and element-access calls, rest parameters: C169's second half, C170, C179, C185), H09b4 (D-102's narrowed branches, C171) and H09b5 (only instances calls may use, the instance limit, a narrower fallback, type arguments of one IR type: C172, C176, C177, C184). No scope is dropped. H09b1:
  - **Demands by name.** A typed call of a generic method whose result may hold generic class instances (D-148's test) through an interface, through a union of object types, or of an object literal's method through the literal's own type is a demand by the method's name (`namedInstanceCall`; `demand.name`, and for a literal's type `demand.shape`): it calls the instance of its signature that the receiver lists, else the erased copy as before (an `ir.InstanceCall` with the method's name, whose `Otherwise` is D-079's interface call or the shape field's call, with its result converted to the call's type, as before; a call through a union of object types keeps the base's dynamic result: its instance's result is boxed (`ir.InstanceCall.Dynamic`) and the erased copy's stays as it is, where review round 2's snapshot converted both to the call's type and a template or `console.log` of a plain implementation's result threw (R2-1)). The arguments are lowered once, to the erased copy's parameter types, as the base passed them, and the call is a demand only if each is a value of the instance's parameter type as it is (`passedAsIs`, `passesAsIs`: the same type, a dynamic value's place, a wider number, a union's member, a class's ancestor or an interface the object provides), never a dynamic value, and no parameter typed with the method's type parameters is of a type that conversions don't verify (`takesUnverifiedObjects`, `verifiesObjects`: an object type, an interface, a dynamic value or a union with one, where a primitive, a class, or an array, tuple, map or function of those is verified): the instance branch takes the value as it was before it was boxed, `Otherwise` the boxed one. A spread argument makes no demand; an optional argument left out passes as left out (review round 2, N2). A call whose argument a copy, a check or an adapter would pass to the instance (a `Dog[]` where T is `Animal[]`, D-094's PTS1001 in typed code; an `any`, a JSON value, where T is an object type; a function with another parameter list) takes the erased copy with the arguments as they are, as before: converting them to the instance's types turned such calls into PTS1001s that sent the build to the fallback, or into checked copies and adapters where the base, like Node, kept identity and skipped the check (review round 1, F1 and F3).
  - **Which pairs.** A demand by name meets every class's generic method of that name whose class, or a subclass, is assignable to the receiver's type (`specializeMethod`, `mayReceive`, remembered per receiver type and class, `classType`), whose class lists the instance for the call's signature, or an entry without one, which ends the search as D-148's do; the instance's type arguments are the ones tsc infers relating the class's method to the call's resolved signature (`PtsInstantiateSignatureInContextOf`), so a generic interface (`Paired<U>`'s `pair<T>`), a generic class's instances and a method whose type parameters differ from the interface's get theirs. Dispatched demands (D-101) infer them so too, where they took the abstract method's by position: the two kinds share a class's entry for a name and signature, which the first to pair made, so an override with an extra defaulted type parameter had an instance only if the interface call came first (review round 1, F6). A demand by name meets every object literal's generic method of that name whose literal's type is assignable to the receiver's too (`specializeValue`, `reaches`); a call through a literal's own type meets only the methods of literals of its shape, and no class's.
  - **Object literals' generic methods** (`objectMethod`): a generic method of an object literal (its own type parameters or its context's), outside erased code, is a generic function whose instances run with the method's closure, as a generic arrow function's do (D-190: `Shares`, `shareCaptures`, `scope`), taking the literal first as `this` (`genericFunc.class` is its shape, `genericFunc.field` its field), so `this`, captured variables and cells are the erased copy's; its closure's descriptor lists them under the method's name. A method that fills a function-typed property (`make: <T>(x: T) => Box<T>`) has no `this` and is a generic function value (D-190), whose value calls find its instances.
  - **The fallbacks.** Assignability can't tell a class that may meet the call from a structural twin that never does (review round 1's `p14`: an unrelated class with the same `make<T>`, whose instance for the call's type makes a generic class instance that doesn't lower after its trial), so the driver falls back in two steps (`lower.Fallbacks`): `AllInstances` (this slice's), then `ValueInstances` (H09a's: generic arrow functions anywhere and dynamic results, no demands by name, no instances of literals' methods), then `DeclaredInstances` (D-148's). What a program loses to a failure of its demands by name is their instances alone, never H09a's.
  - **Run time and C.** `pts_method_instance_find(object, site, name, signature, &closure)` (`object.c`) searches the class chain's entries as `pts_instance_find` does (an entry without a function ends the search), then, if none has one and the object's field of that name holds a closure, that closure's entries of the name, handing the closure back. Each call site has a cache (`pts_site`, D-036's) of where the field is, or that there is none, for a descriptor none of whose classes has an entry: searching the fields by name at each call cost a call on a literal or a class without an entry 1,800 more instructions than the base's (review round 1, F5); with the cache 60 (literal) and 40 (class), and a dispatched call's lookup 13 (209 → 222 per call; callgrind, `bench3/` in `h09b1-parent-probes.tar.gz`). Every method entry takes the closure (an object literal's method's, else NULL and unused), the object and the call's arguments (`instanceTable`), D-101's dispatched entries too, so a call site makes one call (`instanceCall`).
  - **Array arguments.** The C of every instance call (`instanceCall`) takes the instance only if each array argument is left out (NULL, an optional parameter not given: review round 5's T1, where the guard read its descriptor) or its elements are of the instance's parameter's kind (one compare of the descriptor's `element`): an array an erased copy made holds dynamic values, which typed code may hold as a `number[]` unchanged (C187), and the instance would read them as numbers (`7.5` for `7`); the erased copy reads them dynamically (review round 4).
  - **Safety.** An entry of a literal's method casts the object to the literal's shape, as the shape's own method entry already does for the erased copy (`pts_entry_S_m`), so both rely on one invariant: a literal's method closure is only ever in a field of an object of its literal's shape. Spreading a literal's method property (C182) and assigning to one are PTS1001, a frozen object keeps its layout and fields (D-157; the `Readonly` type of a literal with methods that `Object.freeze` returns is PTS1001 to use, C183), and a method read as a value is a bound wrapper (D-044), so no program reaches another layout; a change that allows spreading or assigning methods must check the layout in both places.
- **Reason:** C169's first half: the RED tests (`run/generic_interface_methods.pts`, `generic_object_literal_methods.pts`, `generic_interface_methods_threads.pts`) threw C169's TypeError on every line and match Node; the first three lines of the skipped `generic_method_factories.pts` match Node; the H09 matrix's 18 interface-method and object-literal-method calls are OK, nothing worse, its 538 other OK cases print what they printed; the `any` boundaries of D-155 still throw (`generic_method_any_boundaries.pts`). The reproducer's prototype, written independently, took the same design (the convention, the run-time search, the pairing, the gate).
- **Alternatives rejected:**
  - Converting the arguments to the instance's parameter types and passing those to the erased copy too (review round 1's snapshot): F1 and F3 above.
  - Passing no dynamic value to an instance while making instances for any parameter type (the third snapshot): `customers.get(id)`, typed `Customer | undefined` by tsc, is a dynamic value, so `Opt.fromNullable(customers.get(id))` kept the erased copy, whose object (a dynamic `value`) `Opt.flatMap`'s instance read typed and threw (corpus `functional/option_maybe`); with `verifiesObjects`, `Opt.flatMap` (`o: Option<T>`) makes no demand either and the corpus program runs as on the base.
  - Passing an `any` argument to the instance unchecked (as a union's member, `ToUnion`): a wrong-typed value would reach typed code unchecked, which is not memory-safe; checked or copied, it is F3.
  - Passing a dynamic value that tsc types other than `any` (the fifth snapshot's `passesDynamicAsIs`): a union call's dynamic result may be an erased copy's `Box<any>` (a check then threw where the base passed it on, review round 3's R3-2), and `ToUnion` passed an erased copy's object unchecked.
  - Instances for parameters of any type (snapshots 1–5): an erased copy's object converts unchanged to a union of object types and an instance read its `any[]` as a `number[]` (review round 3's R3-1, C187: `[7]` read `[7.5]`, a lie through `any` crashed), where the base's erased copy read it dynamically. Until typed reads verify such objects (C187, H09b2), H09b1 makes no demand where a parameter typed with the method's type parameters is of a type conversions don't verify.
  - Pairing a demand by name with every class's and literal's method of the name (the round-1 snapshot): instances no call uses, whose failures sent unrelated programs to the fallback (F2); assignability keeps the pairs a call may use, and the two-step fallback the cases it can't tell apart.
  - Instance tables on shape descriptors: a shape is the layout of many literals (shapes are interned by their members), each with its own method closure, so the instances belong to the closure; and an entry that forwards to a closure can't fall back to the erased copy, which is the call site's.
  - Two entry conventions, a class's `(object, arguments)` and a literal's `(closure, object, arguments)`, with two calls at each site: one convention costs a class entry one unused argument.
  - Pairing a call through a literal's own type with classes' methods: its object is always a literal of that shape.
  - Making demands by name in the fallback build too: the reproducer's `i08` (polymorphic recursion through an interface) then stops a program that compiles on the base at the instance limit, with PTS9001 after it (C172).
- **Supersedes:** D-148's and D-190's known gap "interface calls of generic methods, object literals' generic methods … take the erased copy" for calls; D-079's "an interface's or object literal's generic method has its erased signature and is called with its result checked" now only where no instance is found; D-190's fallback from `AllInstances` to `DeclaredInstances`, now through `ValueInstances`; D-148's by-position type arguments of a dispatched demand's instance.
- **Known gaps:** C187 (H09b2, high, pre-existing): typed reads don't verify an erased copy's objects, which convert unchanged to unions of object types; H09b1 makes no demand where such an object could reach an instance through a parameter typed with the method's type parameters, and an instance call takes an array argument only if its elements are the instance's parameter's kind (the C emitter's guard in `instanceCall`, for every instance call, D-148's and D-190's too), so a `number[]` that typed code took from such an object (holding its `any[]`) takes the erased copy, as before (review round 4's `s00`, `s01`); a value misread upstream that the guard can't see (a class instance whose array field holds such an `any[]`, an object of the wrong member) still reaches an instance, as it reaches any typed code on the base. Calls that pass a dynamic value or take a parameter of an object type, an interface or a union of them typed with the type parameters (`repo.save(user)` with `user: User`, `saveAll([user])`, `orElse<number>({ tag: "some", value: 3 }, 0)`) keep the erased copy, as on the base: the skipped `run/generic_method_object_arguments.pts` (review round 4's `s02`) pins them for H09b2. An `any` argument keeps a call on the erased copy, whose objects have dynamic fields for the type parameters; typed code reading one through its type throws `TypeError: … it is declared value` where Node reads it, as on the base (C186). A call through an element access (`m["make"](5)`, `quoted["make"](6)`) keeps the erased copy, as on the base (C185, H09b3); an arrow property where an interface declares a method (`const m: Maker = { make: <T>(x: T) => new Box(x) }`) is wrapped in a `functionAsMethod` adapter, whose closure lists no instances (C169's adapters, H09b3); `?.` calls of every demand kind keep the erased copy (C179, H09b3); a demand by name meets every method of its name, so code grows with methods × demands (the reproducer's `growth2_40`, 40 literals and 40 classes called once each with its own type: 6,031 → 42,431 lines of C, a build 1.1 → 7.7 s), and polymorphic recursion through an interface reaches the instance limit, with C172's internal error, so the program falls back to `ValueInstances` (C172, C176, H09b5); a structural twin's failing instance does so too (`p14`), and the interface call then takes the erased copy (C176).

### D-192 · Typed reads verify declared types; a union takes a value as a member that declares its properties; dynamic values go into unions with a check unless a typed collection holds them; maps and sets convert by a checked copy (H09b2)
- **Date:** 2026-10-20
- **Decision:** C187 and C186: an object an erased copy makes holds dynamic values where the type parameters go (an `any[]`, a Map's values, a dynamic field); a union of object types took it unchanged as the member it fits by leaving properties out (`{ tag: "none" }` for `{ tag: "some", value: any[] }`, since tags are strings in the IR), and typed code narrowed to the other member read its dynamic values as typed ones (D-072's reads checked kinds only: 7 read 7.5, a number read as a string crashed) or threw "it is declared value". Five parts:
  - **Typed reads verify** (`object.c`). A read site (`pts_field_address`, `PTS_GET`'s field and getter, `pts_optional_get`) caches a field or getter only if its declared type implies the property's (`reads_as_is`: the same kind, then the same check or `pts_check_implies`, which a per-thread cache remembers; a field the runtime declares no type for is trusted by its kind, as before), or if it reads an object as an object type: the object's properties are only ever read through sites, each verifying its own as it is read, so a property read as another type needn't hold for the others (review round 4's H1, H2). Otherwise it reads the value converted *as it is* (`pts_value_try_convert_as_is`: a dynamic value of the type, any object as an object type, a closure of another type adapted, D-075) into a new cell, uncached, and throws D-073's `TypeError: Cannot read property p of C as T: it is declared U` if the value isn't one as it is. Nothing is copied at a read: a write into a copy would be lost, and every read would copy again. An absent optional property stays absent. Writes through a site convert a value to the field's or setter's declared type across kinds too (`pts_field_set_slow`, `pts_property_find_slow`), where a kind mismatch threw a read's TypeError. So a typed read never misreads, whatever a union took. This includes an array of a subtype's elements read as a union member's array of its supertype (a class's `Dog[]` taken as `{ kind: "empty" }` and read as `{ kind: "pack"; members: Animal[] }`): it throws, where the base read it and let a `Cat` pushed through that view be read as a Dog (`run/union_member_covariant_arrays.pts`); typed code can't pass a `Dog[]` where an `Animal[]` goes directly either (D-094).
  - **A union takes a value as a member that declares its properties** (`convert.c`). tsc narrows a union of object types by discriminants and `in`, which the IR can't tell apart, so a value may be read as any member that declares a property it has.
